## K: Kiwis–kwise

### Kiwis
 
 <i>n.</i> Kiwi concert party

>  <b>McKEON</b> Later, Dr Phillips was the conductor of the Kiwis Orchestra in France. <i>(1971,Fruitful Years, The,87</i>)

>  <b>DOWNES</b> At the height of the bitter and tragic battles of Ypres and Passchendale.. the Kiwis and their now well camouflaged canvas theatre could be found.. at the nearby town of Dickebusche.<i>ibid.</i> 54 The next production put together by the Kiwis was a musical revue called Y Go Crook... The Kiwi concert party had by now grown to a large company of forty-eight members . <i>(1979,Top of the Bill,53</i>)

>  Wally Prictor, the female impersonator who left the Kiwis to go into business as a milk bar poprietor.. had since.. rejoined the party. <i>(Apr 30, 1952,Evening Post,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ team; (in singular an individual member of the team etc.)

>  <i> ~ n.</i> New Zealand wingless bird [Maori]. Also used colloquially for a New Zealander. New Zealand touring teams are sometimes known as the Kiwis. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kiwi ... n. N.Z. wingless bird [Maori]. (Coll.) New Zealander; <i>the Kiwis</i>, N.Z. touring teams. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> `At last', it was announced in 1908, `the pride of the victorious Kiwis has been humbled in the dust' [by the Victoria College women's hockey team]. <i>(1949,Victoria University College:,121</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> NZ Expeditionary Force entertainers; NZ Divisional concert party

>  [programme cover for New Zealand Divisional Theatre at the Front 1917 : a picture of a kiwi with lemon-squeezer on peering up at a Pierrot girl with caption] <i>The Kiwi looks on.</i> [?A.P.Paterson] <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,59</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> The `Kiwis', the New Zealand Pierrots. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,123</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Aug 24 in 1939 Miller Camps, Tracks and Trenches 123]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The same evening the `Kiwis' (our Pierrots) gave a highly entertaining performance in front of our billets. <i>(Sep 15, 1917,New Zealander's Diary,260</i>) [NOTES: (c1922)]

>  The existing pierrot troupes, `The Kiwis', `Two' and others have reached a high standard of excellence. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,10</i>)

>  <b>McKiNLAY</b> [Title] Ways and Byways of a Singing Kiwi. <i>(1939,Ways & Byways</i>)

>  <b>McKEON</b> it was at Nieppe [in 1917] that we first made contact with the `Kiwis', the divisional concert party. ... We were to see the `Kiwis' in many other settings before the war ended. <i>(1971,Fruitful Years, The,129</i>)

>  To have shepherded the now famous all-soldier `Kiwis Revue' through an Australian tour which began in April, 1946, and to finish up with the company smiling, successful and intact in 1950 is no mean achievement. This responsibility has been that of Terry Vaughan, the Kiwis Producer and Musical Director. However, it is no new task to him, having been with the Kiwis ever since their inception in the Middle East in 1941 when the 2nd N.Z.E.F. formed its own Entertainment Unit with men of the Division. Sir Bernard Freyberg, V.C., now His Excellency the Governor General of New Zealand, has always kept a paternal eye on his `Kiwi Concert Party'. <i>(Sep 6, 1950,Programme of The Kiwis Revue Company</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) NZ Army rugby team [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The famous Kiwis Army rugby team didn't have the best of luck for their final reunion, in Wellington this month. <i>(Apr 2, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> i haven't seen a decent game [of rugby] since the Kiwis played on this ground in 1946. They were soldiers and they brought to the game something of the spirit of daring that we never see today. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,75</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `We're off at the high port,' Spandau says, but the play becomes all scrambly instead and the referee leaps in to award a penalty to the Kiwis. <i>(1970,On the Ball,137</i>)

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> Such was the setting ... on the day [24 November 1945] that the now famous `Kiwis', were to lay against England in the first of the Victory internationals. <i>(1973,Listen...!,9</i>)

>  <b>HOWiTT</b> it is generally conceded that J.B. Smith played his greatest Rugby with the 2nd NZEF team, the Kiwis, in Great Britain and France in 1945-46. <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,264</i>)

>  Ron Stewart was one of the men who chose the celebrated New Zealand Army team, the Kiwis, in 1945.... <i>(Dec 18, 1982,Press, The,64</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) NZ rugby league team

>  Kiwi is now a common name of the footballing Newzealander [sic]. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words]

>  <b>DRYDEN</b> The fullback whop made the team was Aucklander Jack Fagan, the surest goal-kicker ... ever to play for the Kiwis - and a good friend. <i>(1978,Out of the Red,159</i>)

>  <i>Kiwi</i>: any native person from NZ; in particular, the national Rugby League team [cf All Blacks (rugby)] <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  NEWCASTLE. - The Kiwis hope a big crowd at the opening match of their rugby league tour here today will help their cash flow problems. <i>(Jul 13, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> WW1 pierrots

>  <b>WOLFE</b> Also to come out of the First World War was a remarkable line-up of performing servicemen, the best known of whom were the Kiwis, who first trod the boards in northern France. <i>ibid.</i> 35 [Caption] The Kiwi Concert Party photographed upon its return from entertaining the troops in the trenches of France in 1917. <i>(1991,Kiwi,34</i>)

>  "The Kiwis" are the best known company of amusement purveyors with the Division, and they were first on the field as N.Z. Divisional Entertainers. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,iv/47 : 248</i>)

>  The existing pierrot troupes, "The Kiwis", "Tuis" and others, have reached such a high standard of excellence that it makes it doubly hard for a new troupe to create an impression. <i>(Oct 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,iV/49 : 10</i>)

>  in reference to the Kiwis, our orchestra was handed over to the Division over 18 months ago. <i>(Aug 16, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/50 : 35</i>)

>  The third and last group of returned soldier entertainers, popularly known as the Kiwis, Brigade Entertainers, late N.Z.E.F. , will appear in the Municipal Theatre, Hastings, on Monday and Tuesday. <i>(Feb 14, 1920,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

### KiwiSaver
 
 <i>n.</i> a national superannuation and savings scheme introduced 1 July 2007 [Kiwi Saver, Kiwisaver, Kiwi-saver, etc]

>  He also notes the Clark Government's signature economic achievements: KiwiSaver and the Cullen fund. <i>(Aug 15, 2009,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>) [NOTES: Kiwi Saver, Kiwisaver, Kiwi-saver, etc]

>  Know you should be doing something about Kiwisaver, but don't know where to start? .. Now think carefully how Kiwisaver fits into your plan for building a retirement nest egg. <i>(Jul 15, 2007,Sunday Star Times,C12</i>)

>  KiwiSaver is a voluntary, work-based savings initiative to help you with your long-term saving for retirement. <i>(2007,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.kiwisaver.govt.nz]

>  The expected name - KiwiRail - continues the Government's branding theme that includes Kiwibank and KiwiSaver. <i>(Jul 1, 2008,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  This budget's extension of Kiwisaver was a truly historic and courageous gesture. <i>(Jun 2, 2007,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  KiwiSaver providers offer everything from aggressive funds to ethical funds for Christians. <i>(Apr 26, 2008,NZ Herald,C10</i>)

### Kiwisaver
 
 the name of a government--initiated voluntary work-based savings scheme, set up in 2007 to boost the habit of saving or retirement. [Kiwi Saver, Kiwi-saver]

### KKK
 
 <i>n.</i> the upper middle class suburbs of Wellington: Karori, Kelburn, Khandallah. [ORIGIN: initialism]

>  <b>Ricketts</b> References to "bloody KKK mothers" (Karori, Kelburn, Khandallah), to Oriental Bay and Epuni, clearly identify the setting, but the play's emotional subtlety and power would grip any audience. <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),191/56</i>)

>  in nearby Lambton Quay, the doorman at Kirkcaldie & Stains doffs his top hat to the imperious ladies of the KKK (the suburbs of Karori, Kelburn, Khandallah) while, amid the chandeliers and wood panelling at Pravda, their husbands gather for business lunches ... <i>(Nov 2007,Cuisine,125/186</i>)

>  The youngest [sic] of two girls, Williams grew up in what she calls "the KKKs": Karori, Kelburn and Khandallah. <i>(Mar 22, 2009,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),14</i>)

>  Being a KKK (Kaori, Kelburn, Khandallah) lady made her especially remiss [at keeping a date]. <i>(Sep 10, 2011,Your Weekend,24</i>)

### knee high
 
 <i>n.</i> children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Action terms which refer to the shooting of the marbles are: Knuckle down Stiff, Knee High, Funks [Flunks sic] or Funnicks, Slips, Firsts and Lasts and Forces. ... The term `Knuckle down Stiff' (Taranaki [18]80, Gisborne [18]85, Waitara [18]95) is mentioned by [Lady A.B. Gomme Vo. 1, p.113]. ... As Taranaki [18]80 says, `The term was called out in order to prevent too easy a success against a marble near the ring'. it was less advantageous to fire with the hand on the ground than with the hand elevated, perhaps at `Knee High'. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### knick knock
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> One common prank was Knick Knock. in this game, children knocked on someone's door, then ran away, but it could be played more elaborately: in Tick Tack we stuck a pin into the middle sash of a window; a button was suspended from this on a piece of cotton. Then a long length of cotton was attached to the smaller piece and led away to the gate. The cotton was pulled every now and then and the button tapped against the window. it was nighttime, and folks would pull up the blind and peer out. Some more adventurous spirits would come out and investigate and would maybe get entangled with the cotton. Then the culprit would run away to some other house. (1900; Moeraki) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,90</i>)

### knifehand
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a carcase-cutting worker in a freezing works.

>  Beef boners and skilled knifehands are required for the Monday to Friday night shift operation at our Eltham Plant. <i>(Dec 23, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,30</i>)

>  Because even works knife hands earned $30,000-$40,000, Kerry Lash's store was doing very nicely. <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,49</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> When exams finished in November 1974 one of my friends got me a job as a knifehand at the freezing works. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,12</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> He'd had eight months as a knife-hand in a bacon factory, a month scrub-cutting, ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,53</i>)

>  The meat was the best Westfield [Freezing Works] could provide - one of the old digs happened to be a knife hand. <i>(1977,Best of Homespun,65</i>)

### knifey
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game, stagknife.

>  The younger children here finished so they leave ceremoniously, and play `knifey' in the yard. <i>(1901,NZ illustrated Magazine,iV/852</i>)

>  <b>COMBS</b> Another fieldsman ... may have seen the ball but has been indulging spasmodically in `knifey' <i>(1939,Harrowed Toad,90</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> <i>Stagknife</i> (K-31), a universal game of nineteenth-century childhood, was important because it was played with the most treasured play object of all boys, the pocket knife. Although known in nearly all parts of New Zealand by the name of Stagknife, the game was also known as Bites, Jackknife, Stabknife, Knifey, Momley Peg, and Throwing the Knife. ... There were generally two players ... who each sought to deflect the point of the pocket knife from certain parts of the body by a flick of the hand in such a way that the blade would stick firmly into the ground. ... Every movement had to be done three times. [172] Typical movements were: (1) Place the open pocket knife (loose) [name of movement] [sic] on the closed knuckles of the right hand, point the knife towards the thumb, then with a quick upward flick of the hand to the left, deflect the point of the knife into the ground. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,171</i>)

 <i>v.</i> to do odd jobs (cf knock about hand).

### knock off
 
 <i>v.</i>

 <i>v.</i> to dispose off; sell

 <i>v.</i> to race (a horse) hard [contrast P: knock 6. v.i. (racing) to welsh]

 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to stop work or to complete a farm job.

### knock one all to the pack
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have a bad effect on

>  <b>HYDE</b> Liquor knocks them all to the pack <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,190</i>) [NOTES: nf. Partridge]

### knock one for a row of ash-cans
 
 <i>phr.</i> to impress greatly; to astound

>  i suppose you're going to get a shampoo & a permanent wave too. ... You'll knock her for a row of ash-cans. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in italy,12</i>)

### knock out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  it has now been cleared up, and we think for some time, when we expect to knock out 7ozs a-day. <i>(Aug 5, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> it is very jolly to live in one's own house ..., because we can knock out enough to live upon at any time, ... <i>(Jan 4, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> They knocked out in this day as much gold as sufficed to make them ... two rings, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,18</i>) [NOTES: [check cit: ?suffised]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to earn, esp. on goldfields esp. knock out tucker

>  <b>PYKE</b> By most of the neighbouring miners, they were regarded as two industrious young men, who worked very hard for a bare living - `just knocking out tucker', as the phrase went. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/xiv/47,1889. 1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> i can knock out tucker enough for the pair of us. <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,24</i>)

>  <i>knock out ... NZ & Austral. sl.</i> earn. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,415</i>)

### knock over
 
 <i>v.</i> to fall (a tree), knock down

>  <b>HAY</b> A single bushman could have knocked that tree over before dinner time. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,184</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to kill farm stock.

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> The rifle and emergency ammunition also had its uses. it was ideal for knocking over an old horse for dog tucker or killing a beast for beef. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,82</i>)

### knock rotten
 
 <i>v.</i> trounce, fight [ca1941 ST Patrick's College, Silverstream `You mustn't let him knock you rotten'. {sc in a boxing contest]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1945,NZ Slang,120. 134</i>)

### knock round
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BELL</b> i have been knocking round the Colonies since then, pretty hard up generally - but not always. i made a rise in Queensland once, at the Towers, but it all went soon; and now i have given New Zealand a turn ...; and have had the good fortune to drop on you. <i>(1899,in the Shadow of the Bush,30</i>)

### knock the corners off
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The wife is a healthy, hale woman and after knocking corners off both hubby and the girl, she fired the pair into the street, he in his night-shirt and she with nothing but the rags of her chemise in her hand. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/1/5</i>)

### knock the drawing room out of
 
 <i>phr.</i> knock the stuffing out of?

>  <b>BLAKiSTON</b> it [the horse] was not fit to ride, and i was not going to ride it again. Mr Tripp smacked his thigh, clapped his hands and laughed. `That will knock the drawing room out of you!' said he. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,1952</i>) [NOTES: f. c. 1869]

### knock the wool out of one's head
 
 <i>phr.</i> to wake one up, to make (one) think clearly.

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> Seems to have knocked some of the wool out of my head already. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,40</i>)

### knock under
 
 <i>v.</i> to die

>  i am trying, by dint of resolution, to pull through all [my sickness] and hope a change for the better, but fear i shall have, some of these days, to knock under. <i>(1870,McLean Papers,32/168</i>) [NOTES: F Maning]

>  <b>VOGEL</b> When father knocks under, me and my brother we'll come into the business. <i>(1898,Maori Maid,158</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> give in, give way to

### knock up
 
 <i>v.</i> to build rapidly (and roughly); to build or complete (something, a task) in a rough-and-ready or makeshift way

>  We soon managed ... to knock up a decent dwelling <i>(Dec 11, 1842,Chambers Edinburgh Journal,356</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Dec 11 Letter f. Wellington in Chambers Journal Emigrant Voices from NZ (1848) 356]

>  We partook of their [sc. Maoris'] repast and then walked ..., when being very tired, we knocked up a warre [ed fn <i>Whare</i>, makeshift hut.] for the night and made a humu to cook our pork. <i>(Mar 14, 1849,Torlesse Papers,68,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> A Mrs Someone went there with her husband, and he had to knock up a kind of rough hut for her. <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,76</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... we found we had steered within a quarter of a mile of a low slab V hut, which we had knocked up there a short time before. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,89</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to become tired, too tired to continue

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Next morning ... the naval gentlemen who were knocked up, staying behind till next day to recruit. <i>(1820,Journal,24,1928 ed.</i>)

>  Still walking up the river-bed till 1 p.m. when my pilot who was about 60 years old was knocking up. <i>ibid.</i> 135 About 2 miles east of Starvation Hill Jock knocked up for want of water, and though i rode to the spring ... and supplied him well with water he died. <i>(Mar 18, 1849,Torlesse Papers,68,1958 ed.</i>)

>  Kennard ... came and stayed all night, his mare having knocked up on Roughridge. <i>(Aug 4, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Aug 4 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 106]

>  Two foals nocked [sic] up and were left here. <i>(Jul 3, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,46,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Before midday ... my horse knocked up completely and would not go another step. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,viii/118</i>)

>  [The horses] knock up and stray and are stolen <i>(1868,McLean Papers,29/243 f.</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Richmond]

>  <b>EDGE-PARTiNGTON</b> There was no saying when Nora [a mare] might knock up <i>(1883,Random Rot,308</i>)

>  <b>CHAMiER</b> `Hullo, mate! have you knocked up already?' <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,331</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Many a man knocked up, and comparatively few went through the campaign from beginning to end in the Rangers. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/269</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> .. the mare completely knocked up, so he took the saddle off and left his steed to follow ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,136</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To become exhausted and unable to continue. .in 4;`Earlier in the race he ran well but he couldn't maintain this - he knocked up in the straight'. <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],653</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to exhaust

>  <b>WAiTE</b> The dodging round rocks ... very nearly knocked me up <i>(1856,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Waite Letter to Joseph Thomas 11]

>  Started in quest of my itinerant horse. ... at last found the wretch ... and nocked [sic] my little mare up for a fourteen-night. <i>(Oct 6, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,106,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to collapse, especially if overdriven.

>  <b>EVANS</b> One bullock knocked up altogether. <i>(Nov 4, 1867,Letter,242</i>) [NOTES: in Vance High Endeavour (1965)]

>  <b>TAiT</b> These horses got no chaff or grass at the mid-day break, and such was their incredible hardihood that none would ever 'knock up.' <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol i,94</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> To push the slow and lame ones beyond their endurance would mean they would 'knock up' and lie down to be left behind. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,85</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> As we were going up the Gentle Annie [near Rai Valley] an old bull knocked up. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,43</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> in the spring the sheep are mustered in for shearing and the horses carry ewes or lambs that have 'knocked up.' <i>(1977,Old Duke : the story of a hard-case horse,7</i>)

### knock-back
 
 <i>n.</i> a refusal, a rebuff

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Well, a knock-back from one of you's isn't going to make me lose any sleep. <i>(1946,That Summer,55</i>) [NOTES: ("Making of a New Zealander")]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The lady quietly brushed him off and told him she would prefer to ... walk the remaining three miles to her home, rather than trust further in riding in a coach one of them would be driving. ... the knockback the men had got from the lady, ... made those fellows come to the conclusion that what they had just come through, was one of the worst accidents ever to happen in those parts. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,36</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> <i>(1960,Maori Girl,161</i>)

>  <i>knock back ... NZ & Austral.</i> refuse, rebuff; <i>knock-back</i> refusal, rebuff. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 622; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 273]]

### knock-backs
 
 <i>n.</i> children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> in the nineteenth century the ring game seems to have been the most popular marble game. (in more recent years marble games with holes have had the greater role.) There was Big Ring, in which a circle a yard or more in diameter was drawn on the ground; there was the mullibar variation, in which an outsize taw was catapulted amongst the marbles in this circle. There were Little Ring, Liney, Follows, Follow the Taw, Holey, and then, on a less important scale, Butcher's Board, in which the marbles were bowled through the holes in a board, Eye-Drop, Knock-Backs, Leggings-Out (or Leggin's-Out), Span-'Em, and Backits. The last was more usually a girls' game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### knock-me-down
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The knock-me-down costume worn is, in the writer's opinion, the dearest part of the turn out. <i>(Aug 11, 1906,NZ Truth,60/3/8</i>)

### knock-shop
 
 <i>n.</i> brothel

>  <b>MARRiNER</b> We effect ignorance of the eyes canvassing us, engage in some halting small-talk, cosy as nuns in a knock-shop. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,71</i>)

### knockabout
 
 <i>n.</i> short for knockabout hand

>  <i>knockabout n.</i> man of all work on a station a rouseabout. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>knockabout n.</i> man of all work on a station, rouseabout. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>PiNNEY</b> Middleton got a full board [in 1885], though fewer Australians had come than usual, and shearers were rather scarce. He commented:`Knockabouts [37] are plentiful enough but three-fourths of them are not worth their salt in a woolshed.' <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,36</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>knockabout</i> handyman, or station hand from c.1875. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,66</i>)

### knockabout hand
 
 <i>n.</i> a rouseabout

>  <b>CHAMiER</b> Two cadets ... [gave] their services as knock-about hands. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,7</i>)

### knockdown
 
 <i>n.</i> [NB usage: I gave him a knockdown (never, `I knocked him down"); an introduction (to a person).]

>  Now i'm a shade on the bashful side myself, as men go, so you'll have to build me up ahead to the Mrs and any stray women folk there may be the station, a man always wants a `knock down' to hop off with when he's breaking a bit of fresh ground with the women, it ain't like city life ... <i>(May 6, 1911,NZ Truth,206/4/6</i>)

>  i was standing in a public hall in Wellington one evening, when i heard one young fellow ask another to `give him a knockdown to that tart in the green skirt'. i gathered that he was asking for an introduction to a young lady. <i>(May 1930,National Education,197</i>) [NOTES: (C.J. Hickson)]

>  <i>Knock Down.</i> - (1) introduction: "He gave me a knock down to his sister.' (C.) (2) Spend (wages) lavishly or quickly, usually on drink. (Canterbury Rhymes, p. 12) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  He called me over and gave me a knock-down [to a girl] <i>(1943,NZ New Writing,1/6</i>) [NOTES: Sargeson Episode]

>  He called me over and gave me a knock-down [to a girl] <i>(1943,NZ New Writing,1/6</i>) [NOTES: Sargeson Episode]

>  in the village he stopped outside his [Bill] old lady's place and yelled: `Breakfast!' He took an age giving a me knockdown to Mum and fooling around with some kids in the wash house, ... <i>(Mar 3, 1950,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>) [NOTES: (P. Llewellyn)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> knock-down: introduction (to someone). <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>)

>  <i>knock-down n.</i> (slang). an introduction: v. of a cheque, to spend it all usually on drink. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's)</i>)

>  <i>n.knock-down n. (Sl.)</i> introduction: v. of a cheque, spend it all, usually on drink. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

### knocked cold
 
 <i>adj.</i> killed

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>knocked</i>, wounded; <i>knocked cold</i>, killed: New Zealanders' and Australians: in G.W., and diminishingly afterwards. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,462</i>)

### knocked out
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> And Willie Cox said he was that knocked out he picked a cigarette up out of the blood and smoked it. My word! he must have been half dotty. <i>(1913,Stories,134,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

### knocked up
 
 <i>adj.</i> pregnant

>  <b>FARRELL</b> She is knocked up. She has a bun in the oven. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,160</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock and workers] exhausted.

>  All hands being knocked up, took it easy. <i>(Jan 12, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> .. if there was a long uninterrupted spell of hot fine weather, all hands and their dogs would be just about knocked up at the end of the run. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,127</i>)

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> They left one knocked up calf. <i>(Dec 24, 1872,Balfour Diaries</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> knocked up - too weary to continue. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,165</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> exhausted, fatigued

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> At last after a fatiguing walk we came to ... the pa ..., where we arrived quite knocked up about nine ... <i>(Apr 6, 1839,Journal,2/87</i>)

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... one Ewe ... being knocked up <i>(1848,Journal,200</i>)

>  <b>STRANGE</b> ... which i reached quite knocked up and exhausted <i>(Mar 7, 1849,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1849 Mar 7 Journal Strange [Naturalist Acheron] 1851 Canterbury Papers 79]

>  ... the doctor quite knocked up with sea-sickness <i>(Oct 3, 1850,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Oct 3 J.E.W. (1951) 39]

>  <b>PHiLLiPS</b> H.P. Seal & Charlie came home ... All very much knocked up. <i>(Dec 22, 1855,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,25</i>)

>  M.A.P. became very much knocked up <i>(1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point</i>)

>  Clendon [Keri Keri] i have been more knocked up this summer - than any other <i>(1861,McLean Papers,19/86 f.</i>)

>  Mustered lamb weaners. Found 26 drowned in a little creek and a large number nocked up. <i>(May 14, 1891,Chudleigh Diary,375,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The mob were too knocked up to go any further. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,239</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Overland travellers arriving knocked-up at the Taramakau mouth had scarcely time to draw breath before hurrying on to Six Mile Creek; <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,147,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>T.C.KiERNAN</b> All hands being knocked up, took it easy. <i>(Jul 12, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 12 T.C. Kiernan Diaryin 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 123]

### knocker
 
 <i>n.</i> shock, blow

>  <b>DAViN</b> The price had been a bit of a knocker. <i>(1956,Sullen Bell,213</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bowler hat

>  <b>SARGESON</b> knocker:<i>hard knocker</i>, bowler hat <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary to 1964 Sargeson Collected Stories 1935-1963 301(1940 Man and Wife 31).]



 
 <i>n.</i> hide, gall

>  The holes and lumps which studded the field were similar to the crude entrenchments of a Maori battlefield, and how the Rugby Union can have the `knocker' to send senior teams to play on such a ground is beyond my understanding. <i>(May 4, 1907,NZ Truth,98/3/1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an itinerant door-to-door salesman during the depression

>  Jim Edwards and his family on ... door-to-door salesmen, or as they called themselves, `the knockers'. <i>(May 17, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1986 May 17 2YA Wellington Spectrum Documentary `The Days of the Knockers']



 
 <i>n.</i> a small leather pad fixed near the heel of hand-shears to keep the blades from closing too far.

>  <i>Knocker</i>. - A small leather pad fixed near the heel of shears to keep the blades from closing too far. (C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  The sheep they shore were not so well woolled on the belly and carried less wool on the points than they do now, so that fewer short blows were required to take off the trimmings; but the shears they used were one inch and a half shorter in the blade and, being joined by a single instead of a double bow, required a wrist of iron to drive them continuously throughout a summer's day. A piece of raw hide known as a knocker is now used to prevent the shears clashing when closed, but before this was introduced the clack and snap of steel meeting steel was a noise inseparable from any busy shearing shed. <i>(1938,High Country,84</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> The shearers buy their shears from the station and then proceed to cut them and bend them, and embellish them with drivers,' `knockers,' "jockeys' and `cockspurs' until their makers would hardly recognise them. <i>ibid.</i> 28 The knockers on the shears silence the sound of steel on steel and all you hear is a faint tap-tap as the shears close, and sound of shuffling feet as the men shift their position. <i>(1951,High Country,26</i>)

>  <i>knocker ... NZ & Austral</i>. (in pl.) leather pad on handle of sheep shears to prevent blades closing completely. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,415</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Knocker.</i> Small pad of leather, flax or binding, usually attached to a spike cut near the heel of the shears to keep the blades from clicking when closing. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  <i>knocker:</i> An inset of leather near the heel of hand shears. These stopped jarring and prevented the blades from closing too far. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/25</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <b>TRiTTON</b> <i>(1959,Time Means Tucker,31/1</i>) [NOTES: [check ?Aust]]

>  A little piece of plastic or leather - the "knocker" - is placed between the blades near the base to soften the impact of each clip. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 62/88</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of knocker-line

>  i think levers should be placed on all levels, so that he [mine engine-driver] could pull the knocker, instead of at the present time he has to put his head out in the shaft and perhaps have to pull about 100-1500 [sic] feet of line. i think there should be a stiff penalty as to anyone being allowed to handle the knocker line except the person in charge, as it tends to confuse the enginedriver. <i>(Nov 3, 1911,Maoriland Worker,35/5/3</i>)

>  <b>BUCKLEY</b> The `Knocker' was heard in the depths of [Cornish] mines, tapping against the rock, out of sight, feared lest he brought down a fall of rock because he was not fed. <i>(1983,Of Toffs and Toilers,34</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small leather pad on shearing blades [used to prevent clashing or overclosing.]

>  knocker: a small leather pad fixed near the heel of shears to keep the blades from closing too far. <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> A piece of rawhide known as a knocker is now used to prevent the shears clashing when closed. <i>(1938,High Country: the evolution of a NZ Sheep Station,84</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The shearers buy their shears from the station and then proceed to cut them and bend them and embellish them with 'drivers', 'knockers', 'jockeys' and 'cockspurs' until their makers would hardly recognise them. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,26</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> knocker: piece of leather or cork placed near the heel of a blade shears to absorb the harsh click when the metal faces meet .. The knocker also prevents the blades from closing too far and thus gashing the ball of the thumb which lies in line with one blade. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,47</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> He puts a 'driver' on it - a strap made of leather or lamp-wick which prevents his hand sliding forward on to the blades - and a 'knocker' which fits between the blades to stop the jar and snap as he closes them. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,91</i>)

### knocker-line
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  i think leavers should be placed on all levels, so that he [mine engine-driver] could pull the knocker, instead of at the present time he has to put his head out in the shaft and perhaps have to pull about 100-1500 [sic] feet of line. i think there should be a stiff penalty as to anyone being allowed to handle the knocker line except the person in charge, as it tends to confuse the enginedriver. as it tends to confuse the enginedriver. <i>(Nov 3, 1911,Maoriland Worker,35/5/3</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Two `knocker-lines' of half-inch-diameter wire-rope were installed in each hoisting compartment so that they could easily be reached from any point in the shaft and signals transmitted to the winding-engine driver. <i>ibid.</i> 325 <i>Knocker-lines</i>: Half-inch-diameter wire-ropes suspended in the shaft and used to signal the winding-engine driver. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,183</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> The only other means of communication with the surface [above the goldmine] was primitive. A long wire, known as the `knocker line', extended down the shaft and was accessible at each level. Each pull rang a bell in the winding house. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,5</i>)

### knocker-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Cranes] pluck the hoppers [of coal] out of their bogies and swing them over the ship's holds. A `knocker-out' strikes the bottom release mechanism, and eight tons of coal pour out. <i>(1948,Our Open Country,105</i>) [NOTES: (`Westport')]

### knocking-down
 
 <i>n.</i> dissipating (cf. knock down v.1)

>  <b>GREY</b> They seemed to derive intense satisfaction from the knocking-down process until their resources were exhausted <i>(1879,His island Home,32</i>)

### knockoff
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... jokers at work breaking the day into periods. From early morning to morning smoko, to lunch, to afternoon smoko, then to knockoff. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,13,1976 ed.</i>)

### knop
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small hollow sphere of wool used as a fill product and for soaking up oil spills. (See [woolspill](../W#woolspill))

>  <b>SQUiRE</b> Knops - are small spheres of wool. The most used are soft knops which are hollow and are an ideal fill for pillows, duvets and chair swabs. They are also useful for soaking up oil spills. They have high bulk and resilience, and are fire resistant. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,24</i>)

>  WRONZ has already started, with Producer Fibre Technologies, such as dust free wool, wool knops ... <i>(Jun 8, 1994,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

>  <b>SQUiRE</b> Woolspill - a WRONZ [Wool Research Organisation of NZ] developed product by which floating booms filled with wool soft knops, trapping up to forty times their own weight, can mop up oil spills. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,44</i>)

### knot
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird

>  <b>HUTTON</b> <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,30</i>)

>  <i>Tringa canutus</i>, Linn. A specimen of this cosmopolitan species (the Common Knot) from Pelorus Sound ... was next commented on. <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/43</i>)

>  The knot (<i>Tringa canutus</i>) is another northern bird which ... travels south across the equator. <i>(1901,TrNZi,33/255</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Huahou, ... 2. Tringa canutus knot</i>; a bird. [1957/65 <i>Huahou ... Calidris cabutis, knot;</i>] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,77</i>)

>  <i>174. Calidris canutus canutus</i> (Linnaeus, 1758) <i>Knot (Huahou) </i>Breeding high arctic latitudes; migrating south and reaching its southernmost limits in New Zealand. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,49,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> Present local authority plans ... could destroy the breeding grounds for ... the rarer bittern, godwit, white-faced heron, shag, pukeko, crake, knot, golden plover and greenshank. <i>(1985,G'day Country,77</i>)

>  <i>Knot Calidris canutus</i> (LiNNAEUS, 1758) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Huahou, lesser knot, red knot. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,203</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> swag

>  <b>PiCARD</b> You may look down on a man who gives his swag, drum, knot, or parcel of bags a shove round the land ... <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,7</i>)

### knotting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The designs were then painted.. and the whole border finally given two coats of knotting. <i>(Nov 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,89</i>)

### knotweed
 
 <i>n.</i> redshank

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> REDSHANK (<i>Polygonum persicaria</i>) is also called persicaria, spotted persicaria, knot weed, smart weed, willow weed, and lady's thumb. Perhaps also it is this weed that on the west coast of the South island is called water pepper or Walker's weed. it is an annual weed of gardens, crops, and wet pastures. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,59,1967 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (locally) water pepper

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> WATER PEPPER (<i>Polygonum hydropiper</i>), called also knot weed in some places, is a plant similar to the former, but it lives in slow-running streams, grows 3 ft. high, and has flower heads 6 inches long, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,59,1967 ed.</i>)

### knowledge economy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Peter Hoar asks whether the Government has finally woken up to the importance of iT, or is its knowledge economy policy still under construction? <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Computer,96</i>)

>  The challenge to Education Minister Trevor Mallard is to do something about it and adequately fund the knowledge economy they are so keen to see in New Zealand and ensure future generations aren't crippled with the same debt as mine. <i>(May 18, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### knuckle
 
 <i>v.</i> to hit with the fist

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Next time i see Southam i'll knuckle him for that. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,151</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a blow with the fist [ca1951 Foxton (Colin Gordon)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> No slouch when it comes to a bit of knuckle- knuckles! (freezing works) a cry to `clear the knuckles'. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,164</i>)

### knuckle down
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Action terms which refer to the shooting of the marbles are: Knuckle down Stiff, Knee High, Funks [Flunks sic] or Funnicks, Slips, Firsts and Lasts and Forces. ... The term `Knuckle down Stiff' (Taranaki [18]80, Gisborne [18]85, Waitara [18]95) is mentioned by [Lady A.B. Gomme Vo. 1, p.113]. ... As Taranaki [18]80 says, `The term was called out in order to prevent too easy a success against a marble near the ring'. it was less advantageous to fire with the hand on the ground than with the hand elevated, perhaps at `Knee High'. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Many heated arguments arose as to who said `everythings' or `nothings' first. if a player's `taw' touched a marble and lay alongside it, the others would shout `knuckle down' just before his turn. ... `Knuckle down' meant that the player had to hold his taw close to the marble attacked and fire it in that very inconvenient position. ... When a boy lost all his marbles at play he was `scat' [?related to skint ?HWO] . ... A `ringer' was a taw which, when fired at the commencement of the game, lodged in the track of the ring itself; that, and knocking a marble out when he `fired' were the perfect shots. ... To fire a shot while holding the hand nearer to the ring than where the taw had lain, was to `funk', an unforgiveable crime. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,176</i>) [NOTES: [Waitara, Taranaki, 1899: reference, game of Little Ring]]

### knuckle-down stiff
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

### knuckle-duster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HiSLOP</b> i have mounted many nuggets in my time. A small pick and shovel with a nugget attached used to be a great seller, as was also the heavy signet ring with a nugget on top - `knuckle-dusters' they were billed. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,102</i>)

### knuckle-up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,165</i>)

### knucklebones
 
 <i>n.</i> the game and the objects

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> in the game of `knuckle-bone' (<i>koruru</i>) round pebbles were used in a game played [56] with the hands. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,55</i>)

>  <b>GRiEVE</b> [Playground] Crazes came and went [in the 1930s]. One week it would be `stag-knife', another week `knuckle bones'. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,87,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> in Summer - Cat's Cradles (Newtown [18]90, Dunedin [19]09); ... Trolleys on summer nights (Wellington ([19]30); Knucklebones (Newtown [18]90, Dunedin ([19]09); ... <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,1/342</i>)

>  <i>(1968,Landfall,22/362</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> <i>Knucklebones</i> (K-34) was played in both Europe and Polynesia, independently at first. in the nineteenth century distinct European [177] and Maori versions were known. More recently, the two types have merged, and today the Maori children, who are mainly responsible for perpetuating the game in New Zealand, include in their games movements borrowed from the European tradition. ... in most of the provinces the game was known as Knucklebones, and it is widely reported as having been played by New Zealand children of European origin until about 1900. Occasionally it was known as Chucks, Chuckstones, Chuckystones, Fingerstones, Hucklebones, Jacks, Jackstones, Knuckles, or Knucklestones. Children still played it here and there throughout the country in 1949, but they knew little about it until plastic knuckles were imported in 1954 in large quantities and revived the game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,176</i>)

>  <b>ROSiER-JONES</b> She takes her knuckle bones out of her pocket, and throws them into the air. She can beat all the other kids at knuckle bones. ... She has gone through one-sies, two-sies, and [86] three-sies without one knuckle bone falling off her hand. <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,85</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> <i>Knucklebones</i> (K-34) was played in both Europe and Polynesia, independently at first. in the nineteenth century distinct European [177] and Maori versions were known. More recently, the two types have merged, and today the Maori children, who are mainly responsible for perpetuating the game in New Zealand, include in their games movements borrowed from the European tradition. ... in most of the provinces the game was known as Knucklebones, and it is widely reported as having been played by New Zealand children of European origin until about 1900. Occasionally it was known as Chucks, Chuckstones, Chuckystones, Fingerstones, Hucklebones, Jacks, Jackstones, Knuckles, or Knucklestones.  <i>abbr.</i> Kerridge-Odeon.

>  He had gone to Sir Robert Kerridge's office, the millionaire who has a big new building in Queen Street [Auckland], and offered ... a publicity stunt if K.O. would put up the money ... <i>(1984,Some Other Country,141</i>) [NOTES: 1965 Stead `A Fitting Tribute' in 1984 Some Other Country 141]

### ko
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori digging stick, a spade

>  Pereira takes me into climate-controlled storage rooms holding.. a 6 m-high rack of ko (Maori digging sticks)... <i>(Jul 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 58/83</i>)

>  Te Rauparaha used a ko as his preferred weapon and his blade was wood, so let's keep it safe. <i>(Feb 19, 2004,Dominion Post,inside Out supplement/10</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> They have another wooden tool about seven feet long, pointed like a hedge stake, and a piece of wood lashed on about two feet from the ground to place the foot upon to aid in thrusting the instrument into the ground. They call the tool koko. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals,165,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ko, s. ... also a certain tool with which the natives of New Zealand plant their sweet potatoes: a perforator. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,164</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> We passed through a native plantation, or potato ground, where the inhabitants were busily employed in planting their crops. They were using the <i>ko</i>, a wooden instrument something resembling a spade, with which they root up the matted fibres of the fern below the surface of the soil. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/290</i>)

>  This implement (called a <i>ko</i>) might be just as well termed a lance, or pick; . <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/89fn</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> [caption] CULTiVATiNG SOiL WiTH THE KO, OR DiGGiNG STiCK. <i>ibid.</i> 321 The principal agricultural tool was the digging- stick (<i>ko</i>), this being a pole or shaft of hard wood from seven to ten feet in length, with a step or foot-rest (<i>hamaruru</i>) lashed on about a foot from the end that entered the ground, this end being pointed or with an edge at foot and sides. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,40</i>)

>  . the gouge-like impressions of the wooden ko with which it had been dug. <i>(1911,TrNZi,43/597</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [As Rakaihaitu] went from place to place he hollowed out with his magical <i>ko</i>, or wooden sharp-pointed digging implement, the beds of those great lakes. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol ii South island) 112</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The <i>ko</i> was about seven feet long, sharp at the digging end, with a step or rest for the foot about a foot from the point. The top of the <i>ko</i> was slightly curved, and it was often carved into the form of a head. [sic] and adorned with feathers. it was also a custom of the <i>ko</i> -men to decorate the top of the implement, near the head, with <i>aurei</i>, the bone mat-pins formerly commonly used, ... <i>(1930,Maori, The,184</i>)

>  <i>ko</i> : Maori spade. <i>(1947,Reed Dictionary of Modern Maori,174</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `There's an artefact down there,' he said firmly. `A ko i think, or perhaps a spear.' <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,126</i>) [NOTES: (Don't Blame Yourself)]

>  The ever-reaching strong hand of death is another image commonly spoken about at Maori gatherings today, but it is not clear whether the stylized hand portrayed on meeting house maihi (bargeboards), or on ko (spades), or on some very old examples of taonga have the same korero attached to them. <i>(1984,Te Maori,27</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The <i>ko</i> -men worked across from the corner, gradually extending their front as the field opened out; and all kept time as they worked, moving the handles of the <i>ko</i> to the right and left alternately, as they made the holes for the reception of the seed <i>kumara. </i> <i>(1930,Maori, The,186</i>)

>  its long sweet whistle seems to the Maoris to utter a call for all who hear them to take up the "ko" - the native tool for digging. <i>(Sep 28, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

### Ko Huiarau
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori parliamentary structure [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  More than a thousand people gather at the velodrome in Manukau, Auckland, to celebrate a much-awaited dawn ceremony before witnessing certification to representatives of various tribal areas for their involvement in the newly reconvened Ko Huiarau Parliament, an ancient parliamentary structure set up in 1808. <i>(Mar 11, 2000,Dominion,27</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### koarea
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Trachichthodes</i>, golden snapper, red snapper (See [golden snapper](../G#golden-snapper))

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species i will name a part, viz. ... Rauwarea [koarea], a snapper; ... <i>[Glossary </i>118] koarea golden snapper (<i>trachichthodes affinis</i>)]. <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40</i>)

>  AUSTROBERYX AFFiNiS (Gunther[u-dieresis]). Golden Snapper; <i>Koarea.</i> A highly esteemed food fish, of very beautiful colours when first taken from the water. ...Distribution: Common, Bay of islands and Great Barrier island; taken as far south as Cape Campbell. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Trachichthodes affinis</i> (Guenther). GOLDEN SNAPPER; KOAREA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,24,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GOLDEN SNAPPER (KOAREA) <i>Centroberyx affinis</i> (Gunther) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,175</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE GOLDEN SNAPPER<i> Trachichthodes affinis (Gunther) </i>... Maori name: KOAREA. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,50</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> RED SNAPPER ... <i>Other names: Trachichthodes affinis</i>; golden snapper; nannygai (Australia); koarea (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,118</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> RED SNAPPER [<i>Trachichthodes affinis</i>] ... Also known as golden snapper, and koarea (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Rocky and sandy grounds, in the north, are the home of <i>Trachichthodes </i>... , a golden fish known variously as golden snapper, koarea, and (in Australia) nannygai. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,215</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>GOLDEN SNAPPER Centroberyx affinis (Trachichthodes affinis)</i> (Nannygai, red snapper, koarea) ... in spite of its name the golden snapper is not related to the true snapper and is in fact more similar to the roughys. ... Schools of golden snapper often move out over open sand flats ... and are then regularly caught in bottom trawls and sold as red fish. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,177,1984 ed.</i>)

### koareare
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Typha angustifolia</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Koware, <i>Lindsay. Typha angustifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>) [NOTES: ?is this correct W.122 <i>ko[o]areare. </i>]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Koareare is the edible part of the root of the raupo, and was got in the hapua (lagoons) and re (swamps). The name runs off the tongue glibly when given its southern pronunciation, `ko-a-ray-a-ray'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]

 <i>n.</i>

### koaro
 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The Tuwharetoa Tribe had a little lake called Rotoaira, and they had native fish there called the kowaro. <i>(1926,NZ Parliamentary Debates,CCXi/289</i>)

>  Whitebait are the juveniles of our migratory galaxiid fishes. These scaleless fish species include the inanga (around much of the country), banded kokopu (especially around Auckland) and koaro (especially in streams draining bush-covered and mountain areas. <i>(Apr 2005,National Waterways Project,18/1</i>)

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> Eventually, they were identified as the young of banded kokopu (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>), shortjawed kokopu (<i>Galaxias postvectis</i>), and koaro (<i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i>). <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,10</i>)

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> The second most common species is the koaro (<i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i>), often called mountain trout. it is most common in milky, snow-fed rivers, where it may make up half the catch, especially following floods.<i>ibid.</i> 49 Koaro occur among boulders in rapids, and they are seldom visible in such inaccessible habitats. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,13</i>)

>  Last May, two fish were found in a trench being cleared in Newtown. One was a kokopu and the other was a koaro. <i>(Jan 29, 2001,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> Whitebait in pristine condition are beautiful... They are the fry of several species of Galaxiads, maculatus, brevipinnis, argenteus, postvectis, and fasciatus (or inaka, koaro, and three kinds of kokopu). <i>(1989,Homeplaces,104</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> The koara is a small spotted fish from 1 1/2in. to 2 1/2 in. in length found only in Lake Rotoaira, south of Taupo. it is said by the Natives to resemble the koara of the sea in shape, <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,142</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> ELEPHANT EARS A species of whitebait. The young of the native Koaro, so named by West Coast whitebaiters because of their large and projecting pectoral fins. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,61</i>)

>  The trout food that was so reduced in Lake Taupo, and about which Rotorua Maori were concerned, was primarily koaro. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/17</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> it may be useful to give a list of the different fishes the Maoris distinguish in the Taupo district, ... Koaro. - Not found in Taupo Lake, but only in Rotoaira. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,129</i>)

>  GALAXiAS HUTTONi Regan. <i>Koaro</i>. Vast shoals appear in Lake Roto-aira in November. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/119</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Galaxias huttoni</i> Regan. KOARO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,14,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Galaxias huttoni</i>: [European Name ?] [Maori Name] Koaro. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> KOARO <i>Galaxias koaro</i> n. sp. ... The koaro has previously been considered identical with Galaxias huttoni described by Dr. Tate Regan from a South island lake. <i>ibid.</i> 36 The koaro is known only from Lakes Roto-pounamu and Rotoaira. Maori efforts to acclimatize the species in Taupo have failed over many generations, according to what an old Maori told me. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,35</i>)

>  <b>DENNiS</b> The juveniles of five species of galaxias make up the whitebait catch: inanga (<i>Galaxias maculatus</i>), koaro (<i>G. brevipinnis</i>), banded kokopu (<i>G. fasciatus</i>), giant kokopu (<i>G. argenteus</i>) and short-jawed kokopu (<i>G. postvectis</i>). <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,187</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> The whitebait species that develop in the sea are: <i>Galaxias argenteus ..., G. fasciatus ..., G. postvectis ..., G. brevipinnis </i>..., and <i>G. maculatus </i>... An adult koaro, <i>Galaxias brevipinnis </i>is illustrated here but the whitebait of all five species are similar to this in shape <i>(1982,Collins Guide,109,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The koaro (also known as `maehe') is found in Lake Waikaremoana, ... <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,74</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Koaro Galaxias brevipinnis</i> Widely distributed ... Good, large whitebait. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,48</i>)

>  For inanga, who are not strong climbers like their cousins, the koaro and short-jaw galaxiids, this is the end of the road. <i>(Aug 26, 2006,Dominion Post,E7</i>)

>  inanga are the most common species, but whitebaiters may also capture the young of giant kokopu, koaro and banded kokopu. "Shortjaw and giant kokopu are described as threatened species. ..." <i>(Aug 17, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

>  Lake Chalice, because of its underground, steep outlet-stream, contains only the native koaro... <i>(1990,Tramping in South island Forest Parks,19</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Marine) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Before European settlement, Maori netted koaro whitebait - <i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i> - by the hundredweight in Lake Taupo. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> growing tip

### koau
 
 <i>n.</i> a shag, kawau

>  shag - ka'h [koau, kawau] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 282 (|= macron precedes) shag, ka|h [koau] c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Koau - the ordinary shag found on the sea coast; a general name for all shags. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]

### koauau
 
 <i>n.</i> a flute

>  After dinner the old man produced his flute; it was not a koauau: it was a piece of gas pipe about 10 inches long, with three holes at the lower end ... whilst the metal was not so melodious as the wood, nor so mellow, the result was pleasing and sounded very well. <i>(Feb 28, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),26</i>) [NOTES: From c1920]

>  He [bagpipes] sound like one t'ousand koauau, Maori flute ... <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,164</i>)

>  He proceeded toward the edge of the cliff, slowly, breathing into his <i>koauau</i>, to warm it into a sweet tone and to supple his fingers for the running of the <i>rangi</i>, the air to his <i>waiata. </i> <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,35</i>) [NOTES: J.C. ANDERSEN]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>koauau</i>: kind of musical instrument played with the nose; also a flute for the mouth. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  On Sunday, he will play the koauau - an end-blown flute - the putorino - an instrument that is both flute and trumpet at the same time - the pukaea - a "lusty and spectacular" six-foot long trumpet - and the nguru - a tiny wooden flute played with the nose. ... Listen closely and you might just hear the evocative sound of a Maori flute. You may never have seen a koauau, but the sounds of the instrument, and others like it, permeate our homes and workplaces almost every day. <i>(Sep 24, 2004,Dominion Post,B10</i>)

### koe
 
 <i>n.</i> you, second pers. pro., sing., dual, and plural

>  <i>koe, korua, koutou,</i> second pers. pro., sing., dual, and plural. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### koeae
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of granite trout, <i>H. meandratus.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ko e ai; A certain fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,165</i>)

### koeaea
 
 <i>n.</i> hiwihiwi (<i>Chironemus fergussoni</i>)

>  The hiwihiwi (<i>Chironemus fergussoni</i>), tupuku, ngutere, tangahangaha, and koeaea (<i>Corridodax pullus</i> Forster) (butterfish), are amongst those caught <i>(1926,TrNZi,58/646</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

### koehoperoa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [koekoea](../K#koekoea))

### koeke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The principal indigenous insects not found in England are the Koeke[e1-acute], a large locust-grasshopper (flying like a jack-snipe), ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/23</i>)

### koekoe
 
 <i>n.</i> ?=Tetrapathea tetrandra, NZ Passion flower ?? kohia.

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|oe k|oe; A certain shrub. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,165</i>)

### koekoea
 
 <i>n.</i> formerly called screamer; longtailed cuckoo, kohoperoa, long-tailed cuckoo, koehoperoa, kohoperoa. (See [cf. pipiwhauroa](../P#pipiwhauroa))

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> The koekoea is the long-tailed cuckoo. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,10</i>)

>  i also hear the distinctive screech of a koekoea, the long-tailed cuckoo, and watch as it timidly flies high into a nearby tree. <i>(Nov 19, 2005,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Two migra-[123]tory cuckoos (the Cuculus fasciatus, Forst.), called kohaperoa, or koekoia, and a very small and beautiful kind (the Cuculus nitems of the same author), called by the natives pipiwawaroa - which appear on the coast at Christmas - mark the period of the first potato-harvest. <i>ibid.</i> 2/193 40. <i>Eudynamys taitensis</i>. ... Kohaperoa of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. `Koheperoa', from a specimen. <i>Miss Stone.</i> Kohapiroa.<i> Polack?</i> Koekoia of the natives. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/122</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/193]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> in early spring the koekoea's note is weak and feeble. ... This the the New Zealand cuckoo a bird of passage. <i>(Dec 25, 1852,Journal,8/62</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the koekoea, second cuckoo, arrives. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/115</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 54. EUDYNAMiS TAiTiENSiS. Sparrm. Long-tailed Cuckoo. Koekoea <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,20</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> EUDYNAMiS TAiTENSiS. (LONG-TAiLED CUCKOO.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Koekoea, Kawekawea, and Koheperoa \{,and Kohaperoa\}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,75,{1888 2nd edn 1/127} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> EUDYNAMiS TAiTENSiS. (LONG-TAiLED CUCKOO.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Koekoea, Kawekawea, and Koheperoa \{,and Kohaperoa\}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,75,{1888 2nd edn 1/127} ed.</i>)

>  <i>The Kohoperoa or Koekoea, Long-tailed Cuckoo</i> (Urodynamis taitensis) <i>(1904,TrNZi,36/113</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The shining cuckoo, or <i>pipi-wharauroa, </i>and the long-tailed cuckoo, called koekoea and also kohoperoa, are regular summer migrants from the tropics, ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,NZ (Vol ii South island) 170</i>)

>  <i>koekoea[a], ko[o]hoperoa</i>, a migratory bird (Urodynamis taitensis), long-tailed cuckoo, screamer; winters in Samoa group. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>koekoea</i>: long-tailed cuckoo. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Feathers of the albatross (<i>toroa</i>), long-tailed cuckoo (<i>koekoea</i>), and heron (<i>kotuku</i>) were also valued. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,284</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> LONG-TAiLED CUCKOO <i>Eudynamis taitensis Maori names</i>: Koekoea, Kawekawea, Kohoperoa. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,181</i>)

>  <i>228. Eudynam[y]s taitensis</i> (Sparrman, 1787)<i> Long-tailed Cuckoo (Koekoea) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,60,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> Also another Maori ... the small boy is raggedly dressed in brown -he wears a brown felt hat with a `koe-koea' feather placed rakishly to the side. <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,61</i>) [NOTES: 1978 Gordon]

>  This cuckoo, the koekoea, is not to be confused with the bronze of shining cuckoo, called by the Maoris pipiwharauroa. <i>(Dec 7, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

### koekohe
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kohikohi](../K#kohikohi))

### koha
 
 <i>n.</i> golden handshake, compensation

>  After paying an $83,000 tax bill for a Hawke's Bay health trust that paid its workers and trustees "koha", the Health Ministry has warned other trusts not to make the same request... inland Revenue said yesterday that koha is taxed at 33 per cent if it is deemed to be a payment for business... inland Revenue says, in relation to koha, "efforts on behalf of Maori" will be subjected to tax, once expenses have been deducted, because it is considered to be part of normal business income. <i>(Nov 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  A Hawke's Bay health trust paid its workers and volunteers "koha" for work done... Wellington Tenths trustee Peter Love says using koha to pay workers and volunteers is a "misinterpretation" of the ancient practice, which is restricted to particular circumstances. "That is what i know koha to be all about. it should never be paid for work like that and it's not a regular payment. it is a discreet payment that is very private between who is paying and who is getting it. it is done with reverence and solemnity." <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Helen Clark said no MP should receive cash for a service or from someone soliciting a service - regardless of whether it was called koha or lafo. <i>(Sep 5, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> donation, gift

>  And no one can quibble about the cost - a koha to the monks for the privilege of sharing their life. <i>(Jun 2003,Wel-com (Wellington),12</i>)

>  Eva would also have been pleased to see the people eat fish till it was coming out their eyeballs -thanks to a huge koha from Moana Pacific Fisheries. <i>(Feb 1998,Mana,No. 20: 6</i>)

>  Each night they'd put on a concert, and hope to gather enough koha to put petrol in the bus for the next leg of the journey. <i>(Dec 1998,Mana,No. 25: 6</i>)

>  Simply pass around the hat and casually mention that if they expect clean linen, a good night's sleep and their bellies filled then they can cough up a koha. <i>(Sep 27, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)

>  [Hdg] Koha payment focus of Kapitit case. One person's illegal payment is another's koha or gift, the Environment Court was told yesterday... People would aks him how much koha was required and he told them, similar to how marae advised propsective visitors on an appropriate amount. <i>(Aug 27, 2002,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  Plates of biscuits or sandwiches go to the kitchen and a two dollar koha is chucked into the kitty. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 14 :64</i>)

>  A $15 weekly charge [at the language nursery] has been suggested, but not finalised. Payment might be made by way of the traditional Maori koha (contribution). <i>(Mar 23, 1982,Evening Post,5/5</i>)

>  The chief Shinto of Japan, the Rev Hirooki Nukaga, yesterday gave the Prime Minister, Mr Muldoon, koha for Koha. Koha, the Maori word for contribution or gift, is the name of a sacred white horse given to the Nikko Toshogu shrine by Mr Muldoon during a visit last year. <i>(Dec 7, 1982,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>HULM3E</b> 293 Koha = gift <i>(1983,Bone People, The,468</i>)

>  instead of paying an entrance fee, people seeing Te Maori would be asked to give koha (a gift). ... `Notices in Maori and English will be put up, with the English notices, for example, saying voluntary contributions are expected, ... The suggested koha would probably be about $3-$5. <i>(Jul 15, 1986,Evening Post,38</i>)

>  The gift of money had been left [by the TV crew] as a koha for food. <i>(Sep 1986,New Outlook,10</i>)

>  Each one had to get an offering, a koha, for their own family. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of History,26</i>) [NOTES: (Binney)]

>  The committee organising Te Maori in New Zealand was working on the notices suggesting the koha system, which would be worded in a way which `isn't threatening, but explains why contributions are expected.' <i>(Jul 15, 1986,Evening Post,38</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera</b> Each patient pays Paraiti in coin or in food - a koha, no matter how small. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,253</i>)

>  Koha can be split into two types. The first, helping cover costs of a function. The other,. paying individuals for a service. The main things koha is used to pay for are at tangi, hui, visits to other marae and special functions. <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The unprecedented number of young opera hopefuls raising funds for overseas study has meant a surfeit of opera and koha for family and opera buffs. <i>(Aug 16, 2000,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  i was one of the few young kids that went down on a sledge with my father, pulled by a horse and asked for a koha (donation) from the campers that came to Matauri Bay every year, in order to pay our rates. <i>(Apr 6, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  Koha is the Maori term for gift giving and is an important part of the Maori way of life. Koha is a traditional part of the powhiri, where the manuhiri are invited to a marae, and lay down their gifts for the tangata whenua. The koha is laid down by the last speaker of the manuhiri, it signifies that there are no more speakers and the manuhiri have finished. <i>(Feb 26, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  The late night atmosphere will be laid back and entry by koha. <i>(Sep 9, 1992,Capital Times,1</i>)

>  They don't begrudge what they are given. "it's koha," says Hemopo. "it's from the heart." <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Then perhaps he'll teach broadcasting skills to young Maori, although not for money: "i'm a koha bloke." <i>(Apr 26, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  My uncle died, and i had to give a koha at the tangi. <i>(Nov 30, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

### koha-for-consent process
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The so-called koha-for-consent process is certainly a result of the Resource Management Act passed under the previous National Government... <i>(Jul 29, 2004,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

### kohaha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> This morning we found a kohaha tree, the berries of which the natives are very fond of. <i>(May 23, 1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 May 23 Brunner Exped Middle isd. in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 317]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> This morning we found a kohaha tree, the berries of which the natives are very fond of. <i>(1848,Great Journey,96,1952 ed.</i>)

### kohai
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kowhai](../K#kowhai))



 
 <i>n.</i> variant of kowhai q.v.

>  <b>MONRO</b> The kohai is also a very common tree in the woods [in Otago], and grows to a large size. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 242]

### kohalition
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Maori Party, whose overhang doomed National, seem to have missed out completely even though it was looking a sure bet, a gift, a kohalition. <i>(Oct 16, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C18</i>)

### kohanga
 
 <i>n.</i> short for kohanga reo q.v.

>  [Hdg] Kohanga man accused of abuse. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>MACRAE</b> He said he needed it for kohanga business. ... The kohanga were getting a grant of twelve thou a year. <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,142</i>)

### kohanga reo
 
 <i>n.</i> A `language nursery' where (esp. urban) Maori pre-school children can learn and enjoy their language and culture; Maori language nursery. [ORIGIN: Maori, see also language nest]

>  i'm pleased to see <i>Kohanga Reo</i> showing language being not only spoken but also muttered/sung/chanted/shouted. <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),82</i>)

>  The park is building a kohanga reo as well. <i>(Aug 1991,Metro,66</i>)

>  [She] told the Post today she had been used as a scapegoat and the problems of the kohanga went even deeper than simply her presence there... He was surprised the kohanga had gone. The marae wanted to consult with the kohanga and have a say in its management, <i>(Aug 11, 1999,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Thye also visited a Wainuiomata marae to get input from kohanga reo and Access scheme organisers and from Access participants. <i>(Aug 27, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  A decision to set up a kohanga reo (language nest) was made by the Maoris in the association. <i>(May 17, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  The name comes from a 14th-century kohanga reo movement. Provencal ... was the predominant literary and poetic language, but there were fears it was dying for lack of everyday use. A group of culture vultures formed The Very Gay Company of the Seven Troubadors of Toulouse to stop the rot. <i>(Oct 26, 2002,Dominion Post,F13</i>)

>  The nga kohanga reo (language nurseries) will open now in Wellington and Wainuiomata ... <i>(Mar 23, 1982,Evening Post,5/5</i>)

>  ....effort was being redirected into establishing kohanga reo, a language nursery where young children can learn Maori. <i>(Jul 14, 1982,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Schools should hire native Maori speakers on contract to teach Maori to new entrants who have been through the language nursery (kohanga reo) programme <i>(Jul 28, 1983,Dominion,2</i>)

>  ...people who have skills and experience as well as the motivation to assist the kohanga reo (language nursery) movement now booming throughout New Zealand...."There can be no question the kohange reo, besides perpetuating Maori language and culture, will enhance the life-style of young Maori New Zealanders in a very significant way.' <i>(Jul 29, 1983,Dominion,2</i>)

>  At present there are 35 language assistants, people from the community who are fluent Maori speakers with an in-depth knowledge of things Maori. They help in primary schools with kohanga reo children and help teachers in taha Maori. <i>(May 17, 1986,Dominion,5</i>)

>  But there are things, he says, he will remember with `great vividness.' One is his visit to the Waiwhetu kohanga reo during last year's language hearing. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  The gate across the road into the Nga Hau e Wha Christchurch National marae is chained and double- padlocked. Behind the palisade fence the weeds are knee high. The whare nui is boarded up ... None of its carvings and tukutuku are in place and the carvings are still tapu. The whare wananga beside it, meant to house the kohanga reo, lies uncompleted, torn building paper flapping. <i>(Mar 5, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <b>MACRAE</b> Renei's not been long at a new kohanga reo and the kuia had a few things she wanted to discuss. <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,142</i>)

>  Government allocation of funds for te kohanga reo (Maori language pre-school centres) will be put on the same footing as early childhood services from February next year. <i>(Jul 28, 1989,Dominion,5</i>)

>  Mariu spends most of her time on her patch, clucking at the kohanga reo kids reminding them to korero Maori when they slip into English in moments of excitement, ... <i>(Jan 1987,Metro,111</i>)

>  Because the kohanga reo grew so rapidly for so long, they are still often seen as an undimmed triumph... The number of children attending the kohanga - both licensed and unlicensed - peaked at 14,000 in 1996, according to the Ministry of Education. <i>(Sep 15, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  At kohanga reo English is never spoken. <i>(Jul 24, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

>  [They] have recently formed a kohanga reo, a whanau support group, a matua whangai group and a kokiri work skills unit. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> We have our language nests now. Kohanga reo <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,83</i>)

>  Major provisions include:.. provision of fully funded and resourced kohanga reo, kura kaupapa Maori and wharekura for every marae... <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/100</i>)

>  This year marks the tenth anniversary of one of the fastest growing parts of the education system - the Kohanga Reo. <i>(Nov 26, 1992,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [kohoperoa](../K#kohoperoa))

### kohatu
