### kohe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> The trees which the natives chiefly make subservient to their purposes, are [besides the pines cowrie, totarra, towha and zarida mentioned above] ... the henow, from which they extract a black dye, the towha, a tree resembling the sycamore, the <i>river river,</i> the grain of whose wood is similar to that of the beech, a species of cork-tree, called by these people vow, a large tree named eckoha, and another named kycata, a tall and beautiful tree, ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/245</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Hartighsea <i>spectabilis</i>, Adr.Juss.; ... Nat. name, `Kohe', <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> 1. <i>Tetrapathaea tetrandra</i>, kohia

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Passiflora tetrandra. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>) [NOTES: check this: ?mistranscribed Koke]

### kohea
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Passiflora tetrandra</i> Kohea

>  Passiflora tetrandra Kohea <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/124</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

### kohekohe
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i>, a spreading tree of northern coastal forests, with large glossy leaves; aka New Zealand cedar [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [also kohe](../A#also-kohe))

>  A century of vigorous regeneration has seen its once denuded lower slopes now clothed in tall kanuka, pohutukawa, puriri, kohekohe, karaka and mahoe forest. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/16</i>)

>  .. into the crowned hillside groves of kohe kohe and back to a gully of totara and other trees ... <i>(Aug 2005,Capital Times,6</i>)

>  The kohekohe trees, which are also scarce in the rest of New Zealand, are currently facing the driest season since records began in 1872. <i>(May 31, 2001,City Voice,2</i>)

>  in a winter when flowering and fruiting of kohekohe is abundant, the tuis and other native birds have a feast, not only of nectar from the flowers, but also of the red, fleshy coats of the seeds. <i>(Jul 9, 2005,Dominion Post,E17</i>)

>  At Motatau, where the broad-leave native tree kohekohe was a major food for possums, half of 50 trees surveyed in 1997 were almost totally defoliated. <i>(Jul 26, 2000,Evening Post,34</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The predominance of small wooden buildings with vertical cladding and kohekohe shingles were a distinctive feature of the town. <i>(1983,illustrated History of Taranaki,35</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> eckoha [=? e kohe, or? (ko) hekohe] <i>(1817,Narrative of a Voyage,2/245</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Kohekohe (<i>Laurus kohekohe</i>) - A fine handsome tree, .. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),48</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>Kohikohi</i> another of the many laurel trees of the soil, grows to a heighth [sic] of fifty feet. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/398</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> i noticed ... a butterfly very nearly resembling the Admirable (English) and a tree called the kohekohe which flowers in the winter ... <i>(Apr 13, 1839,Journal,2/100</i>)

>  <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/11/139</i>)

>  <b>HOGSKiN</b> Kohekohe is a fine tree ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,27</i>)

>  The kohe kohi [sic] is a fine grained red wood ... much used for shingles; ... <i>(1843,Letters from New Plymouth,58,1968 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Nov 27 in 1843 Letters from New Plymouth (1968) 58]

>  The Kohekohe is a fine tree ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,105,1956 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1844 J.B.Williams NZ Journal (1956) 105]

>  We had our blocks at work today pulling down some stiff stumps of Kohikohi (a red wood used for fencing and roof-shingles). <i>(Jun 30, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/99,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> We slept under the shelter of a koke-koke tree, and just upon the verge of the forest [at Nelson]. <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,1/49</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kohe-kohe, <i>Col[enso]. Dysoxylum spectabile. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  The Kohekohe, (<i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i>), is a handsome tree which is only found plentifully in the North parts of the island. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/46</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>KiRK</b> in the North island the native kohe-kohe (<i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i>), which yields a tough reddish-coloured wood ... is also caled cedar by the settlers. <i>ibid.</i> 22 KOHE-KOHE.- (<i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,14</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kohekohe ... reaches sixty feet. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/191</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i>, Hook. Kohekohe.--A large tree, 40-50 feet high. its leaves are bitter, and used to make a stomachic infusion; wood tough but splits <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,127</i>)

>  [Native name] Kohekohe .. [Settler' name] Cedar .. [Family] Meliaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> DYSOXULUM SPECTABiLE (<i>Hook., F</i>.) ... The `KOHEKOHE'>- This interesting tree is indigenous to the Northern island, but is often local. ... The tree is generally called by the settlers `The native Cedar'. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Kohe-kohe [Maori name] .. Cedar [Settlers' name] .. Dysoxylum spectabile .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,83</i>)

>  Kohekohe (cedar) .. Dysoxylum spectabile .. Wood light, soft, and easily worked, heart blood-red in colour; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>kohekohe n.</i> Maori name for a New Zealand tree, sometimes called Cedar, <i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i>, Hook (N.O. <i>Meliaceae</i>). <i>(1898,Austral English,251</i>)

>  The higher portion of the hills and steep spurs are clothed with the various species and variety of birch (beech), to which along the shores of the [Marlborough] sounds were added pukatea and kohekohe, the latter locally called cedar. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,17</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i>] Kohekohe. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,96</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWEL;L</b> <i>Dysoxylum spectabile (The Kohekohe)</i>. A tree, often 50 ft. in height. ... This tree is known to settlers as the New Zealand Cedar. Maori name, <i>Kohekohe. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,222,1940 p. 235 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWEL;L</b> <i>Dysoxylum spectabile (The Kohekohe)</i>. A tree, often 50 ft. in height. ... This tree is known to settlers as the New Zealand Cedar. Maori name, <i>Kohekohe. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,222,1940 p. 235 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> Of the primeval growth that once covered [Stephen island, Marlborough], little or nothing remains - only here and there an inconsiderable grove of cedar - kohekohe - a species difficult to burn ... . <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,97</i>)

>  <i>kohekohe</i> , a tree (Dysoxylum spectabile), fragrant flowers hanging from stems and bole in mid-winter. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>kohekohe</i> tall tree. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Kohekohe .. .. kohekohe .. .. <i>Dysoxylum spectabile</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  The various kinds of timber that are found in New Zealand that are valuable for building and other purposes are :- kauri; yellow pine (Dammara Australis);.. matai or mai, black pine (Podocarpus spicala);.. kohekohe, cedar (Hartighsea spectabilis); rewarewa, honeysuckle (Knightea excelsa); tanekaha or tawaiwai, pitch pine (Phyllocladus trichomenoides); manoaa, yellow pine (Dacrydium colensoi). <i>(Dec 13, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas Suppl. 1</i>)

### koheriki
 
 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a highly perfumed native plant, smelling of aniseed and added by shepherds to tobacco.

>  Another highly perfumed native plant growing there [New Plymouth] was the koheriki (aniseed - Angelica rosaefolia) highly appreciated by shepherds as well as their flocks. in fact, shepherds are so fond of it that it is becoming scarce in a wild state, and shepherds often dry the leaves and rub them up in their tobacco to give it an aroma. Koheriki is a little plant 1ft to 2 ft in height that is very suitable on rock-work in the open. <i>(Jan 21, 1929,NZ Journal of Agriculture,61</i>)

### koheru
 
 <i>n.</i> an elongated smallish sea-fish of north-eastern coastal waters, electric blue on back, silver-white on belly [Maori koheru[o-macron]].

### kohimako
 
 <i>n.</i> korimako

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNiS MELANURA. (BELL-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

### kohoperoa
 
 <i>n.</i> longtailed cuckoo, koehoperoa (See [koekoea](../K#koekoea))

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Kohaperoa </i>- This bird is remarkable for its long body, and short cock's beak. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),65</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the birds that distinguished themselves in this woodland concert, were the tui, or mocking-bird, the korimaku [a-acute], kohapiroa, and tiaki [a-acute]. The little restless piwakawaka [a2,a4-acute], a kind of thrush, who is incessantly hopping from twig to twig, put forth its single note. <i>ibid.</i> 1/300 The <i>kohapiroa</i> : this little fellow is remarkable for taking particular care of itself,... it fills the bushes with melodious notes, only equalled by musical bells : ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/108</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Two migra-[123]tory cuckoos (the Cuculus fasciatus, Forst.), called kohaperoa, or koekoia, and a very small and beautiful kind (the Cuculus nitems of the same author), called by the natives pipiwawaroa - which appear on the coast at Christmas - mark the period of the first potato-harvest. <i>ibid.</i> 2/193 40. <i>Eudynamys taitensis</i>. ... Kohaperoa of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. `Koheperoa', from a specimen. <i>Miss Stone</i>. Kohapiroa. <i>Polack? </i>Koekoia of the natives.<i> Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/122</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Koheperoa (<i>Eudynamis Taitensis</i>) is a long-tailed, brown-plumaged cuckoo, ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/223</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> an iron pot (or pan) for boiling; a goashore.

>  <b>PARKiNSON</b> <i>(1770,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1770 Parkinson Journal A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand.]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> The native oven,<i>hangi</i> or <i>kohua</i>, made in the well-known manner with heated stones, is situated either in the open air or in a house (te-kauta) constructed of logs at a small distance from each other. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/43</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> <i>(1960,At Home in NZ,44</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Sarah Selwyn in 1960 Drummond 44]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> [The Maori] should show me the country and in payment ... i should give him a <i>`kohoa'</i> (that is, a three-legged iron pot) <i>(1947,Pioneers Explore Otago,35</i>) [NOTES: c1853 Otago Reminisc. Nathan Chalmers in 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 35)]

>  ...Cooked in an iron pot or kohua. <i>(1862,From The Black Rocks,38</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>kohua, n. </i>Maori word, for (1) a Maori oven; (2) a boiler. There is a Maori <i>verb Kohu</i>, to cook or steam in a native oven (from a noun <i>Kohu</i>, steam, mist), and an <i>adj. Kohu</i>, concave. The word is used by the English in New Zealand, and is said to be the origin of <i>Goashore (qv.). </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,251</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,72</i>)

Card play ran high ... The stakes were usually thrown into a three-legged iron pot - the `kohua' of the old-timers ... <i>(Apr 11, 1921,Quick March,29</i>)

>  <i>kohua n</i> -s [Maori] : a Maori earth oven <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1254</i>)

>  <i>kohua n. </i>Maori oven or boile [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <i>kohua ... n.</i> cooking pot. See <i>go ashore. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The third method, boiling (or <i>kohua</i>), seems to have been used more rarely. Because the Maori did not make pottery, their only means of heating a liquid was by dropping a red-hot stone into a wooden bowl containing the liquid. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,1</i>)

### kohuhu
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pittosporum tenifoluim</i>, a small tree or shrub, related to tarata, often used for ornamental hedges; (black) matipo [ORIGIN: Maori, [1846 Raoul Choix de plantes de la Nouvelle-Z'elande Cohou-cohu [P. obcordatum] (And)]] (See [also kohukohu](../A#also-kohukohu),[karo](../K#karo))

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Koh|uhu, s. A shrub so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,165</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pittosporum <i>tenuifolium</i>, Banks et Sol. ... common as far south as Akaroa, ... Nat. names, `Mapauriki', <i>Cunn</i>.; `Kohuhu', <i>Col.</i>; `Karo' of Middle island, <i>(1853,Flora NZ,ii/21</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kohuhu, <i>Col[enso]. Pittosporum tenuifolium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ...<i> (e.g.)</i> the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ...; the Horopito <i>Drimys axillaris</i>; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>; the Kohuhu, <i> Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety, <i>Sophora tetraptera</i> var.:<i> grandiflora</i>; the Koromiko-taranga, <i>Veronica,</i> several species; the Mairehau, <i>Phebalium nudum;</i> the Toro, <i>Persoonia Toro</i>; the Pukapuka, <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>; the Northern Maire, <i>Santalum Cunninghamii;</i> the Tawari, <i>ixerba brexioides</i>; the Tipau, <i>Myrsine Urvillei</i> and <i>M. salicina</i>; the Tangeao, <i>Tetranthera calicaris</i>; the Ramarama, <i>Myrtus bullata</i>; the Ti, <i>Cordyline australis</i>; the Kahikomako, <i>Pennantia corymbosa</i>; the Pate, <i>Schefflera digitata;</i> the Horoeka, <i>Panax crassifolia:</i> and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> This species is largely used for the formation of ornamental hedges, and is than called the `Matipo.' This is a misomer, as the Maories apparently applied this name to a species of <i>Suttonia. P. Tenuifolium </i>was called by them the <i>Tawhiwhi</i> or <i>Kohuhu. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,199</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  [kohuhu See Matipo and Black-stemmed Matipo] <i>(Sep 19, 1975,Evening Post,2/8</i>)

>  <b>MORRE & iRWiN</b> <i>PiTTOSPORUM TENUiFOLiUM. </i>Kohuhu is another small tree that grows naturally in both North and South islands. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,66</i>)

>  KOHUHU/ (Black Matipo)/<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>/ Tree up to 10m. with black bark. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,8</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium </i>subsp. <i>colensoi</i> KOOHUUHUU ... Small tree, sometimes a shrub. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,104</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> As a timber, kohuhu carries the distinction of being almost twice as strong as English oak, but lacks durability in the ground, and this limited its usefulness. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,49</i>)

>  in exchange they were give [sic] a choice of native plants such as tarata, karamu, totara, kohuhu, broadleaf, mingimingi, putaputaweta, harakeke (flax) and cabbage tree. <i>(Dec 8, 2005,Wellingtonian,14</i>) [NOTES: Exact species of the plant referred to here is not known.]

### kohukohu
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Scleranthus biflorus</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Scleranthus <i>biflorus </i>... Nat. names, Northern island, `Kohu-Kohu', <i>R.Cunn.;</i> Middle island, Naereore[es-acute], <i>D'Urville. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 74</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kohu-kohu, <i>R.Cunn. Scleranthus biflorus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Kohukohu. Scleranthus biflorus, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/47</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Scleranthus biflorus Kohu kohu <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/126</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The material used among the Tuhow natives ... as a menstruating diaper is a variety of moss (generic term <i>rimurimu</i>) known as <i>kohukohu</i> or <i>angiangi.</i> it is probably <i>Hypnum clandestinus. </i> <i>(1905,TrNZi,37/90</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Lembophyllum clandestinum</i> (H.f. & W.) Lindb. Maori names: <i>Angiangi, kohukohu</i> ... Goldie listed `angiangi' as <i>Hypnum clandestinum,</i> a `fern', but <i>H. clandestinum</i> is a moss, now known as <i>Lembophyllum clandestinum</i>. [Williams identifies <i>angiangi</i> as <i>Usnea barbata</i>, a lichen] and it is probable that the word was applied generally to soft absorbent mosses and lichens. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Stellaria media</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kohu-kohu, <i>Lindsay. Stellaria media. Lyall. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, kohuhu

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pittosporum <i>tenuifolium</i>, Banks et Sol. ... common as far south as Akaroa, ... Nat. names, `Mapauriki', <i>Cunn</i>.; `Kohuhu', <i>Col</i>.; `Karo' of Middle island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 21</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Kohukohu [Maori name] .. Kohukohu [Settlers' name] .. Pittosporum obcordatum .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> Best specially mentions kohukohu <i>(Pittosporum tenuifolium)</i> as important for raising the `tapu' on sick people. <i>ibid.</i> 79 <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i> Sol. ex Gaertn. Maori name: <i>Kohukohu </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pittosporum obcordatum</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pittosporum <i>obcordatum,</i> Raoul. ... HAB. Middle island; ... Nat. name, `Cohou-Cohou', <i>Raoul. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 22</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Cohou-cohou, <i>Raoul. Pittosporum obcordatum </i> <i>ibid.</i> 765 Kohu-kohu, <i>Geolog. Surv. Pittosporum obcordatum. </i> <i>ibid.</i>765 Kohoukohou, Geolog. <i>Surv Pittosporum obcordatum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Pittosporum cornifolium.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Pittosporum cornifolium </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Pittosporum cornifolium </i> <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Pittosporum Kirkii.</i>

### kohurangi
 
 <i>n.</i> a flowering epiphyte found high on forest trees [Maori kohu mist + rangi sky]. =sky mist.

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The epiphytic shrubs include two very showy members - the SKY-MiST or KOHURANGi of the Maori (<i>Senecio kirkii</i>) which when flowering high upon a forest tree can be seen from afar. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,149,1961 ed.</i>)

[(1873) 113] The Rifleman is the smallest of our New-Zealand birds; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,112,{1888 2nd edn 1/113, 114} ed.</i>)

### kohutuhutu
 
 <i>n.</i> fuchsia (See [kotukutuku](../K#kotukutuku))

>  The woods used are totara burr, wairangi, kauri, lancewood, puriri and kohutuhutu. <i>(Apr 23, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> ... (the fruit of the Kohutuhutu, <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>) ... the juice of the Tupakihi (<i>Coriaria sarmentosa</i>), a berry similar in taste to that of the elder, whose leaves, branches and seed, are highly poisonous; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),111</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>korai, koutu utu, miro</i>, and <i>putuhutu,</i> are wild forest fruits, prized only by the elder natives. The <i>tu pakihi</i> (Coriaria sarmentosa), or elder-berry, is a very pleasing fruit. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/288</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kohutuhutu, <i>Col[enso]. Fuchsia excorticata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Kohutuhutu Fuchsia excorticata, Linn. f. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/41</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  The berry of the Kohutuhutu, or Kotukutuku (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>); the Kohoho, or Poroporo, (<i>Solanum aviculare</i>), which ... was sometimes planted <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>KiRK</b> KOHUTUHUTU - FUCHSiA.- <i>(Fuchsia excorticata.) </i> <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,22</i>)

>  [Native name] Kohutuhutu .. [Settlers' name] Fuchsia .. [Family] Onagraceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  Kohutuhutu (fuchsia) .. Fuchsia excorticata .. Timber very strong, durable, and hard; used for house blocks and fencing posts; is almost indestructible by fire; grain prettily marked <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Fuchsia excorticata (The Tree Fuchsia).</i>.. Maori name <i>Kotukutuku </i>or <i>Kohutuhutu</i>, and of the fruit, <i>Konini. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,294,1940 p. 311 ed.</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]hutuhutu</i>, an irregularly-growing tree (Fuchsia excorticata), tree-fuchsia: more commonly ko[o]tukutuku, but ko[o]hutuhutu adopted to avoid possible confusion with ko[o]tuku, white-heron. Very often called konini, but konini is the berry of kohutuhutu, edible if rather insipid. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

 <i>n.</i>

### koinga
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Fig. 7. - Smooth-hound, <i>Mustelus antarcticus.</i> ...This shark was known to the Maoris as `koinga.' <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/262</i>)

>  Fig. 7. - Smooth-hound, <i>Mustelus antarcticus.</i> ...This shark was known to the Maoris as `koinga.' <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/262</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> dogfish

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Squalus fernandinus</i> Molina. SPiNED DOGFiSH; OKEOKE or KOiNGA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,9,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SPiNED DOGFiSH (KOiNGA OR OKEOKE) <i>Koinga lebruni</i> (Vailliant) ...Spined Dogfish is so named from the single spine in front of each dorsal fin, and Dogfish probably because of its supposed carnivorous habits. There are several species of small sharks that are known as Dogfish. [<i>ibid.</i> 82]. The Maori name for Spined Dogfish is Koinga or Okeoke, and the fish is used by them as food, usually being hung outside and dried, when it will keep for long periods. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,80,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>koinga</i>: spine shark; grows to 2 ft. in length. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SOUTHERN DOGFiSH ... <i>Other names: Squalus acanthias</i>; spotted spiny dogfish, spotted dogfish, Kirk's dogfish; koinga (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,187</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SOUTHERN DOGFiSH [<i>Squalus acanthias</i>] ... Other names include spiny dogfish, spotted dogfish, and koinga (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,10</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>SPOTTED SPiNY DOGFiSH Squalus acanthias</i> (Spotted dog, southern dogfish, koinga) A common cosmopolitan species ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,64,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] koinga 123 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### koiro
 
 <i>n.</i> conger eel

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The <i>Koiro</i>, conger eel, (fam. anguilliformes,) attains a length of about five feet, it is much esteemed by the natives. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,625</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> One evening he marched up to our house and said: `My mouth waters for `koiro' (conger eel); come, the tide and moon are rising, collect thy gear.' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,5</i>)

>  LEPTOCEPHALUS CONGER (Linnaeus). <i>Conger-eel; Koiro</i>. Called `matamoe' by the Maoris of Arahura. Commonly taken by the trawlers around the North island, but rarer farther south. Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/119</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Leptocephalus conger</i> (Linnaeus). CONGER-EEL; KOiRO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,17,1971 ed.</i>)

>  [Chathams] Plainly ... i see old Kirapu gather his <i>kupeng' tchitok'</i> (pole net) ... to net <i>mararii </i> (kelp-fish) and <i>koiro</i> (conger eel) and i go with him. <i>(1928,Bishop Museum Memoirs,iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 378</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 378]

>  8. <i>Leptocephalus conger</i> Linnaeus. Conger Eel. Maori: Ko-iro, Ngoiro. <i>(1929,TrNZi,61/140</i>) [NOTES: Young]

>  <i>Conger-eel. Leptocephalus labiatus</i> (Castelnau). ...This is the koiri of the Maoris, common around Wellington ... in the common New Zealand conger the male is readily recognized by its greatly flattened head and small anus. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/229</i>)

>  <i>Leptocephalus conger</i> (Linnaeus). Conger eel. <i>Koiro. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/403</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... Others omitted are ... the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), ... and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> CONGER EEL (KOiRO) <i>Leptocephalus verreauxi</i> (Kaup) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,135</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> CONGER EEL ... <i>Other names: Conger verreauxi</i>; ngoiro, koiro (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,198</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> CONGER EEL [<i>Conger verreauxi</i>] ... The Maori names koiro and ngoiro are sometimes used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> trumpeter [This is surely an error: nf Williams, and Phillipps has proper assignment elsewhere; trumpeter Kohikohi.]

>  LATRiDOPSiS LiNEATA (Forster). <i>Trumpeter; Koiro. </i>Very common around Stewart island. ... Recorded from South island and southern coasts of North island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

 <i>n.</i> New Zealand marsh crake/rail; spotted rail

### koiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> human remains. [ORIGIN: Maori, also called koiwi tangata]

>  if identification is established, Mr Te Puni says it would then be up to the major stockholders - Te Papa, the Government and most importantly, iwis - to decide what becomes of the moko mokai and koiwi (skeletal remains). <i>(Mar 10, 2001,Dominion,21</i>)

>  Earlier this year beach walkers noticed koiwi at a Cape Palliser shoreline site following significant erosion. <i>(Dec 2011,Heritage Quarterly,2</i>)

>  At issue is whether or not koiwi (human remains) are buried in Waikanae wetlands, which would be destroyed during construction of a 360-metre section of the road... The Environment Court had not accepted the oral history of kaumatua (Maori elders) that koiwi existed in swamp [sic] because they were unable to locate their precise positions, he said. <i>(Sep 28, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### koka
 
 <i>n.</i> kokako

The matuku, or bittern, once common enough is not mentioned, nor is the koka or crow, nor any of the smaller birds. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a cape

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Method of Cooking.</i> Kokopu are cooked in small baskets called <i>tapora</i>, formed by plaiting leaves of mauri or kokaha (both Astelia), or some similar leaves. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,20</i>)

### kokahakaha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JENNiNGS</b> it is undulating red birch country - with open patches & valleys growing tussocks, snowgrass toetoe, kokahakaha, with numbers of flowers - swampy or rather - peaty, comparatively dry now but no doubt very wet in winter. <i>ibid.</i> 7 in front and just out of the bush was an open pakihi part of the same that extends onto Mt Peel - like a very large paddock covered with snowgrass and kokahakaha (Astelia) a plant with soft silvery flaxlike leaves growing about 2 ft high. <i>(1880,Diary of an Expedition to the Table Land,4</i>)

### kokako
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) New Zealand crow [=blue-wattled/orange-wattled crow; =blue gills; wattle bird;] (See [crow](../C#crow))

>  The kokako sounds incredible. it's not like a sparrow going tweet. it has overtones and many timbres in its call. <i>(Sep 26, 2001,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  Our first night is spent under a tent-fly about 1.5km as the kokako flies from Hunters Hut. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]kako</i>, two birds (Callaeas wilsoni - North island, and C. cinereous[sic] - South island), blue-wattled crow and orange-wattled crow, the colour of the wattles being the only difference; nearly if not quite extinct: this is the bird to which the echoic name bell-bird was first applied. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>)

>  <i>kokako</i>: orange or blue-wattled crow. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <i>kokako n </i>-s [Maori] <i>New Zeal</i> : WATTLE CROW <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1254</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Kakako .. kokako .. <i>Callaeas</i> ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> KOKAKO <i>Callaeas cinerea Other names</i>: Wattled Crow, Organ-bird, Gill-bird, Bluegill (N.i.). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,236</i>)

>  <i>kokao n. </i>New Zealand blue-wattled crow [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <i>kokako .. n.</i> N.Z. blue-wattled crow [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins)</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> We used to shoot our own meat, deer and kokako and pigeons ... <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,103</i>)

>  <i>kokako n. </i>bluish-grey NZ variety of crow with blue or orange wattles. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 274]]

>  Melody awoke us all at 5 o'clock - tuis, mokos, kokakos, or crow .. all piping forth in the beautiful sunshine. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The rare Kokako, or Organ-bird .. is, rather than the Korimako, the true Bellbird of the New Zealand forest. <i>(1938,Children of Tane,227</i>)

>  "There was something about the kokako that obviously struck a chord in me that night .. and i realised i wanted to join the team working to save it from extinction." <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 12</i>)

>  Native crows - the Maori's kokako - which were very plentiful a few years ago, are being driven further back. <i>(Aug 3, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  Mr Buckingham has sought the South island kokako for 23 years and found a kokako feather on Stewart island in 1986. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,12</i>)

>  in the seasons that followed, a second wave of threatened birds was released: kakapo (1982), tieke (1984-87) and kokako (1981-94) ... <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/23</i>)

>  The bird, called Tamanui (Maori for big boy) is the last known kokako from an isolated population in Taranaki and conservation scientists say keeping his genes in the kokako gene pool is hugely important. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kokako</i>, n. <i>Callaeas cinerea cinerea</i> and <i>C. cinerea wilsoni, New Zealand crow.: Callaeas Cinerea Cinerea,</i> a blue-grey native song-bird with conspicuous wattles, quite unrelated to the crow [Maori kokako[o1-macron]]; <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,129</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|okako; Name of a certain bird. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,166</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Kokako</i>; called, by some, the New-Zealand crow .. it has a strong black beak .. and a small brilliant light-blue flap hanging down on each side, from the ear. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),64</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The<i> kakako</i> [a2-acute] is a species of crow, not unlike its European counterpart. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/301</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> <i>29. Callaeas cinerea. Lath. </i>.. Kokako of the natives. - New Zealand crow. <i>Yate. Dieffenbach</i>. Kakako of the natives. <i>Polack. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/191</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/191]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> in the lower regions the forest was much enlivened by birds. i shot some parrots [fn Nestor meridionalis. Kaka of the natives.], pigeons [fn Columba spadicea, Lath. Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Tikke/ A kokoko/ land birds <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,52</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> .. the green Koukaato fluttering among [the pohutukawa's] blossoms, singing its lively and melodious notes ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,102,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We have also heard the kokako or New Zealand crow a beautiful glossy black bird ... <i>(Dec 7, 1846,Journal,4/147</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> By night had snared a hawk and twenty singing birds, chiefly kakako, [check spg] thrushes and robins. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped. to Kawatiri in Taylor Early Travs 245]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The natives have a saying that the huia stole the plumage of the kokako, the native crow, and gave its own tail instead. <i>(Mar 6, 1849,Journal,6/37</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BiRDS MET WiTH iN PELORUS ...New Zealand Crow - Kokako <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,65</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The first is the kakako (Callaeas cinerea), the New Zealand crow; generally a pair together ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,139</i>)

>  Callaeas cinerea. Kokako. New Zealand Crow. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 42. GLAUCOPiS WiLSONi. Bp. Blue-wattled Crow. Kokako. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,16</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> GLAUCOPiS WiLSONi. (BLUE-WATTLED CROW.) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Kokako <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,153,{1888 2nd edn 1/1} ed.</i>)

>  .. the once silent woods now resound with the blows of the felling-axe .. the Wattle-bird is no longer to be found in its old haunts; it seeks shelter amongst the higher parts of the bushy gullies .. the condition of the forest is changing under the effects of .. constantly-recurring bush fires .. The Kokako loving a moist temperature will probably soon entirely forsake its ancient places of resort. <i>(1874,TrNZi,6/146</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kokako (<i>Glaucopis Wilsoni</i>) is a crow, and is not uncommon in the Kaipara. it has blue wattles on the beak. its note is peculiar, being sometimes a low, hollow boom, and at others a shrill and somewhat bell-like tone. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> <i>(1882,Out in the Open,194</i>)

>  <i>Glaucopis cinerea</i>, Gml. Wattlebird or Crow, Kokako. - i first became acquainted with this bird on the flats of the Wilberforce river, west of the Rakaia gorge, on 20th April, 1856; it was abundant amongst the isolated beech trees .. <i>ibid.</i> 377 (T.H. Potts) 40. <i>Glaucopis Wilsoni,</i> Bp. Wattle-bird, Blue Gills, Kokako. <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,376,39 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  (Reischek) GLAUCOPiS. - Crow, two species (<i>Kokako</i>). <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/102</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kokako, n.</i> Maori name for <i>Blue-wattled Crow.</i> See under Crow and <i>Wattle-bird. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,251</i>)

### kokihi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [New Zealand spinach](../N#new-zealand-spinach))

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kokihi, <i>Col[enso]. Tetragonia trigyna. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Kokihi. Tetragonia expansa <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/42</i>) [NOTES: Kokihi. Tetragonia expansa]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>T[etragonia] expansa, Murr. ... Kokihi</i>. ... This has long been cultivated in Europe as an edible plant under the name of `New Zealand spinach'. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,192</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Another New Zealand native, New Zealand spinach, or kokihi, so impressed the ship's botanist, Sir Joseph Banks, that he took it back to England and grew it in his own garden. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?=native spinach: contrast New Zealand spinach <i>T. tetragonioides</i>

>  On the New Zealanders learning to write, they used ... the crimson juice of the berry of the Kokihi, (a species of <i>Tetragonia - T. trigyna,</i> -) ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/38</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>TETRAGONiA ... trygina </i>... Beach Spinach. KOKiHi. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,27</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Tetragonia trigyna</i> NATiVE SPiNACH ... Kookihi ...Rather succulent sprawling herb ... Stems often red-stained. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,146</i>)

### kokiri
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Parika scaber (Cantherines convexirostris)</i>, the leatherjacket, filefish, rough leatherjacket, cream-fish, triggerfish.

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> i observed in one of the canoes a very singular fish, which the natives call cokiddie, or the spear-fish. it was about the size of a perch, and shaped very like it, except the head, which was rather [23] oblong, like that of a pig; its skin was quite rough, and behind its head nature had armed it with a sharp bone, about two inches long, which it could extrude and draw in at pleasure. it was from this bone that the natives gave it the name of cokiddie, which signifies spear in their language, and therefore appropriate enough. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/22</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kok|idi, <i>s</i>. ... also a certain fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,166</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> The natives ... had taken several very fine Kokori (<i>Ballistes</i> sp. Linn.) ... its skin is of a dirty - olive colour, and rough, resembling shagreen. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excurs Nthn isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 52]

>  <b>MANiNG</b> <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,44</i>)

>  CANTHERiNES CONVEXiROSTRiS (Gunther[u-dieresis]). <i>Leather-jacket; Kokiri</i>[o-macron]. Large numbers are skinned and sold in Auckland as `cream-fish.' ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (less common, South island). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  Such a fish is the kokiri (<i>Cantherines convixirostris</i> Guenther) (leather jacket). <i>(1926,TrNZi,58/646</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Cantherines scaber</i> (Forster). LEATHER-JACKET; KOKiRi. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,56,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> LEATHER-JACKET (<i>Cantherines convixirostris</i>), Kokiri of the Maoris, is a curious rough-skinned fish with a small mouth and a trigger-like spine on the back which can be locked into a vertical position at will. This fish resembles brown suede leather except for the fins, which are bright yellow. it is a good food fish and is sold in the Auckland markets in the form of skinned fillets under the name of `cream-fish.' <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Various fish were caught but the most satisfactory was the leather jacket (<i>kokiri, Cantherines convixirostris</i>)[sic] which is the plague of line fishermen owing to their small mouths removing the bait. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,215</i>) [NOTES: also 240-241]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> ROUGH LEATHER JACKET, TRiGGER-FiSH or FiLE-FiSH (KOKiRi) <i>Parika scaber</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,374</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kokiri ... 6. Cantherines convexirostris, Leather-jacket</i>; a fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,130</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kokiri</i>: leather jacket (a kind of fish). <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> LEATHERJACKET (<i>Novodon[sic] convexirostris</i>), or kokiri of the Maoris, is a small rough-skinned fish with a tiny mouth ... it is a good food fish and is sold in the Auckland market in the form of skinned fillets under the name of `cream fish'. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/294</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> LEATHERJACKET [<i>Navodon[sic] scaber</i>] ... The names filefish and triggerfish are sometimes used, but more properly belong to a related group of tropical fishes. The Maori name kokiri is still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,24</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>LEATHERJACKET Parika scaber (Navodon scaber)</i> (Triggerfish, cream fish, kokiri) ... [314] They make excellent eating and are usually sold under the name of cream fish. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,313,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Leatherjacket [title]/ This cheeky little thick-skinned fish, also known as creamfish, triggerfish or <i>kokiri</i> in Maori, is widespread around the rocky coast of New Zealand. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,76</i>)

>  [species name] leatherjacket .. [scientific name] <i>Navodon scaber</i> .. [other name(s)] cream fish, filefish, kokiri, triggerfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a throwing-stick

>  <b>MANiNG</b> They hold in their hands some reeds to represent darts or <i>kokiri. </i> <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,48</i>)

### kokiri centre
 
 <i>n.</i> conference centre

>  The dining hall, Te Poho o Hine i Tuhia o Terangi, has been built; a kokiri centre has been established for meetings and training courses and the tennis courts upgraded. <i>(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,11</i>)

>  The proposal to establish a kokiri basic skills centre in Porirua as an alternative way of learning for young Maoris and islanders has been virtually abandoned ... <i>(Jul 14, 1982,Evening Post,10</i>)

### kokiri unit
 
 <i>n.</i> an enterprise [ORIGIN: Maori, also kokiri elliptical]

>  The idea of Maori Affairs Department community services staff operating as Kokiri units in the Wellington area as a trial run... Kokiri (to advance) units will consist of department staff who will provide a full-time support service to community action programmes. <i>(Feb 3, 1981,Evening Post,16/1-2</i>)

>  PiPiTEA PA AS A KOKiRi BASE THE MAORi Affairs Department is keen to see the Ngati Poneke Maori Association offer Pipitea Pa as a base for the Wellington kokiri unit. ...a new pilot scheme which replaces the old district office administration with kokiri (advancement) units staffed in partnership with community volunteers. <i>(Mar 16, 1981,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  <i>kokiri n. </i>term applied to Maori community administration groups (<i>Kokiri Units</i). [M, = to advance] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  "So up at this kokiri," says Waho, "we're going to teach the kids commercial sewing and cooking skills." <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

### koko
 
 <i>n.</i> tui

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... they ornament their canoes with the same [feathers] the natives call the bird <i>koko</i> or tui ... <i>(Apr 19, 1839,Journal,2/109</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The lone cry of the <i>ko ko</i>, (a species of goatsucker) echoed plaintively from amongst the dense copse-like underwood, ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/6</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> . ...koko, the mocking or Parson Bird. <i>(1848,Leaf from the Natural History of NZ, A,8</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The lone cry of the <i>koko</i> (a species of goatsucker) echoed plaintively from among the dense, copse-like underwood. <i>(1850,Savage Life,2/6</i>)

>  The sooner these apt pioneers of civilization [ie. the shepherd and the herdsman] arrive to `disturb the ancient solitary reign' of the wild pig, and the koko, the sooner will this fine district be ready for whole villages of Englishmen. <i>(Jan 25, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>) [NOTES: (Planipes)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> PROSTHEMADRA NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (TUi OR PARSON-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Tui and Koko; the young bird distinguished as Pi-tui or Pikari. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,87,{1888 2nd edn 1/94} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Koko - the tui (also spelt kokoo to show the accent is on long second vowel). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]

>  <i>Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae,</i> Gml. Parson-bird, Poe-bird, Tui, Koko. - Often builds under the shelter of Rubus twines, which many of us have learnt to fear or respect as bush-lawyer ... More than once i have found it near the top of a matipo (<i>Myrsine</i>) ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,276,11 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  ... `koko' is the name given to the bird tui, the parson-bird ..., by the Moriori, and also used by the Maori in the south of New Zealand. <i>(1897,TrNZi,29/165 fn</i>)

>  Birds seen occasionally [on Three Kings were] kingfishers - kotaretare <i>(Halcyon sanctus)</i>; moreporks - koko <i>(Ninox novae-zealandiae)</i>; fantails - tireeraka <i>(Rhipidura flabellifera); ..</i> <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/154</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser)]

>  <i>koko n</i> -s [Maori] : TUi <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1254</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fishing-net

>  <i>koko n.</i> a fishing net supported between two poles. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a digging stick, ko (See [ko](../K#ko))

>  <b>ELDER</b> They call the tool koko. <i>(1819,Marsden's Lieutenants,165</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

### kokomako
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [korimako](../K#korimako))

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNiS MELANURA. (BELL-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kokomuka. <i>Veronica</i> sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

Of M'Quarrie cabbage there is abundance, and of fine growth, some of the leaves measuring two feet in diameter.Patches clear of scrub are clothed with Lutaki tussock. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/176</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Outlying islands)]

>  [The Snares are] almost entirely covered with scrub and trees of stunted growth, the Tupari, Akeake, and Kokomuka. Of M'Quarrie cabbage there is abundance, and of fine growth, some of the leaves measuring two feet in diameter.Patches clear of scrub are clothed with Lutaki tussock. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/176</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Outlying islands)]

>  The island [sc. Snares] is mostly covered with bush, the akeake (<i>Olearia</i> sp.) and kokomuka (<i>Veronica elliptica</i>) being the commonest trees. <i>(1889,TrNZi,21/380</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Hebe elliptica</i> KOOKOOMUKA ... Bushy shrub ... <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,68</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Veronica parviflora</i>, Vahl

### kokopara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PRENTiCE</b> ...the `kokopura', a sort of trout. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,33</i>) [NOTES: (Otago)]

>  (Thomson, Otago) <i>Parapercis colias</i> Forster. The stomachs of the blue-cod examined ... contained ...: Red-cod, sea-perch (<i>Helicolenus</i>), kokopuru (<i>Tripterygion</i>) ... <i>(1913,TrNZi,45/229</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Fifty years ago the Waimate Creek was full of native trout, or Kokopuru; ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,241,1954 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> Not to mention the whitebait called kokopara, that somehow transmogrified to be the cockabully... <i>(1989,Homeplaces,10</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Koukoupara - is a fish like the kokopu, but differently marked, and following the example of one of his informants, the translator [sc Beattie] has translated this as `mountain trout'. Koukoupara is the spelling adopted in the lists, but the [Maori] compilers have varied this with kopara, koupara, and kokaupara. Never once do they use the southern form of kokopara, which the southerners pronounced kokobala, which the early settlers soon turned into `cockabully'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>)

### kokopere
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHAND</b> inihina - Hinahina or Mahoe, in Maori - was considered the best wood for the rubber [in firemaking]; but Karamu, Karaka, Ake, Rautini, and Kokopere (Maori, Kawakawa) were used as the <i>Kahunaki</i>, or grooved piece of wood. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,8</i>)

### kokopu
 
 <i>n.</i> greenstone

>  According to stories surrounding kokopu if the stone is worn for the wrong reasons it turns brown. <i>(Aug 20, 2003,Capital Times,11</i>)

>  She visited West Coast carvers who told her about the different types of greenstone and their significance. Kokopu greenstone is the most treasured. <i>(Aug 20, 2003,Capital Times,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> cockabully (See [also  bully](../A#also--bully),[trout (mountain](../T#trout-(mountain),[native); para](../N#native);-para))

>  A biologist.. identified the fish as juvenile banded kokopu, well known for the ability to crawl up steep slopes... Banded kokopu (Galaxias fasciatus) are found only in New Zealand and are related to the inanga or whitebait. <i>(Jan 29, 2001,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> Whitebait in pristine condition are beautiful... They are the fry of several species of Galaxiads, maculatus, brevipinnis, argenteus, postvectis, and fasciatus (or inaka, koaro, and three kinds of kokopu). <i>(1989,Homeplaces,104</i>)

>  Conservation Department staff are searching for New Zealand's most elusive and endangered freshwater fish - the short-jawed kokopu - by shooting electric currents into in [sic] bush streams. ...The couple were searching for the short-jawed kokopu, scientifically known as galaxias postvectis - often called the native trout - whose young is one of the whitebait species. <i>(Feb 12, 2000,Dominion,7</i>)

>  Kokopu species and koaro were widespread in the [Wellington] region, but the annual whitebait catch, silting up of waterways, and culverting streams interrupted their life cycles... "Banded kokopu are remarkably aggressive colonisers, they can climb steep damp places, but they sure as hell can't fly."... Kokopu had been found in stormwater drains, toby holes, and hidden under leaves on moist ground. The fish could breathe through damp skin, because they lacked scales. <i>(May 7, 2001,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  The kokopu of the Maoris is the well known fish found in streams and from its appearance often called the New Zealand trout. <i>(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  The kokopu is a fat sluggish fish, found lurking under stones and rotten logs in all the streams in the colony, however small, where not running over a clear or stony bottom. <i>(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kok|opu, s. Name of a certain fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,166</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Near Taupo in a lake] ... it was surprising what a number of [177] kokopu we picked up This fish attains a size of nearly a foot and of about 1/4 of a lb. weight it is filled with roe. it is of a dark yellow color [sic] <i>(Nov 19, 1845,Journal,3/176</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Taupo] is a place of great resort for the kokopu which here attains its largest size, about 10 inches. <i>(Apr 26, 1847,Journal,V/47</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> Eels and kokopu are found in the river.... Hinau berries are most plentiful. The oven is being opened; it contains a large quantity of harore, or wild mushrooms. ... Eels and kokopu are found in the river. <i>(May 1850,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1850 May Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 52]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... kokopu, which [in the Waikato] are a fine delicate eating fish. <i>(1850,Journal,6/501</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Hoho, <i>kokopu</i>, a large fresh-water fish, having a very great head and mouth; it is about two feet long, and nearly one thick; it makes a loud noise, which can be heard at some distance. it is scaleless, and resembles an eel. i have not seen it. <i>ibid.</i> 499 ... the <i>kokopu</i> is another fresh-water fish, nearly ten inches long ... <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,414</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Silver eels attaining a goodly size are found in every stream; a sort of grayling is taken in a few of the clearest rivers; and the spotted Kokopu, called trout, ... abounds in every brook. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The principal ones are the <i>tuna</i>, eel, the <i>pipiharau</i>, lamprey, the <i>kokopu,</i> and <i>inanga;... </i> <i>(1870,Te ika a Maui,498</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> There are two sorts in the lake ....; for the larger kind, the <i>kokapu'</i>, rarely attain the length of five inches, and the smaller, named <i>inanga</i> 2, much resemble whitebait in appearance and flavour. [ed fn] 1. Native or mountain trout (Galaxias fasciatus) 2. <i>Galaxias attenatus</i>, the adult fish of whitebait. <i>(1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts,2nd ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Meade Ride Through Disturbed Districts (2nd ed) in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 476]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 94. KOKOPU. ... This is the general Maori name for several very common fishes in the New Zealand streams and lakes [of <i>Galaxias</i> spp.] <i>ibid.</i> 129 it may be useful to give a list of the different fishes the Maoris distinguish in the Taupo district, ... Kokopu. - Common in all rivers and the lake. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,127</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 94. GALAXiAS FASCiATUS. Gray. Kokopu. <i>(1872,Fishes,59</i>)

>  <b>Mrs GRACE</b> He brought us a basket of potatoes and a string of `Kokopu,' a small, fresh-water fish, very delicious, and which is found in the Lake and rivers of Taupo. <i>(Mar 1, 1878,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1878 Mar 1 Mrs Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 295]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Kokopu - throughout these lists the translator [sc Beattie] has translated this as `native trout'. A good-sized one would weigh 1lb. The translator has eaten one and found the flesh sweet, but it was full of bones. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> KOKOPU (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>). ... `Kokopu,' Dr. Hector says, "is the general Maori name for several very common fishes in the New Zealand streams and lakes, belonging to the family of <i>Galaxidae</i>. ... The kokopu of the stream is caught by them with bobs; but is not much cared for. Not more, as a rule, than one or two fish will be found in a pool; half a dozen at the most. it is also called rawaru and para. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,138</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> KOKOPU (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>). ... `Kokopu,' Dr. Hector says, "is the general Maori name for several very common fishes in the New Zealand streams and lakes, belonging to the family of <i>Galaxidae</i>. ... The kokopu of the stream is caught by them with bobs; but is not much cared for. Not more, as a rule, than one or two fish will be found in a pool; half a dozen at the most. it is also called rawaru and para. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,138</i>)

>  The following is a list of the fishes which are chiefly met with in the market: hapuku, kahawai, red-snapper, snapper, tarakihi, moki, trumpeter, frostfish ... gurnard, mullet, sea-mullet, ... flounder or patiki, ... grayling, smelt, kokopu, sardine, sprat, eel (tuna), black-eel, conger-eel [168], and silver-eel. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  Several varieties of smelt (<i>Retropinna</i>) called by the Natives `inanga', ...; bullheads of different sorts, and a slimy fish the `kokopu' (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) the young of which are supposed to be the New Zealand `whitebait' are widely distributed ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,431</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kokopu, n.</i> Maori name for a New Zealand fish; any species of <i>Galaxias,</i> especially <i>G. fasciatus</i>; corrupted into <i>Cock-a-bully </i> (q.v.). See <i>Mountain Trout</i>. [1820, 1886 Sherrin] <i>(1898,Austral English,251</i>)

>  <i>Ko&#769;.ko-pu</i> (k&omacr.&#769;.k&omacr.-pu; <i>colloq.</i> k&obreve.k&#769;.o.-pu&#769;.), n. [Maori.] (Zoo.l.) Any fish of the genus <i>Galaxias</i>; - called also <i>cockabully. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The bully, or bull-head, and the kokopu, or native trout, live entirely in fresh water. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,74</i>)

>  The kokopu proper is a fat, sluggish fish found lurking under stones and rotten logs in all streams in the colony ... <i>(1903,TrNZi,35/318</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The <i>kokopu</i>, a small freshwater fish, was, and is still, taken in the streams of the interior, but the <i>inanga </i>is only found in the lower parts of the rivers, ever at Ruatahuna. <i>(1925,Tuhoe: The Children of the Mist,11</i>)

>  The name kokopu on the west coast of the North island is applied to <i>Galaxias fasciatus,</i> which on the east coast is called para. At Lake Taupo kokopu ia applied to <i>Galaxias brevipinnis. </i> <i>(1926,TrNZi,58/640</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <i>Galaxias fasciatus </i>Gray. KOKOPU or PARA... Galaxias brevipinnis Guenther. GUDGEON; TAiWHARU or KOKOPU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,13,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>: [European Name] Native trout or mountain trout. [Maori Name] Kokopu. ... <i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i>: [European Name] `Gudgeon' [Maori Name] Taiwharu or kokopu. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>BUiCK</b> it has been reported that [moas] caught the small fresh-water fish - the kokopu, the inanga, the kakawai, and the karohi (swamp spotties). <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,236</i>)

>  <i>Galaxias fasciatus</i> Gray. <i>Kokopu.</i> ... A large specimen 15 ins. long was caught at Waitati Bay and kept in seawater for several days. Common in creeks running into the harbour. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/402</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>PHBiLLiPPS</b> KOKOPU OR PARA <i>Galaxias fasciatus </i>Gray ... This is the `trout' of the first New Zealand settelers in the early gold rushes. it is mentioned in several works which deal with those days and, with the ever present eel, it furnished food supply to many thousands of the first cosmopolitan throngs on out-back prospecting claims when food... <i>ibid..</i> 21 GUDGEON <i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i> Gunther ... This is the taiwharu of North Auckland and the kokopu of the Arawa tribe who also call the young (together with the young of Retropinna retropinna) inanga in Lakes Rotorua, Rotoiti and Taupo. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,15</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]kopu</i>, a fresh-water fish (Galaxias fasciatius), a fat, sluggish fish, formerly common in open drains and caught by children with cupped hands; name corrupted by them to cock-a-bully and abbreviated <i>bully</i>. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> KOKOPU or NATiVE TROUT (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>). A sluggish fish found under stones, banks ... it is a dull brownish fish variously marked, ... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,63</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> ... to wade up the clear gully stream in pursuit of the small koura, or crayfish, and any native trout or kokopu we could spear, or guddle. <i>ibid.</i> 186 We had been in the habit of getting water for the billy tea at a little waterfall above a mountain pool, in which there swam a lordly and outsize kokopu, a little more than a foot long. <i>(1952,Faring South,79</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> KOKOPU <i>Galaxias fasciatus</i> Gray. it is often called Mountain [ibid. 115] Trout but it is also found practically at sea level, just above high- tide mark, as i have often caught specimens in small creeks near the sea at Portobello, in Otago Harbour. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,114,(2nd edn) ed.</i>)

>  <i>kokopu</i> The Maori name of a small freshwater fish. See <i>cock-a-bully. </i>-- <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  <i>kokopu n</i> -s [Maori] : any of various New Zealand fishes of the genus <i>Galaxias</i> that resemble the trout - compare GALAXiiDAE <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1254</i>)

>  <i>kokopu n</i> -s [Maori] : any of various New Zealand fishes of the genus <i>Galaxias</i> that resemble the trout - compare GALAXiiDAE <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1254</i>)

>  <b>HiNTZ</b> The kokopu and the smelt in combination could mean much for Taupo. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,66</i>)

>  <i>kokopu ... n.</i> N.Z. fish, kind of trout, called also cock-a-bully. [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> There was an accommodating pool here well stocked with <i>kokopu</i>. ... These little native trout soon became tame ... <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,44</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> There was an accommodating pool here well stocked with <i>kokopu</i>. ... These little native trout soon became tame ... <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,44</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Kokopu (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) was good but bony eating - the Maori knew three varieties, rauinahehe, reretawa and para. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,13</i>)

>  <i>kokopu n.</i> = COCKABULLY. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> (Cuv. & Val). BULLY; TOiTOi or KOKOPU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,49,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The other principal species formerly taken were the inanga and kokopu (<i>Galaxias</i> and <i>Retropinna</i>), upokororo (<i>Prototractes</i>), and panoko (<i>Cheimarrichthys</i>); while the lamprey (piharau and korokoro) and fresh water crayfish (koura) were taken in considerable numbers in some districts. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,71</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 41. <i>Clinus littoreus</i>, ... (Blennius littoreus, G. Forster, ...) Named `Kogop' by the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. ... 45. <i>Tripterygion varium.</i> ... (Blennius varius(?), G. Forster ...) ... [212] Named `Kekogop' by the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/211</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/211-212]

>  <i>Tripterygion tripenne</i> Forster. The kokopu spawns in October and November. The young are very common in the [Otago] harbour during the summer months ... <i>(1913,TrNZi,45/230</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family BLENNiiDAE (cockabullies). is `cockabully' a transliteration and transference of `kokopu', the Maori word for a different fish, the galaxiid; or does `kokopu' come from `cockabully'? in any case, cockabully, kokopu and bully are used for quite different fishes. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,229</i>)

>  All.. seem to be affected with a disease indigenous to a fish which inhabits this lake, and [is] called by the natives the kokopu. <i>(Jan 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

>  Also the well-known and delicious whitebait, the inanga - which i believe to be the larval stage of the common eel - cannot ascend to the lakes; but the name "inanga" is given locally to a tiny fish that is said to be the young of another fish, the "kokopu"... it enjoys a name, <i>Galaxias attenuatus<i\>. <i>(Mar 20, 1918,Blackwood's Magazine,368</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> ?sea -trout; ?blennies ?young of cod.

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> ... with the following results: 581 eels (from 1ft. to 4ft. in length); 8 dozen flounders, of various sizes, large numbers of aua, or kataka (mullet); about 70lb. or 70lb. weight of pilchard, or mohimohi; a few snapper and kahawai; hundreds of red cod and rarii; and a number of kokopu or rawaru. <i>(1908,Fishing and Sea-Foods,64</i>)

>  ... large numbers of aua or kataha (<i>Agonostoma forsteri</i>); about 60 lb. ... weight of pilchard or mohimohi (<i>Clupea sagax</i>), two varieties; ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>), and what i believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (<i>Percis colias</i>). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long. <i>(1903,TrNZi,35/319</i>) [NOTES: Mair Fish in Piako River]

### kokopuru
 
 <i>n.</i> native trout (See [kokopara](../K#kokopara))

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Fifty years ago the Waimate Creek was full of native trout, or Kokopuru; ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,249,1954 ed.</i>)

### kokoreke
 
 <i>n.</i> quail (New Zealand), native quail (See [koreke (native quail)](../K#koreke-(native-quail)))

>  The native quail, or koreke (<i>Coturnix novaezealandiae</i>), became extinct some time after 1860 because of hunting by Maori and Europeans and reduction of tussock habitat by burning and grazing. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/47</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> 57. COTURNiX NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. Quoy. et Gaim. Native Quail. Koreke. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,22</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> COTURNiX NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (NEW-ZEALAND QUAiL.) ... Native names.- Koreke \{and Kokoreke\}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,161,{1888 1/225} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The most noticeable absentee from the birds recorded [in the Maori food lists c1880] is the koreke or quail, once extremely plentiful. ... The matuku, or bittern, once common enough is not mentioned, nor is the koka or crow, nor any of the smaller birds. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Koreke (<i>Coturnix N.Z</i>.), the native quail, was once very plentiful ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/223</i>)

>  55. <i>Coturnix novae-zealandiae</i>, Quoy. et Gaim. Quail, Koreke. - At this early date of the settlement of the country it is painful to have to write of a species as a thing of the [484] past ... it seems a strange statement ... that probably no youngster has ever found a nest of native quail. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,483</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> ... the quail (<i>koreke</i>: Coturnix N.Z.), the rail (<i>mohoperu</i> and <i>moeriki</i>: Rallus sp.) and many other land-birds were cooked or potted down. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,105</i>)

>  Mr. Mieville, in his reminiscences already quoted, says, `Quail (or koreko) shooting was delightful sport. They were round my station in thousands.' <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/275</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  <i>koreke</i>, a bird (Coturnix novae zealandiae), Maori quail, now extinct, its place being taken by the imported Caliornia and Tasmania quail, now very plentiful. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  The N.Z. Quail or <i>koreke, Coturnix novae-zelandiae</i>; it became extinct in 1870. <i>(1955,Cook's Journals,1/276 [fn.]</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Beaglehole (ed) Cook Journals 1/276-277 [fn]]

>  <i>124. Coturnix novaezelandiae novaezelandiae</i> Quoy and Gaimard, 1830, <i>New Zealand Quail</i> ... extinct since about 1870.(Koreke) <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,40,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> New Zealand once possessed a native quail, the <i>koreke</i>, which became extinct after the large-scale burning of its grassland habitat. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,92</i>)

### kokorimako
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>YATE</b> The <i>Kokorimako</i> is about the size of the sparrow, with a small, oblong, dark eye; ... has a remarkably shrill quick cry, <i>Te te te te</i>, which it keeps up for some minutes ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),55</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Formerly when they received their name from the parent a kokorimako was eaten in the feast ... that the child might be an orator and renowned for the sweetness of his voice, as the kokorimako is for his singing, ... <i>(Jun 16, 1849,Journal,6/112</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> it is the Kokoromaka of the natives, and the Bell-bird of the settlers. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/24</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Amongst [dawn songsters] the kakorimaka [sic] (Anthornis melanuris) is the most conspicuous. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,139</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNiS MELANURA. (BELL-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

### kokota
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kok|ota, <i>s</i>. A cockle so called; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,166</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Kokota</i>: A white bivalve. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,193</i>)

>  [species name] pipi .. [scientific name] <i>Paphies australis</i> .. [other name(s)] slovata, kokota <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### kokote
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There were several kinds of shellfish, including kutai, kokote, and huangi; of fish, tamure (snapper), takeke (guardfish), arara (travalli). <i>(Jun 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

### kokowai
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> The countenances were entirely bedaubed with a solution of the red <i>kokowai</i> earth. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/79</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kok|o ai, s. Red ochre; red paint for the skin; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,166</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Stones] used by the natives to grind their kokowai or red ochre upon. <i>(Nov 8, 1839,Journal,2/164</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> in the bored lobes of the ears, some white down from the breast of sea-fowl was hung pendent, and the face rubricated with a profuse quantity of red ochre (kokowai.) <i>A garland of white and pink flowers of the petalous kaikatoa</i>[o-acute], encircled the head. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/65</i>)

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> The natives are very fond of daubing their heads with a sort of red paint which they call `cocoi'; i saw a large manufactory of it on the banks of the Waikato. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,61,1952 ed.</i>)

>  Mr Brown ... said it was nothing being merely a party going to Motiti to fetch Kokowai (Red ochre). <i>(Dec 23, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,390,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> One of them, a very suspicious-looking fellow, painted over and over with kokowai, never let the tomahawk out of his hands, ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/61</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> On the morning of my landing i visited Pipitea pah: several canoes were drawn up along the beach; some of them beautifully carved at the head and stem, and all coloured with <i>kokowai,</i> or red ochre. <i>ibid.</i> 1/265 The whole of the carved work ... are coloured red with <i>kokowai</i>, an ochre, found principally on the sides of the volcano of Taranaki. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/235</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> The low eaved huts with their deep gables smeared with kokowai (red ochre), ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,5</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The whole of this carved wah was painted red, with an ochre, called "kokowai.' <i>(1866,Polynesia,154</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Rubbing <i>kokoai</i>, or ochre, over my face, and sticking feathers in my hair, he [Rauparaka] declared that i should be his <i>Pakeha-Maori</i> for ever. <i>(1866,Chatham islands,16</i>)

>  <i>Kokowai</i> is a kind of pigment, burnt, dried, and mixed with shark-liver oil. <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/75</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Maori name for Red Ochre, an oxide of iron deposited in certain rivers, used by the Maoris for painting. it was usually mixed with shark oil, but for very fine work with oil from the berries of the <i>titoki</i>(q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,251</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> `His head' with the hair neatly arranged and copiously ornamented with feathers, reclined against a carved post, which was painted with kokowai, or red ochre.' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,i/124</i>)

>  `Kokowai is a kind of pigment, burnt, dried, and mixed with shark-liver oil.' <i>(1878,TrNZi,9/75</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> That to him [sc the Maori] the monstrosities which decorated his whare runanga (meeting-house) posts ... and the rafters painted with kokowai (iron-ore rust), for a ground colour in red, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,7</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> McKillop had [Rauparaha] by the throat ... and ... his naked body was as slippery as an eel's, coated with a mixture of <i>kokowai</i>, or red ochre and shark-oil. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/119</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [Te Heuheu] said he was sending his women and children to Wellington with mats and <i>kokowai</i> (red ochre, mixed with oil) for Te Rauparaha and Ngatata. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,23</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]ko[o]wai</i>, red-ochre; earth from which it has procured by burning; a pigment for Maori carving and a rouge for Maori faces. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>kokowai</i> : red ochre. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <i>kokowai n</i> -s [Maori] 1 : red ocher used in New Zealand as a pigment esp. on woodwork 2 : the earth from which kokowai is obtained <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1254</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kokowai</i> : red ochre, earth from which red ochre is obtained by burning. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <i>kokowai n</i> red ochre pigment used by Maoris for painting [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <i>kokowai n</i> . red ochre, pigment used by Maoris for painting [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <i>kokowai n. NZ </i> burnt red clay, red ochre. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: 1986 NZPOD 416 [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>BARROW</b> <i>(1963,Life & Work of Maori Carver,28</i>)
