### koloss
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Koloss one day with a bomb under him and the next day he returns to his unit as good as ever. <i>(May 11, 1942,NZEF Times,i/45 : 6</i>)

### komako
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNiS MELANURA. (BELL-BiRD.) ...<i>Native names</i>. Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

### komate
 
 <i>n.</i> Made familiar to Eng. users from the opening of the well-known haka.

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Komate</i>. A dead or a wounded soldier: a sick horse: New Zealand soldiers': 1915-18. Ex Maori <i>ka mate</i> dead. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,464</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Terms originated in the first Great War [include] ... <i>komate</i>, a dead or wounded soldier or horse (from the Maori <i>ka mate</i>). <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> [ca1950's (R.Mason) dead; done for, finished; of no further use]

>  <b>MiTCHELL & STRONG</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ..<i>komaty,</i> dead, from Maori <i>ka mate</i>; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <i>komate adj. </i>dead [Corr. of Maori <i>ka mate</i>]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>komaty</i> dead or wounded soldier, WWi, later referring to a horse; from Maori `Ka mate', `First was death!, the opening lines of Te Rauparaha's famous haka celebrating deliverance from his enemies, now chanted by the All Blacks before doing test battle, by the national rugby league team if they have won a test battle. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,66</i>)

### komiromiro
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> MYiOMOiRA TOiTOi. (PiED TiT \{NORTH iSLAND TOMTiT\}.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Miromiro, Komiromiro, \{Pimiromiro,\} Ngirungiru\{, Pingirungiru, and Pipitori\}. ... [40] its note in the early morning is like the Maori syllables <i>ngi-i-ru, ngiru-ngiru</i>, from which it derives its name, the first syllable being rather prolonged. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,124,{1888 2nd edn 1/39-40} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> MYiOMOiRA MACROCEPHALA. (YELLOW-BREASTED TiT \{SOUTH-iSLAND TOMTiT\}.) <i>Native names.</i> Miromiro, Komiromiro, \{Pimiromiro,\} Ngirungiru\{, Pingirungiru, and Pipitori\}. ... [40] its note in the early morning is like the Maori syllables <i>ngi-i-ru, ngiru-ngiru</i>, from which it derives its name, the first syllable being rather prolonged. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,126,{1888 2nd edn 1/42} ed.</i>)

### komiti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The judge's box is an empty dray and the "komiti" meet on a dray also. <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,42</i>)

>  ... on one occasion [in the 1850s] during the sittings of the General Assembly, the outside of the house was beseiged by crowds of Natives, listening and wondering what the white people inside could possible be doing, and whether they were holding a secret meeting or runanga to kill the Maories! He [Mr. Rogan\} remarks that at this time the Maories first adopted the term `runanga,' the term `komiti' having previously been used by them in respect to deliberative meetings. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,3</i>)

### komiti marae
 
 <i>n.</i> a marae council [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HUTT</b> The Maori Councils Act 1900 was intended to provide twenty-six proclaimed districts with an elected "komiti marae". The councils had power over liquor infractions, often building upon the pre-existing local committees of elders which in many instances had already introduced regulation of alcolhol onto their marae. <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,52</i>) [NOTES: See Lange "<i>The Revival of a Dying Race</i>", 1972 MA Univ. Auckland. p., 355]

### komutumutu
 
 <i>n.</i> trevalli

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... though i am not so sure about the rawaru (blue cod). Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter) <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

### Kon-Tiki
 
 <i>n.</i> a fishing device [[Check NZ.]]

>  ... the central Bay of Plenty `mystery ship' ... could very well be a very large `Kon-Tiki' fishing device. ... [Footnote:] A `kon-tiki' is a small raft used by fishermen to drift their lines out well beyond the breakers using wind assistance. They are controlled by a line from the shore. <i>(Jan 23, 1963,Evening Post,14</i>) [NOTES: [Check NZ.]]

>  Two children suffered injuries at Waihi Beach on Saturday morning when a speeding bikie swept up a Kon Tiki line which dragged them into the tide. The children both suffered burns from the nylon line. The motor-cyclist, reported to be a member of a gang, was riding illegally ... <i>(Oct 25, 1972,Press, The,2/4</i>)

### konae
 
 <i>n.</i> a basket

>  <b>ZiMMERMAN</b> She showed me, too, how to make the little baskets called<i> konae</i> that are used to hold the food that is put into the earth ovens to be cooked by the heat of hot rocks, ... <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,65</i>)

### konaki
 
 <i>n.</i> a farmer's or timberman's sledge [(North and Chatham Islands) ca1900 A.B. Cockrane's mother (S.W. Auckland) `a sledge in the early days' ca1950 W.H. Mabbett (Dannevirke) a sledge with runners on front and wheels at back. ca1950 H.S. Gajadhar <i>koneke,</i> a sledge in a legal document in State Advances Corporation.] (See [cf. catamaran](../C#catamaran))

>  An old car chassis with a couple of wheels at one end, something like a konaki, will do a more elaborate turn. <i>(Dec 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,10</i>)

>  <b>HARSANT</b> A koneke, a sledge with upturned front runners, was prepared. <i>(1979,They Called Me Te Maari,17</i>)

>  Other than in springtime, when the grass was lush, the cows dropped large firm pats which we shovelled over the railings on to a heap to be carted away in the winter in a konaki (a horse drawn sledge with two wheels at the back and a runner in front) and spread out over the paddocks. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/11</i>)

>  The sledgers would haul them [sc. pile of firewood] on their konekes down a short cut on the mountain side. <i>(Dec 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,1</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kon|ake, s. A slip with the foot. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,167</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Koneke, n. sledge. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,59</i>)

>  <b>HALE</b> i also got some insight into up-country life [in King Country] ... noting ... the primitive Maori `koneka' or sledge. <i>(1914,Woman in the Antipodes, A,86</i>)

>  `konaki' <i>(Apr 1942,NZ Journal of Agriculture,66(4)/270</i>)

>  <b>GiLBERD</b> in Maori country a konaki is a sledge. South of Auckland a sledge is a sledge and a konaki is a `catamaran' - on sledge runner in front and two wheels midships. <i>(May 14, 1951,Letter</i>) [NOTES: R.Gilberd (Northland)]

>  KONAKi A sledge, with or without wheels, for extracting split forest produce. Originally the term referred to a rough wheelless sledge made from the fork of a tree. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <i>(1957,NZ Timber Journal,61/2</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The trunks were lashed to a konaki, which Mother discovered was the Maori word for a horse-drawn wooden sled ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,15,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> The nearest i'd ever come to handling a team was feeding out the hay with Uncle Willy's two old nags in the konaki. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,38</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> A little later, i saw Jacob come home, trudging slowly across the paddock beside a koneke loaded with branches, drawn by an old grey horse, ... <i> 181 <i>koneke</i>-A low two-wheel vehicle, with a swivel runner in front; in effect, a cross between a sledge and a low waggon. Usually horse drawn; more common in the back country than in farming areas adjacent to towns. <i>(1963,in the Sticks,64</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> ... their old white mare Wendy ... dragged the konaki a few more yards across the paddock. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,135</i>)

>  <b>J. GOODWiN</b> '... `Konaki', a farm sledge with the rear part of the runners replaced by small (barrow?) wheels. i might have been inclined to believe that the name was peculiar to my uncle's hilly Sth Auckland farm if i had not seen it in one of Mary Scott's novels.' <i>(Oct 6, 1980,Letter</i>)

>  <b>MARRiOTT</b> Joe could see i was lost, and explained that a konake is like a sledge, only it has two wide wheels almost at the back and when the horse pulls it lifts the front and the load runs on the wheels. On downhill grades the runners drop and act as a brake. A very simple and efficient vehicle. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,20</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> This pile was built on a konaki sledge ... <i>(1978,Elver, The</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> <i>(1981,Sunrise,60</i>) [NOTES: it was a rich compost which would be later carted away in the konaki sledge ... .]

>  The konaki is a partly wheeled and partly runnered vehicle... The konaki has replaced the ordinary farm sledge on many farms in Taranaki. The name konaki is apparently derived from the Maori "Ko neke," meaning to slide, crawl or move from one place to another. <i>(Apr 1942,NZ Journal of Agriculture,270</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Wool was taken out from the woolshed on a konake behind a big draught horse. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,64</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sledge with wheels at the rear that can be horse-drawn and used on slopes. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The most common type of distributor is the square iron tank, either 200 or 400 gallon capacity, mounted on a konaki. The konaki is fitted with the usual iron skid in front, while the wheels can vary in size from 18 in. to 24 in. in diameter. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,511</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> konaki: mispronunciation of the Maori koneke, a sledge. The farm-type sled usually has a pair of wheels at the trailing ends of the skids. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,47</i>)

>  For Sale: konakis for the farm. Two runners in the front and wheels at rear. <i>(Apr 1, 1944,NZ Dairy Exporter,32</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> You raced home and told a parent, knowing that dad [sic] would go down the road with the konaki and bring him home. <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,140</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> jogger, Chatham islands truck

>  <b>SiMPSON</b> [The jogger's] rival is the konake, akin to the jogger, but with iron or wooden wheels and sometimes a sledge runner in front, and it may be drawn by oxen instead of horses. <i>(1950,Chatham Exiles,145</i>)

>  <b>HOLM3ES</b> Only four vehicles were in use on the island up until this time but by 1952 over 100 were in use. [59] Before the coming of motorised transport, the mode of transportation was the `Chatham island Truck' (Konake), a wooden flat-decked horse or bullock-drawn vehicle, with iron or wooden wheels at the back and sledge runners on the front. Also in use was the `jogger' a cartlike vehicle with two pneumatic-tyred wheels, seating about three people and drawn by two horses attached to a shaft. They worked on the principle of the African Cape Cart. The pole (shaft) was attached to the collars of the horses. Tom McClurg of Te Whakuru made the first `jogger' in Chathams, about 1940. <i>(1984,Chatham islands,58,1948 ed.</i>)

### konini
 
 <i>n.</i> the banded rail (See [katatai (1842)](../K#katatai-(1842)))



 
 <i>n.</i> mistakenly, kotukutuku, the fuchsia tree (see also 1: quotations 1889,1942) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The konini is so well known that the tree itself is called konini more often than kotukutuku. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,282</i>)

>  A thick underbrush of konine (fusia) [sic]. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,125/595</i>) [NOTES: 1844 NZ Journ. 125/595 (Wellington Gazette)]

>  in the [alluvial bush land], which is wooded chiefly with <i>pukateas, kaikateas,</i> and <i>rata</i> trees, and thick underbrush of <i>konine</i> (fuchsia) or <i>roe-roe, kaua kaua</i> (or pepper), &c., entwined with kuriwau or supple-jack, ... <i>(1852,Wellington Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Wellington Gazette in Earp Hand-Book for intending Emigrants 230]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Konine. <i>Fuchsia excorticata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>) [NOTES: [1853 is correct]]

>  Fuchsia excorticata Konini <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  Konini Fuchsia excorticata, Linn. f. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/41</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ... on the rifted top of a huge lifeless tree, the great Black Shag, perched motionless; beneath, Bell-birds ... seek the konini, clinging to its brittle sprays ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/48</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>POTTS</b> The berries of the konini ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,114</i>)

>  17. <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>, Sparrm. Wren, Titipounamu, Pi-wau-wau. - Often builds in a small hole in a tree. i know one old konini which is resorted to yearly . <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,282</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  The trees [in Robinson's Bay] - black and white pine, totara, manuka, kowhai, koanini - were most of them of large size, and they resolved to erect a sawmill ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,56</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> A beautiful `Konini' grove. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,2</i>)

>  After a short flight they all settle again in a konini, or wild fuchsia tree. <i>(Oct 1904,NZ illustrated Magazine,43</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> We got great sprays of clematis - and konini, and drove first through a bush path - <i>(Nov 20, 1907,Letters,1/31,1984 ed.</i>) [NOTES: alson in 1907 Mansfield Urewera Notebook (1978 Gordon) 43]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Konini (koh-nin-nee)</i>: The Bush fuchsia. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,81</i>)

>  <b>KNiGHT</b> .. many trees stood about - mostly Konini, just then full of flowers at their best. <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,18</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 18]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The under-scrub was very dense, Matipo, ini-ini, Konini, Pokaka, Mick-a-Mick, Ohau, Ribbonwood, Ngaio, Lemonwood and others, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,242,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Lacebark, wineberry, konini, mahoe, and water ferns characterised the secondary growth in the gullies and on the flats. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,16,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>konini n</i> -s [Maori] : a tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>) of New Zealand that often attains a height of 40 feet and that has pendulous showy flowers - called also <i>native fuchsia </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1254</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The <i>konini</i> or <i>native fuchsia</i> is called <i>kotukutuku</i> in Maori, and has been called <i>tookytook</i> in English. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,168</i>)

>  <i>n</i>. the native fuchsia of New Zealand; its fruit [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> A sweeping drive skirted by rhododendrons and azaleas, growing beside cabbage-trees, totaras, and koninis. <i>(1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,12</i>) [NOTES: [Canterbury]]

>  <i>konini ... n.</i> native fuchsia of N.Z.; its fruit [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <i>konini [=] tree fuchsia</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

>  <i>konini n.</i> New Zealand fuchsia; its berries. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 274]]



 
 <i>n.</i> the dark purple berry of the tree fuchsia [ORIGIN: Maori, the fruit of the Fuchsia excorticata; often mistakenly applied to the tree itself (more properly kotukutuku); an error poss. reinforced or compounded by the entries in early Williams Dictonary: for the tree not the berry is not in the 3rd 1871 edition (check earlier editions): possibly the common error started with the mistaken entry in the 4th edn 1892, corrected in the 1917 (1921) 157 to Fruit of <i>Fuchsia excorticata (kootukutuku</i> is in all edns from 1871 correctly glossed).]

>  We have been shown a sample of jam made from the berries of the "koniny" or wild fuschia. <i>(Jan 2, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  They had feasted on Konini berries until they were "fed up" beyond the bounds of politeness. <i>(Nov 27, 1926,NZ Observer,Xmas Annual : 26</i>)

>  it's also worth remembering, as you sit down to your first palte of konini berries and cream, that today's produce is very different... <i>(Apr 30, 2006,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Konini</i>, n. <i>fuchsia excorticata</i>; a tree. the attrib<i> konini-tree</i> is not technically wrong; <i>konini-berry</i> is, or is tautologous: <i>ibid.</i> 134 <i>Ko[o]nini </i> Fruit of <i>Fuchsia excorticata. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,68</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> The fruit of the kotukutuku, called konini, is a pleasant tasted berry. <i>(1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Exped Middle island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 267]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Fuchsia<i> excorticata</i>, Linn. fil. ... Nat. name, `Kotuku-tuku', <i>Cunn.</i>; of the berry `Konini', <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 56</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>, Linn. kotukutuku. The fruit is called Konini.-- A small and ornamental tree, 10-30 feet high; trunk sometimes 3 feet in diameter. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,127</i>)

>  <b>KiRK</b> Mr Colenso informs me that it is the kohutuhutu or kotukutuku of the Maoris, the fruit being known as Konini, especially in the South island and the southern part of the North island. The settlers sometimes term it Kotukutuku or Konini, but more generally Fuchsia <i>(1889,Forest Flora,53</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Konini,n.</i> Maori name for (1) the fruit of the New Zealand fuchsia, <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>, Linn. <i>(1898,Austral English,251</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> <i>(1882,Out in the Open,114</i>)

>  <b>KiRK</b> "Mr. Colenso informs me that it [<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>] is the Kohutuhutu and the Kotukutuku of the Maoris, the fruit being known as Konini, especially in the South island and the southern part of the North island. The settlers sometimes term it Kotukutuku or Konini, but more generally fuchsia.' <i>(1889,Forest Flora,53</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>F[uchsia] excorticata, Linn. ... Native fuchsia; Kotukutuku<i>he fruit <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,186</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Fuchsia excorticata (The Tree Fuchsia).</i>.. Maori name <i>Kotukutuku</i> or <i>Kohutuhutu</i>, and of the fruit, <i>Konini. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,294,1940 p. 311 ed.</i>)

>  konini The fruit of the New Zealand fuchsia tree, sometimes used for the tree itself. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> During the warm months there was a long season for koninis from the kotukutuku trees (native Fuchsia) then ... the ramarama berries (Myrtaceae) were ripe. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,16</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Other berries popular as delicacies were the fleshy, white, pineapple-shaped bracts and berries of the kiekie (<i>Freycinetia banksii</i>), the ripe, black berries of the makomako or wineberry (<i>Aristotelia serrata</i>) and the small, sweet berries (known as <i>konini</i> the kotukutuku or tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i> <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

### konini berry
 
 <i>n.</i> tautologous for konini q.v.

>  <b>MOORE</b> Konini Berries <i>(1861,Reminiscences,1</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We used to pick fern and cook it, and konini berries [on West Coast in 1860s] <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,109</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Conversely, the French housewives used konini berries [from Akaroa] in their conserves - and <i>tres bon</i> too. <i>(1978,Acheron Journal,88</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Has anyone ever eaten enough konini berries? <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,187</i>)

### konini tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  `Kotukutuku' and `kohutuhutu' have given way to `fuchsia', or to the now less-common misnomer `konini-tree', unless used in the sense that `pear-tree' is used. <i>(1926,TrNZi,670</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Pop Names]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... a konini tree all brown papered and black with berries to be eaten by the handful. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,39</i>)

### konka
 
 <i>adj.</i> a proprietary backing board for stucco made from cement fibres and pumice.

>  <b>ASHFORD</b> These included 'Konka' which had been patented in 195 by R.M. Mander of Palmerston North, and consisted of a backing board (3ft x 2ft x 5/8in) for stucco made from cement, fibres and pumice... The popularity of Konka later increased and it was advertised nationally in the 1930s. <i>(1994,Bungalow in New Zealand, The,46</i>)

>  <b>SALMOND</b> in New Zealand stucco was applied directly to sheet materials such as asbestos cement, - two local products - Konka board and Gibraltar sheathing and a variety of imitations.... Konka was first used in Wanganui in 1912 as a patented concrete sheet 91cm x 76cm by 15 mm thick. <i>(1986,Old New Zealand,204</i>)

### kono
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also konae](../A#also-konae))

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> Look here, this is mine (holding a food-basket (kono) in his hand, which he buried in the earth <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,47</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The food-bearers advanced in a compact body, each carrying a `kono' (small food basket), ... and specifying the food brought forward. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,253</i>)

>  The welcoming over - aha! - the grace of the young women bearing their <i>kono</i>, the little baskets of food. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,31</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The <i>hangis</i> had just ben uncovered and the food put into little freshly-made flax baskets called <i>kono</i>, one for each person. <i>(1948,Taina,15</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> To others my love is a plaited kono/ Full or empty,/ With chunks of riwai,/ Meat that stuck to the stones. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,537,1972 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1972) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 537]

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> kono <i>woven food basket </i> <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> kino <i>woven food basket </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <b>iHiMAERA</b> He laid a kono before him. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,285</i>)

### koomara, koomera
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kumara](../K#kumara))

### koota/kooti/kuti
 
 <i>n.</i> variants of kutu q.v.

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Koota, Kooti, Kuti.</i> New Zealand forms (late C. 19-20) <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,464</i>)

### kooter/kooti/kootie
 
 <i>n.</i> variants of kutu (See [also cootie](../A#also-cootie))

>  <b>BAKER</b> Terms originated in the first Great War [include] <i>chatty</i> ... a louse (the versions koota and <i>kooti </i>from the Maori <i>kutu</i>, a louse had been in use much earlier). <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> [Maori children speaking] `An she got a kootie in her hairs.' ... `She's got kooties in her hairs' ... `That was Rita's kootie. it jumped out of her hair into mine last week.' <i>(1958,Spinster,112</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> <i>(1963,Teacher,129</i>)

>  Mr. Kirk said that Mr. Hanan, while in opposition, had inflamed the passions of the people against apartheld, but we now saying those people protesting against it were `kooters on a kuri' - fleas on a dog. <i>(Jul 2, 1965,Dominion,7/1</i>)

>  <i>kooti n.</i> louse [Maori <i>kutu</i>]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> i'll just find out for myself about the lice ... So [we] buy a tooth-comb, but what we call in the pa ... a `kootie comb'. <i>(1958,Spinster,123</i>)

### kootiest
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> When things at the school are looking their kootiest i cheer up and think there'll be a change soon. <i>(1958,Spinster,125</i>)

### kopa
 
 <i>n.</i> a native oven (Rarawa dial); copper Maori [[??Transfer to copper Maori.]]

>  <b>HAY</b> Fish and meat were frequently roasted ... but the great national culinary institution was the earth-oven, the kopa or hangi. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/153</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> Food was cooked by steaming in a hangi or kopa (an oven dug out in the ground). <i>(1913,Place Names...of Otago and Southland,24</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The pig ... was then placed in the koppah. <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,32</i>)

### kopaki
 
 <i>n.</i> wrapping, flax mat

>  <b>BRATHWAiTE</b> KOPAKi Wrapping. Also a sort of flax mat, fairly small, with a handle on two opposite edges, for carrying food, etc. <i>(1967,Evil Day,379</i>)

### kopakipaki
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOSER</b> That mess, which is nothing but putrified indian corn, ... is called by the natives `ko [21] paki-paki'. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,21</i>)

### kopakopa
 
 <i>n.</i> Chatham island forget-me-not/lily

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The forget-me-not, Kopa-kopa Myosotis, with its fine bunches of flowers and large glossy leaves, is a native of these [Chatham] isles. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,225</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> kidney fern

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The Underwood, consists of ... the delicate lady's hair, the Kopakopa, an elegant plumy fern, <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/134</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Cardiomanes reniforme </i>(Forst.f.) Presl. Common name: <i>Kidney fern </i> Maori name: <i>Kopakopa </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Plantago major</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kopa-kopa, <i>Lindsay. Plantago sp. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Puka ... Kopakopa <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,503</i>)

>  They also used the ashes of burnt `tussac' grass, and a lotion prepared by boiling the leaves of the plant <i>kopakopa (Plantago major)</i>, or from the bruised leaves of the <i>kopata (Pelargonium australe). </i> <i>(1905,TrNZi,37/67</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Plantago</i> sp. Common name: <i>Plantain </i>Maori names: <i>Kopakopa, parerarera ... </i>[in Williams 5th ed.] the term `kopakopa' was identified as <i>Plantago</i> spp. but in the 7th ed. it is identified as <i>Myosotidium hortense,</i> ... Taylor and Andersen identified `kopakopa' as plantain ... <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,79</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> You now use another type of leaf - kopakopa... This heals and closes the wound. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,56</i>)

### kopapa
 
 <i>n.</i> a small canoe

>  <b>GORST</b> i was coming up the river in a `kopapa', a small canoe paddled by a single boy. <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,302</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a young or small kahawai

>  <b>MORELAND</b> KAHAWAi [<i>Arripis trutta</i>] ... in Australia it is known as salmon, sea salmon, and Australian salmon. Small examples are known as kopapa in northern New Zealand. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,50</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> KAHAWAi ... <i>Other names: Arripis trutta</i>; sea salmon, salmon, Australian salmon (Australia). Kopapa is used for half-grown, coarsely-spotted examples in Taranaki and on parts of the Auckland west coast. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,236</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>KAHAWAi </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Arripis trutta</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Ocean Trout (canned). (c) Other common names: Kahawai; Kopapa (juveniles). (d) Principal localities where taken: Bay of Plenty, Gisborne, Nelson. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,30</i>)

>  [species name] kahawai .. [scientific name] <i>Arripis trutta</i> .. [other name(s)] Australian salmon, ocean trout, kawhai, kahawhai, etc., kopapa, sea trout, sea salmon <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Kahawai Arripis trutta</i> ... Known principally by its Maori name, kahawai, in New Zealand and only occasionally (and erroneously, despite its scientific name) as sea trout or salmon. ... The name kopapa is sometimes given to the barred or spotted juveniles. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,92</i>)

### kopara
 
 <i>n.</i> bellbird

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNiS MELANURA. (BELL-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The bell-bird, which has so many Maori names, is here called the <i>koparapara. </i> <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol ii South island) 170</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of wheat. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Oroua shows more resistance to stripe rust than both Kopara and Rongotea. in the medium to high yield potential areas, the choice of autumn sown cultivars is between Rongotea and Kopara. <i>(Aug 22, 1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

### kopata
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pelargonium <i>clandestinum </i>... Nat. name, `Kopata', Middle island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 41</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kopata, <i>Lyall. Pelargonium clandestinum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Pelargonium australe Kopata ... Geum urbanum Kopato <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/124</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury) [is this HWOs error, or in orig. chk??]]

>  <b>FEATON</b> PELARGONiUM AUSTRALE (<i>Willd</i>.) ... The `KOPATA'.- This pretty perennial herb is abundant throughout both islands, ... Kopata springs up indigenously upon nearly all newly-cultivated soil, and becomes a hardy weed. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Kopata [Maori name] .. Pelargonium [Settlers' name] .. Pelargonium Australe .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,75</i>)

>  They also used the ashes of burnt `tussac' grass, and a lotion prepared by boiling the leaves of the plant <i>kopakopa (Plantago major),</i> or from the bruised leaves of the <i>kopata (Pelargonium australe). </i> <i>(1905,TrNZi,37/67</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Pelargonium ... inodorum </i>... Scentless Pelargonium. KOPATA, PUKU-PUKU. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,32</i>)

>  <i>kopata [s] Pelargonium inodorum </i>Willd. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> KOPATA (<i>Pelargonium inodorum</i>) ... is widespread in New Zealand, frequent after fires or bush clearing and in some districts in field crops, ... <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,63</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kopata, <i>Col[enso]. Geum urbanum</i> var. <i>strictum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> <i>G[eum] urbanum</i>, L., var. <i>strictum</i>, Ait. ... The `Kopata' of the North island Maoris, (Colenso); a term also applied, however, to <i>Pelargonium clandestinum. </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,60</i>)

>  Kopata. Geum urbanum, var. strictum <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/41</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> GEUM URBANUM (<i>Linn</i>.) var. <i>strictum.</i> The Common Avens or Herb Bennet. The `KOPATA'.- This plant is common in the central and southern sub-alpine parts of the Northern and throughout the Middle island. <i>ibid./</i> 177 Kopata [Maori name] .. Avens [Settlers' name] .. Geum urbanum .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,123</i>)

>  <b>STARK</b> <i>KOPATA (Herb-Bennett)</i> Geum urbanum <i>var. strictum</i>. This is a variant of the Common Avens of the British isles, Russia, mid-Europe, and parts of Asia. ... The Herb-Bennett is not really a common plant throughout New Zealand ... <i>(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,46</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Geum urbanum</i> L. Common names: <i>Common avens, herb-bennett</i> Maori name: <i>Kopata ... </i>The variety of this widespread species which occurs in New Zealand and Australia is known as var. <i>strictum</i> (Ait.) Hook.f. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,85</i>)

### kopi
 
 <i>n.</i> the karaka tree [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  [caption] The Kopi (Karaka tree) adorned with the carving was taken from private land on the main Chatham island. <i>(Jun 23, 2005,Footnotes,15</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> This is [the karaka's] southernmost habit, though it also grows in the Chatham islands, [234] where it is known to the natives as <i>Kopi. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,233,1940, p. 247 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> it [Karaka] also grows in the Chatham islands, where it is known to the natives as <i>Kopi. Karaka</i> is by far the commonest name. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,247</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> karaka berry [[Williams does not note it as Chatham Islands.]]

>  The glossy green karaka with its bundles of golden fruit is deceptively attractive. The kernels of the fruit contain a strong poison which maims and kills. Nevertheless, karaka berries - kopi - were a significant food for early Maori. <i>(May 2003,Forest & Bird,No. 308/28</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Corynocarpus ... laevigata</i> ... KOPi (for both tree and fruit). <i>(1952,Chatham islands,62</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> karaka nut

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The commonest of the forest-trees [on the Chatham islands] is the karaka, here called kopi (<i>Corynocarpus laevigata</i>), ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,121</i>)

>  <b>SKiNNER & BAUCKE</b> Moriori carvings on trunks of kopi trees: ... <i>ibid.</i> 377 This did not apply to karaka nuts (<i>kopi-i</i> to the Moriori), which, picked and cured, were rigidly divided out to scale. <i>(1928,Morioris, The,B P Museum Memoirs Vol.iX No.5/345</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol.iX No.5 pp. 343-382) 345]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The [karaka] seed is said to be poisonous in its crude state, but is much esteemed by the natives, when cooked after their method. The seeds in this state taste of oil only, and are called <i>kopi. </i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/287</i>)

### kopi nut
 
 <i>n.</i> karaka berry

>  Spent some hours driving the main mob of sheep from the fence where they mob day and night in the water into the bush where it is dry ... and abundance of feed the ground being covered with fallen branches of kopi nuts. <i>(Mar 10, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,267,1950 ed.</i>)

### kopi tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Felled a nest of large kopi trees. Five of them hung on one. <i>(Sep 26, 1884,Chudleigh Diary,329,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... the nest [of the Chatham island bellbird] is usually bult in kopi trees <i>Corynocarpus laevigata </i> <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,279</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  (And) <i>Corynocarpus laevigata </i> <i>(1888,NZ Country Journal,Vol i 1877-XXii 1898</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Corynocarpus laevigata </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>SKiNNER</b> ... i was able to see the two surviving Morioris, ... to study the carvings on the <i>kopi</i> trees ... <i>(1923,Morioris of the Chatham islands, The,4</i>)

### kopipiro
 
 <i>n.</i> parore, mangrove fish

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

### kopu
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of white clover (Trifolium repens).

>  .. the smaller-leaf cultivar (tahora) outyielded the larger-leafed pitau and kopu under set-stocking. <i>(Nov 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,55</i>)

>  Last year we paid $3.70 a kg for Kopu white clover; this year we're paying $4. <i>(Feb 28, 1990,NZ Farmer,22</i>)

>  in a farm trial at Taupo, kopu enjoyed an advantage of 12o% to 160% over huia in autumn and winter. <i>(Mar 1991,NZ Dairy Exporter,17</i>)

>  Kopu - best suited to controlled rotational grazing ibid. 4 Suitable for use in rotational sheep-beef systems, as well as being used in conjunction with larger-leaved cultivars such as Kopu for extra winter activity in dairying situations. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,3</i>)

>  This irish clover produced more annual drymatter than Kopu, Huia or Pitau in trials at Ruakura or Palmerston North. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,79</i>)

A small piece of kopuru would fill a room with its smell and it was said that an important person would die soon after if only one person in a group could smell it. <i>(Jan 15, 2003,Capital Times,6</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. Plectognathi there are three species: Tetraodon Hamiltoni, the Monacanthus parika, and the Diadona globulas. Kopuawai, <i>Koputaratara, Papati</i> and <i>Totara</i>, all these are descriptive names, implying that it is covered with sharp spines, and is inflated; this singular fish is frequently cast up on the shore, and sometimes of a large size. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,625</i>)

### koradi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He whistled the evening hymn monotonously and incessantly, beating time with a koradi stick against the raupo and flax leaves. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,279</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  She grabbed something between a dropper and a post and i a Koradi. <i>(Jan 26, 1912,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> <i>Koradi</i> was the catch-word. Binding together these light flax-flower stalks (the<i> koradis</i>) with the flax blades he constructed a raft ... <i>(1970,Stewart islanders,196</i>)
