 <i>n.</i> a univalve mollusc

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|oramo; A sort of shell-fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,167</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. <i>Cottoideoe</i>, there are six enumerated, of these the <i>Gurnard (kumu-kumu) </i>is most common, and the <i>Trigla Volitans (korama)</i> the most beautiful. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,624</i>)

### korangiwhaiora
 
 <i>n.</i> ?? [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The old bell sounds to signal chapel for korangiwhaiora. <i>(May 23, 2006,NZ Herald,E7,The College Herald ed.</i>)

### korari
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a flax flower-stem [used as firewood or to make mokihi rafts prior to bridges being built.] [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The only passage was by flax korari raft or mokihi. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,Weekly News,15</i>)

>  She grabbed something between a dropper and a post and i a Koradi. <i>(Feb 26, 1927,NZ Dairy Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> caraddy or kraddy: mutilation of the Maori name korari given to the dry stalks of flax flowers (<i>Phormium tenax</i>). [caraddy; koradi; kraddy] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,16</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON & FRASER</b> There was manuka scrub growing which made good firewood, and plenty of 'korari' or flaxsticks. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> flax (plant) [ORIGIN: Maori, The plant itself (in the north).: anglicised in many forms, esp. in the SI: claddie, colladdy, kolladdy, koraddy, etc; the tall flower stem of the native flax; often the plant itself [Maori korari[o-macron]].]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Phormium tenax is a flag-like plant, the sword-shaped drooping leaves of which the natives call Harakeke;the flower-stalk, bearing pink blossoms and resembling agaves, is called Korari. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),150</i>)

>  <b>KENDALL</b> Duaterra and a party of friends were actively employed in cutting koraddee (or flax in the growing state) on the other side of the bay. <i>(Jul 23, 1843,Letter,64</i>) [NOTES: 1814 July 3 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 64]

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Flax-plant Kora[acute]ddee <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/334</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|oradi, s. The flax plant. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,167</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> The hill was covered completely with the flax plant which is called Corade[acute] Mauvre, and which grew in great luxuriance. [Andersen fn.: ..<i>korari</i> is usually the tall flower-stalk of <i>harakeke</i> ..., but in the North sometimes used to designate the plant itself. Here McCrae uses the word <i>maori</i> in its original sense signifying `normal, usual, ordinary'. <i>(1820,Journal,22,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> [The net] is made of Koraddy [korari] or Flax undrest split green and worked up. They make Baskets or Kits as we call them for Potatoes and a Chief or Head of a Familly has a clean basket made of Koraddy for his plate of potatoes ... every meal. <i>ibid.</i> 62 Koraddie or Flax, Vines, Rattan and number of other Creepers crossing the Path and Tripping one up, .... [marginal note] Souple Jacks. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),46,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... if commerce was instituted among his tribe, they would be employed in working for articles that would prove most serviceable to them, by dressing the korari, or flax, felling timber, and planting provisions for other markets. <i>ibid.</i> 1/104 ... we descended into a deep flat valley, covered with the korari, or flax plant. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/78</i>)

>  <b>MURRAY</b> (And) [KORADi] <i>Phormium tenax </i> <i>(1838,Account of Phormium Tenax, An</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... a beautiful vale entirely filled with korady or flax which rendered the walk fatiguing ... <i>(Apr 15, 1839,Journal,2/102</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The flax (<i>korari</i>,) forming the dresses is of two or [176] three kinds; the most coarse, called muka, is employed for working the common garment (<i>kakaou</i>[a1-acute] <i>maori</i>[a-acute].) This flax is also made use of for laying up ropes, lines, &c. The superior dress called Kaitaka[a2-a3-acute], is made from silken flax, (hunga hunga[as-acute].) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/175</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> We passed the night among the korari <i>(1841,Memo of a Journey,14</i>)

>  ... kauradi or Flax. <i>(1843,NZ Journal,92-177</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> TREES [MET WiTH iN PELORUS]. Kawa-Kawa ... Flax - Korari of natives ... <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,67</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Korari, <i>Phormium tenax</i> and <i>Colensoi. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> in Otago the general name of the [flax] plant, as a whole, is `Korari,' - or as metamorphosed by the settler, pronounced and spelt `Koura<i>d</i>i,' [a-macron] or `Cora<i>d</i>i' [a-macron]. The same term is applied in the North island equally to the whole plant, and to the dried flower-stem when cut for rafts or other purposes; or exclusively to the flower-stalk. in the North [83] island, the Flax-plant is also known as `Harakeke,' or `Herekiakia,' - its usual name there. ... The lower part of the flower-stem is `Kaikaha;' the outer part of the leaf `Parakoka;' [a1, o-macron] the refuse of the leaf `Hunga-hunga;' the flax, dressed or not, or scraped fibre, manufactured from the leaf, `Whitau,' or `Muka.' <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,82</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> When the korari (<i>Phormium tenax</i>) is in full bloom, the horn-shaped flowers are filled with delicious nectar. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,94,{1888 2nd edn 1/90} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Harakeke or Korati [sic](<i>Phormium tenax</i>) is the justly celebrated New Zealand flax. it is plentiful everywhere, on bush-land and open-land, rich soil and poor soil, hill and dale, from the Reinga to the Bluff. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/204</i>)

>  <i>korari n</i> -s [Maori] : NEW ZEALAND FLAX <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1255</i>)

>  [Caption] Sister Enid, brother Jim and a friend on a raft tied together with flax and made from driftwood and bundles of dry flax flower stalks known as korari. <i>(Oct 2003,NZ Memories,No. 44/23</i>)

>  in 1887, Tane Tinorau of the Maniopoto tribe and English surveyor Fred Mace drifted through what would become the famous Glowworm Grotto on a raft made of <i>korari</i>, flax flower stalks. <i>(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,No. 34/43</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> flax stick [Many and varied spgs representing the hearing of (?dialectal) Maori: /k/ as [k] or [g] /r/ as [r] or [l] /o:/ as [?], [?], [zero]]

>  <b>MONRO</b> The method of crossing [the Waitaki] is upon a mogi, which is a floating body, somewhat in the shape of a boat, made of bundles of bulrushes tied together, and strengthened by the flowering stem of the flax, called koradi. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 240]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Constructed a raft of flax-stalks, or <i>korari</i>, in length about 22 feet. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 217]

>  The Korari is the name of the flower stalk, often, but wrongly, used as the name of the plant. The edible root is the Aruhe. The word `Muka' is commonly applied to the dressed fibre; it is the correct name for the inner leaves. Cleared bush-land is suitable for the cultivation of the flax plant, and even swamp-land is improved by drainage. in the various flax swamps near nelson ...the finest plants grow upon the soil that has been thrown out of the ditch ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/117</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy NZ Flax)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> For the benefit of the unlearned ... let me here explain that a `Mokihi' is constructed of `Koradies'; - <i>Anglice</i>:- the flowering stalks of the flax, - three faggots of which, lashed [83] firmly in a point to the small ends, and expanded by a piece of wood at the stern, constitute the sides and bottom of the frail craft, ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iii/82-83,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAiNES</b> When i had four made i fixed one to the top of a korari or flax-stalk, ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,209</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> A Kolladie (the flower-stalk of the flax: about seven feet long) was carried by each as a balancing pole <i>ibid.</i> 371 Each carried a long six or seven foot New Zealand alpenstock or colladie (the flower stalk of flax) to feel their way with. <i>(1879,Old identities, The,53</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Korari is the flower stalk of the flax. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 65]

>  <b>SHiRRES</b> William and i determined to supplement them by gathering flax sticks - coradies- at the river side. <i>(Aug 26, 1884,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1884 Aug 26 Shirres Diary in 1972 Mcleod High Country Anthology 113]

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> We collected all the dried <i>cooraddies</i> (flower stalks o f the flax-bush) <i>(1888,Wakatipians,9</i>)

>  <b>FROBiSHER</b> Through bush and fern and flax koladdy, .... no more will follow me poor Paddy. <i>(1893,Sketches of Gossipton,75</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Korari, n. </i>Often pronounced <i>Koraddy </i>and <i>Koladdy</i>, and spelt variously; the Maori word for the flowering stem of <i>Phormium tenax</i>, J. and G. Forst. (q.v.), generally used for making a mokihi (q.v.). There is a Maori noun, kora, a small fragment; and a verb korari to pluck a twig, or tear it off. <i>(1898,Austral English,253</i>)

>  "A Kolladie(the flower stalk of the flax, about seven feet long) carried by each, as a balancing pole or staff.' <i>(1879,Old identities, The,53</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> But now the faithful dog is gone./ Through bush and fern and flax koraddi,/ <i>(1905,Poems & Sketches,124</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> A flax-stick, or dried flower stem, is known to colonists as a korari, koradi, or kaladi. The first name is of course, the correct one ... . <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,106,1940 p. 109 ed.</i>)

>  These koraddi were in great demand as firewood, ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,43,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <i>korari n.</i> New Zealand flax; the flower stem of the plant. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  The dried reed stalks [of flax] (which Maoris and children tied into bundles to make rafts) are called flax sticks, <i>koradi</i> sticks or<i> kraddy </i>sticks, i suppose a corrupted Maori word. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

>  <b>AYSON</b> [Clutha] Camp-fires of dry koradis soon dried clothes and provided every one with hot tea. <i>(1937,Thomas,63</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The [Maori] tops would be whipped by means of a lash of flax tied to a bit of koradi (flax stick) and soon the fun grew fast and furious. <i>ibid.</i> 175 ... i got a koladi and fixed a noose to it ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>R. MASON</b> Cladies were used instead of lathes to make a house for raising seedlings. it was called the clady house. <i>(1942,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Foxton Flaxmill]

>  <i>korari</i>: dried stem of flax plant. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> [He] ... obtained a stout korari stick from a neighbouring swamp. The korari, by the way, is the flowering stalk of the <i>Phormium tenax</i>, or native flax ... <i>(1952,Faring South,69</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The largest circle possible was marked out with koraris thrust into the turf [to mark out a race in Southland c1900]. <i>(1953,Chaslands,40</i>)

>  korari Usually, but incorrectly, koradi. The thick flowering stem of the New Zealand `flax' <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  <i>korari n. </i> flowering stem of the New Zealand flax. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <i>korari n. </i> . flowering stem of N.Z. flax. [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>VEiTCH</b> Waggoners used wood substitutes for fuel for cooking during the journey from Central to Dunedin. `Buffalo chips' (dried dung), `yellow pine' (straw), or `kaladdies' (the flower and stem of flax) were burned in a can which was hung behind the waggon. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,17</i>)

>  <b>R. BENTON</b> heard a Central Otago woman in her late sixties who said: `Look at all those gladdies', meaning flax-sticks, not gladioli <i>(1978,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>GOWANS</b> Her dirty fingers poked into the embers her flax flowers, dead grass and dung, which she called her `yellow pine' or `buffalo chips' or the melodic-sounding `Kaladdies'. <i>(1985,Heart of the High Country,35</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> My Traps went inside, and in an hour some Pork and Potatoes from a "Coppre Mourie' came in Smoking with the Koraddie Baskets, and at it we went. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> we had to ascend by an almost perpendicular path in one place having a Koradi rope to lay hold of. <i>(Jul 10, 1843,Journal,48</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> When we reached the river's edge ... we found that Wahinei had crossed on a Korari mokihi and was repairing a large one of raupo. <i>(Oct 24, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Oct 24 Mantell Diary in 1947 Stephenson Maori and Pakeha 128]

>  <b>BULLER</b> When the korari-flower <i>(Phormium tenax)</i> is in season, the Kakas repair in flocks to the flax-fields to feast on the flower-honey; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,49</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> One night ... Dr. Shortland took up his quarters in a cask, probably an old whale oil barrell which had been washed ashore from some whaling station, and doubtless ... he enjoyed more peaceful slumbers than under the miserable shelter of the korari breakwind at Kuri Bush. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,38</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the tall<i> korari </i>stalks of the flax-bushes dipping to the water-side, [bear] abundant promise of honey liquor for the summer birds, ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol ii South island) 28</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> ... he came pushing through the tall flax-blades and black koradi-sticks and found her sitting demure in her print gown ... <i>(1938,Promenade,44</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> a rounders bat [or does this refer to the stick??].

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> `A common bat [for rounders] was the stem of the Korari, from the flax bush. it had to be still wet because it was brittle when dry. We called it a Kaladdy' (South Clutha, Otago, 1875). <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,154</i>)

### korari honey
 
 <i>n.</i> flax honey (See [cf. manuka honey](../M#manuka-honey))

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> The honey of the flax blossom is also in season, called <i>korari</i>, and, when mixed with fern root, also makes a species of confectionery. <i>(1848,Journal of an Expedition,293</i>)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> Whilst enumerating the fruits of New Zealand, the honey <i>(korari)</i> of the <i>Phormium tenax</i>, or flax plant, must not be forgotten. <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 131]

>  <b>SCOTT</b> One of our party had taken eight hundred pounds ... of "Korari honey' last season. <i>(1860,Rough Notes of Travel,42</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... in December and January [Tuis] leave the forest and repair to the flax-fields to feast on the korari honey. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/98</i>)

### korari stick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Accordingly a raft, constructed with bundles of korari sticks tied together, and called mokihi by the natives, was launched with the next flood tide, and upon this one of the party ... started for the kaik ... <i>ibid.</i> ...their sole shelter [was] a little hut or breakwind constructed of koraris or flax stalks. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The heart ... was carried up country, stuck on the end of a <i>korari</i> stick (flax-stalk), and was taken to the Kuiti district. spg <i>claddie </i> <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/470,1983 ed.</i>)

>  A strict discipline in relation to schoolwork ... was balanced by total freedom in the weekends to ... fight with craddie [chk cr cl??] sticks (that is, flax weapons). We played `Bar the Door', where the last person to escape the line was a hero. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,117</i>) [NOTES: (Elworthy)]

### korau
 
 <i>n.</i> mamaku, or its edible shoots [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|orau, s. A glutinous plant so called; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,168</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> ... in former times the food of the natives consisted of sweet potatoes, taro (Caladium esculentum), fern-root (Pteris esculenta), ... the pulp of a fern-tree (Cyathea medullaris) called korau or mamako, ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/17</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The most remarkable [fern] is koraie which grows from ten to fifteen feet high. The stem and stalk are dark or black, the branches commence about two feet from the ground ... ...The Ponga fern is perhaps the male tree, as it grows frequently near the Korau and is like it in leaf, with the exception of growing taller and the stalk bare, ... <i>ibid.</i> 119 ... black tree-fern (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>), often 50 feet high.] <i>(1844,NZ Journal,109,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [We] received several presents of Pitau the inside of the stem of the mamaku or korau the Cyathea medullaris, which forms a wholesome and palatable food ... <i>(Dec 10, 1846,Journal,4/149</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Cyathea <i>medullaris</i>, Sw. ... Nat. name, `Korau' of the northern tribes, `Mamaku' of the southern, <i>Col. </i> <i>(1855,Flora NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Korau, <i>Col[enso]. Cyathea medullaris. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Also, the cooked leaves and herbaceous tops of [32] the Toi (<i>Barbarea Australis</i>), and of the Poroporo, or Raupeti (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>); and the baked inner stems and sago-like pith of the large black fern tree, Korau, or Mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/31-32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>GRACE</b> A gigantic tree lay by the road, against the trunk of which a number of fronds of a beautiful korau were leaning; one end was stuffed up with fern, the other being open. <i>(Aug 1877,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Aug T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 273]

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... the pith of the Korau (fern tree) ... <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,235</i>)

>  The lovely nikau palm ... with the stately koraus growing beside it, it is slaughtered to furnish cheap decoration for a country ball-room. <i>(1894,TrNZi,26/620</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[yathea] medullaris, Swartz</i> ... Trunk 20-50 ft. high ... <i>Korau; Mamaku; Black Tree-fern. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,948</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The heart of the delicious<i> palms</i>, called E' Rito, is highly esteemed. The korou, which the branches of another of these umbelliferous trees afford is equally prized, together with the saccharine roots of the ti, cabbage-tree. <i>(1938,New Zealand  (Polack),1/287</i>)

>  55/152 <i>korau</i>, edible young shoots of mamaku, which <i>see. </i> <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]



 
 <i>n.</i> wild turnip tops

>  <b>PARK</b> i took her advice and every day gathered half a sugarbag full of puha (sow thistle) and korau, the wild turnip. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,220</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> These fish [eels], `korau' or wild turnip tops, and fern root, were just now the natives' only food; ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,170</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Korau,<i> Lindsay. Brassica Rapa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native fern, Cyathea medullaris or mamaku. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  That monarch is not as well-known as its cousin, the mamaku korau or black tree-fern. <i>(May 11, 1922,Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Some korau stood in a damp hollow over that way and i knew of a wallow beneath them that was often used by the swine. <i>(1997,Wild Boars of Oruaiti, The,58</i>)

### koreirei
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Koreirei, <i>Col[enso]</i>. Roots of <i>Typha angustifolia.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

### koreka
 
 <i>n.</i> quail

>  COTURNiX NOVAE ZELANDiAE, Quoy. Koreka. Quail. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/66</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

### korere
 
 <i>n.</i> ?korero

>  When a matter of importance arose, they would go into a 'korere' reminiscent of an American football huddle. <i>(Dec 2001,NZ Memories,No. 27/41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [korire](../K#korire))

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-NFB `KORERE' (Grey Warbler) <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

### korero
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, Ryan 109]

>  [Hdg] Native Korero. Big Gathering at Waahi. <i>(Jan 31, 1912,Dominion,4</i>)

>  He struck up a korero with a native gentleman. <i>(Jul 9, 1910,NZ Free Lance,5</i>)

>  After many koreros.. it was decided to hit Perrett in the way that would hurt him most. <i>(Oct 4, 1943,NZEF Times,iii/118 : 10</i>)

>  Minorities in our Westminster-style democracy may be heeded somewhat less than in - say - a Maori korero, but they can count on less arbitrary treatment than a petitioner can expect of an autocrat. <i>(Feb 16, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Throughout the 'korero' the Maoris expressed gratitude for the work that Mr Patrick, the school teacher, was doing amongst them. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,129</i>)

>  Nor did she contribute to the roles of the second row, such as supporting the korero, lead waiata, lead or support karanga. <i>(Oct 31, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  They tried to up get [sic] "korero," and demurred to dispersing without their beloved ''utu. " <i>(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,32</i>)

>  "Dad will take anybody in and have a korero." <i>(Nov 2, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),38</i>)

>  A long korero was immediately held in the runanga house. <i>(Aug 27, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  <b>PARKiNSON</b> Orero, To speak, or a speech. [...] <i>(1770,Journal,126</i>) [NOTES: 1770 Parkinson Journal 126, 127 A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...]]

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> Corero .. Speaking. <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),75</i>) [NOTES: (Maori Vocabulary)]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ko'rero, <i>s</i>. a speaking; <i>v.n.</i> speaking. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,168</i>)

>  talk - ko'dero [korero] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112 Also >1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 285 (|= macron precedes)]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> A very Original set of Tattooed Gentlemen cam [sic] aft and there was a grand Corrirow [<i>korero</i>] or Talk on the occasion, ... <i>ibid.</i> 51 ... Noble Virtues of the deceased were to be Eulogized in "Corrirow' or Council talk. ... They formed a Council or Grand "Corrirow' or open Parliament. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> A very Original set of Tattooed Gentlemen cam [sic] aft and there was a grand Corrirow [<i>korero</i>] or Talk on the occasion, ... <i>ibid.</i> 51 ... Noble Virtues of the deceased were to be Eulogized in "Corrirow' or Council talk. ... They formed a Council or Grand "Corrirow' or open Parliament. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  A Korero took place in which the Mauries were found very tractable. <i>ibid.</i> 223 The Koorooroo commenced afresh. <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,218,1966 ed.</i>)

>  After a very long talk (korero) i was asked for my reply. <i>(1842,GBPP,1842 (No.569) 97</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Dec 31 Report of George Clarke, Protector of Aborigines in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 97]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> in the evening i had a long korero with the candidates for the sact. [sacrament] <i>(Jan 7, 1843,Journal,10</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Jan 7 Taylor Journal 1842-1843 [TS:ATB] 10]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> `There were about sixty men assembled, and they proceeded to hold a `korero,' or talk on the all-important subject.' <i>ibid.</i>. 81: `With the exception of an occasional exclamation of `korero, korero,' `speak, speak,' which was used like our `hear, hear,' in either an encouraging or an ironical sense, or an earnest but low expression of approval or dissent, no interruption of the orators ever took place.' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,78</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> A <i>korero</i>, or gathering of the native orators, was held at the mission station, to meet Forsaith; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/58</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> There is to be a great `korero', or public meeting, at Otaki tomorrow. ... We found an immense assembly of natives, feasting and making long-winded speeches; ... They were of course very great in talk, expressing the highest opinion of the absent Governor and the English "rangatiras' who were present; ... <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,32</i>)

>  Some korero about white man's land being South of the Coldstream and Maories' North. <i>(Jan 10, 1849,Torlesse Papers,47,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EDWARD WARD</b> A korerro, or native counsel. <i>(Feb 3, 1851,Journal,121,1951 ed.</i>)

>  After long delay the General Government have sent down Mr. Rogan to assist the two land purchase Commissioners who are on the spot in talking to the natives here, and to ascertain their intentions. ... so the question has advanced a step; a Commissioner from Auckland is sent to have a Korero!! <i>(Aug 8, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> This ended, they retired, ... then ... marched off to the place appointed for korero. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,29</i>)

>  A party of natives ... have gone down to Otaki to the great <i>korero</i> that is being held there respecting the hoisting of the King Flag on the 12th March. <i>(Mar 23, 1861,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> A few years back we used to hear a meeting of the tribes simply termed a `korero', a `talk', or a `ko-miti', a `committee', and it is only lately that our ears have been regaled with the high sounding term, `runanga'. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,28</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Here we stopped, and the canoes were drawn up in front of the <i>pa</i>, amidst much <i>tangi</i>-ing, and shouts of <i>`Haeremai, haeremai, haeremai!'</i> - the usual amount of shaking hands and rubbing noses being over, a long <i>korero</i>, or talk, ensued. <i>(1866,Chatham islands,17</i>)

>  <b>STAFFORD</b> (T.M. Haultain) i had a korero with [the Maoris] <i>(1866,Papers,3/143</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> (The Ohinemuri Deputation) Eicke's constitution, we hear,/ Was so delicate .../ That on the journey he'd be queer,/ Unless sustained by bottled beer;/ Simpson said pulling [sc. rowing] made him dry,/ .../ .../ So of grog they took a good supply/ When they went to Ohinemuri./ [33] Their life was one continual shout,/ They stayed up there and idled about/ .../ But the beer and the grog was running out./ Of course they'd had many a <i>korero</i> [kore|ro],/ Which led to nothing at all ... <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,32</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> They [the Colonel and Maoris] had a corero or parley for some time, while we ... looked to our carbines in case of a row. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,131</i>)

>  Then there was held a grand `Korero' of the principal Native Chiefs, .. <i>(1940,Founding of New Zealand, The,204</i>) [NOTES: 1873 S.Mathew Autobiography in 1940 Founding of NZ 204]

>  <b>TiNNE</b> They were holding a `korero' or talk with some of the chiefs. [?to A] <i>ibid.</i> 31 The korero might have been indefinitely prolonged, for, like all colonials, they [Maoris] are much given to `yarning' ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,27</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... it was arranged we were to have a grand consultation <i>korero</i> next morning, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/3/146</i>)

>  `All this after a very excited `korero' on the empty dray, with the surging and exciting crowd around.' <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,42/3</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The whole matter was carefully explained, and sufficient time allowed them for their usual <i>korero. </i> <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,57</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Korero</i>, n. Maori for a conference, a conversation. The verb means `to tell, to say, to address, to speak, to talk.' (`Williams' Maori Dictionary,' 4th. ed.) <i>(1898,Austral English,253</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> He could not get out of the <i>korero</i>. His <i>mana </i>(i.e. <i>prestige</i>)<i> was at stake. </i> <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,67</i>)

>  <b>iNGRAM</b> Met several Whakatane boys to-day and had a great korero, swapping experiences and information about friends. <i>(Nov 7, 1917,Anzac Diary,76,1987 ed.</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> a conference; council meeting; conversation. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> The open marae where the sun beats down relentlessly is now deserted. <i>Afterwards there were pois and there were hakas, ... and there was to be a korero on politics and the land question ... </i> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,13,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> it was a very long drawn out argumentative <i>korero. </i> <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,41</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> A korero among themselves followed, and the order for trousers was handed in. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,120</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]rero</i>, talk; speak. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>korero:</i> conversation, conference. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  The Maori word for a conference or conversation, `a speaking' <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>korero</i>: talk, consultation. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> Maori for a tribal conference, a conversation [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> Next morning he had a long friendly korero with the Maoris, after which he started ... with his party and a local guide. <i>(1966,Richard Taylor,24</i>)

>  <i>korero ... n</i>. Maori for a tribal conference, a conversation [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> korero: a public discussion <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Korero = talk, argument <i>(1983,Bone People, The,468/283</i>)

>  in the popular sense a taonga is a highly prized possession to which is attached korero (text or story). An example is a whakapapa (genealogy) staff. <i>(1984,Te Maori,22</i>)

>  KORERO [column by Ranginui Walker, on Maori topics.] <i>(Jul 7, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  <i>korero n. NZ</i> talk or conversation; conference. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 274]]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> The fort was used for `koreros,' toy-making (tops, whips, bows and arrows, etc.) and for feasts. Food was brought from home, including herrings we had caught, cooked, and put into vinegar. But `bullies,' mushrooms, and whitebait were among the things cooked in the fort in old fruit tins on wire tripods over two or three ends of candles stuck on a board. ... (1900; Waitara) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,112</i>)

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAiPiRO - - An acursed drink ... TAiHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

>  i think he wanted a korero! <i>(Oct 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,95</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to speak, converse [ORIGIN: Maori, various early spellings]

>  The older Queens.. korero'd long and earnestly. <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 35</i>)

>  Peeti koreros with the workers and chefs responsible for feeding the masses at hui everywhere. <i>(Jun 3, 2006,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66</i>)

>  <b>COLLiNS</b> E-ko-re-roo, <i>To converse. </i> <i>(1884,English Colony in NSW,560</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Language, or to speak Ko-raro <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/331</i>)

>  He spoke amidst repeated cries of `Korrero, korrero'[pots over e's to indicate ?korraro rather than ko-|rare-ro] or, speak, speak, which were sometimes used seriously; <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 145</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Sep 26 Journal W.Wakefield in GBPP House of Commons 1841]

>  Here was a pretty dilemma! The Chiefs ran up and down and kooroorooed. <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,222,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> here i preached and koreroed with y[sic]natives <i>(Jul 18, 1843,Journal,47</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Discussion, a talk; thence, a meeting, get-together; <i>pron</i> often [koriro]. a. Talk

>  <b>HYDE</b> [i say:] `<i>Haere ra</i>, i'm going to Napier to get my divorce.' You never hear such a <i>korero</i>. They don't like a divorce. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,169</i>)

>  korero [?check this] <i>(1847,McLean Papers</i>)

>  We [Dr. Montgomery and C.W. Richmond] had a long korero about New Zealand and the Bay, where the Doctor had been quartered for 5 years. <i>(Aug 20, 1855,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/176,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  Candid comments may be made on any species of public entertainment, in which class huis, certainly, and runangas and koreros with the Governor [Grey], probably, are included. <i>(Mar 8, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/29</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>LUSH</b> ... as i called in at some of the houses and had long <i>koreros </i>with the inmates my progress was so slow ... <i>(Apr 11, 1866,Waikato Journals,81,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Rode to Pokeno, reaching Dr Weekes's ... After a little <i>korero</i> i got the carriage, harness and some `&c.' for [pounds] 12-12-0. ... <i>(Jan 8, 1868,Waikato Journals,136,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>P.S.ARDERN</b> <i>(1900,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: `possibly sub-standard']

>  After a lengthy korero their Worships said they wouldn't undertake the responsibility of dismissing the case. <i>(Apr 3, 1909,NZ Truth,198/6/3</i>)

>  But the everlasting korero about waipiro by members of the women's cant-talkers union [i.e.WCTU] is calculated to arouse curiosity that has been the leading characteristic of the sex since the days of apple-eating Eve, ... <i>(Feb 26, 1910,NZ Truth,244/4/5</i>)

>  The occasion of James Allen, M.P., unloading so much free advice to those who don't need it, and who are after Rhodes scholarships and won't get them, was when that very larned body called the University Council gathered together to have a korero recently. <i>(Jan 21, 1911,NZ Truth,291/4/6</i>)

>  <b>WHiTE</b> Remington could drop me anywhere when the `korero' [fn. `talk'] was over. <i>(1930,Mystery island,27</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> Those M.P. jokers make the entertaining korero <i>ibid.</i> 98 [glossary] <i>korero</i> - Talk, speech [? a Maori, or stage Maori us.] <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,9</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> GiLHOOLY,<i> disappearing</i>: Well, happy korero! To the both of yers! <i>(1969,Awatea,57,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNN</b> ... i conferred with Ambassador Foss Shanahan who had come up from Singapore. it was no striped pants session ... simply a casual Kiwi korero in the open air. <i>(1982,Not Only Affairs of State,116</i>)

>  <b>Hone Kouka</b> .. i am a writer, a practitioner of the theatre, so my korero will lean towards the practical ... <i>(1999,Ta Matou Mangai (Our Own Voice),9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The [Public Service Association] meeting is expected to elect Mr Sorrell who will probably have the backing of the ruling `korero', a factional organisation which supports Left-wing policies and has introduced the association to the foreign policy field. The <i>`korero"</i> recently suffered some minor defeats in voting round the country, but in expected to be still in power when the four-day meeting ends on Friday. <i>(Aug 8, 1964,Dominion,2</i>)

>  More could have been said by Roth about the Korero, the left-wing group that led the PSA from the end of the war up to the mid 1960s. it had many achievements, but some failures. Roth glides over the fact that the Korero forced General Secretary Turnbull out of office, <i>(Sep 26, 1987,Dominion,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (The chosen term for Savage Club get-togethers, and once very popular in RSA use.) Meeting; get-together; re-union.

>  it was Advocate George Toogood who proposed the idea of the fortnightly korero. <i>(Apr 20, 1907,NZ Free Lance,12/2</i>)

>  The von Haast has some mild celebrity as a scribbler of topical verse and local allusion which is useful at Savage Club koreros and students' yells. <i>(Feb 1, 1913,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  [Women's col.] Last Saturday evening the Savage Club concluded their season with a `korero'. The shivoo was given in a free and easy style. <i>(Sep 23, 1905,NZ Truth,14/4/8</i>)

>  <b>FiNDLAY</b> Spoken at a Savage Club `korero' ... <i>(1908,Humbugs and Homilies,188</i>)

>  <i>(1950,2ZB</i>) [NOTES: ca1950 Veteran's Session 2ZB Wellington often used for `meeting; get-together' eg. The monthly korero of the Tin Hat Club.]

>  The Wellington Orphans' Club held its opening korero of the 1952 season in the Savage Club Hall. <i>(May 15, 1982,Evening Post,6/7</i>)

>  Savage Cliff Milne was installed as Chief Savage at the eight korero of the Christchurch Savage Club on Saturday evening <i>(Aug 12, 1957,Christchurch Star-Sun,3</i>)

>  <b>McKEON</b> in 1923, ... i became a member of the Wellington Savage Club ... The koreros (meetings) were held, as they still are, on every second Saturday night ... from May to October. <i>(1971,Fruitful Years, The,224</i>)

### Korero House
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Parliament

>  [Hdg] At the Korero House... There is evidently some good virile stuff amongst the new members of the House. <i>(Jul 7, 1920,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

### korero Maori
 
 <i>phr.</i> speak Maori [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  if anything went wrong i could go up to his house, crack a drink, korero Maori. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,67</i>)

>  My calling was more sports but sometimes i have a problem with that because i would really like to korero Maori. <i>(Jun 1999,Metro,89</i>)

>  All i can do is korero Maori <i>(May 1998,Mana,22 / 35</i>)

### korero purakau
 
 <i>n.</i> storytelling [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Exhibitions of Ta Moko (body art), Hauora (health in massage), Taonga Puoro, (Maori music), Raranga (weaving), Korero Purakau (storytelling). <i>(Sep 27, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

### koreroing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We considered it was safest to do our koreroing with half a mile of water between. <i>(Aug 28, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  (Auckland) When the psalm-singing was ended, it was succeeded by an equally fervent kororoing (talking) <i>(1849,NZ Journal,V/153/284</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> We .. left Waipeha to do some trade `koreroing' with the natives and follow after us along with the chiefs .. <i>ibid.</i> Bk4/3/254 .. this big<i> whare</i> became occupied by all the personages of importance, male and female, irrespective of age, of the tribe, to do their smoking and gossiping <i>korero</i>-ing, not a few making it their sleeping quarters as well. <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/6/108</i>)

### koretu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Hierochloe redolens Koretu <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/127</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

### korikori
 
 <i>n.</i> the common buttercup, <i>Ranunculus insignis</i>

>  Korikori is the name of one of the commonest buttercups, <i>Ranunculus insignis</i>, found from East Cape to Kaikoura. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/49</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Korikori, <i>Lindsay. Ranunculus</i>, various sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Ranunculus pinguis Kori kori <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/124</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> RANUNCULUS iNSiGNiS (<i>Hook.F</i>.) ... The `KORiKORi'.- This fine plant is a native to the Northern island, and a portion of the Nelson district. ... often attaining a height of four feet ... it is known to the settlers as the Big Buttercup, and is probably the largest buttercup in the world. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,7</i>)

>  [Ruahines] Nesting under an inclined rock face is a bed of kori-kori, the mountain buttercup (ranunculus insignis) ... <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,99</i>)

### korimako
 
 <i>n.</i> = bellbird, makomako [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The melodious chirps of korimako jolt me into the foreground of consciousness. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/23</i>)

>  'Korimako' covers one acre of the Downs, only a short flight from the forest so its namesakes the bellbirds can flit across with ease. <i>(Apr 1992,NZ Gardener,27</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> At dawn and dusk, the eloquent song of korimako can sound like ringing bells or like flutes playing. <i>(2001,Which New Zealand Bird?,13</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|odi m|aka; Name of a certain bird. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,165</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> A Stillness, a fine sky over head! no Noise! now and then a Fish Will leap or King fisher dart down and a beautiful little bird called a `colly Mocko' who flutters about a Flower more like a Butter fly with all his feathers spread so that he looks large ... There is another small bird called a Pea Walker [<i>piwakawaka</i>]. [ed fn. suggesting Markham is describing at first the piwakawaka, fantail, and not korimako or bellbird.] <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the birds that distinguished themselves in this woodland concert, were the tui, or mocking-bird, the korimaku [a-acute], kohapiroa, and tiaki [a-acute]. The little restless piwakawaka [a2,a4-acute], a kind of thrush, who is incessantly hopping from twig to twig, put forth its single note. <i>ibid.</i> 1/299 The <i>korimaku</i> [a-acute] is about the size of a thrush, covered with dark-coloured feathers, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/108</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> i was awake before day light by the beautiful singing of the birds ... the koromoko [sic] its solitary song in the still night sounded inexpressibly sweet <i>(Nov 23, 1842,Journal,7</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 8.<i> Anthornis melanura</i>, G.R. Gray, ... [188] He-ghobarra [o-grave, a1-macron] of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound.<i> Forst</i>. Koho-i-mako of the natives.<i> Yate.</i> Korimaku of the natives of the Norther island, and Mako mako of the natives of the Southern islands. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/187</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/187-188]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Korimako or Bellbird is a light green coloured bird, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,402</i>)

>  <i>(1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/63</i>)

>  Of these, the two former (the Tui and Korimako) are the commonest birds of the country ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/8</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  Anthornis melanura. Bell-bird. Korimoko. Abundant <i>(1869,TrNZi</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 13. ANTHORNiS MELANURA. Sparm. Bell-bird. Mocker. Korimoko. Moko-moko. [?=pron `mocko'] <i>A. ruliceps</i>. PELZ. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,6</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNiS MELANURA. (BELL-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

>  it was with much pleasure that i listened here to the sweet note of the koromako[sic]<i> (Anthornis melanura). </i> <i>(1873,TrNZi,5/152</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kuimako or Kohorimako (Anthornis melanura) is a bird about the size of a thrush. <i>ibid.</i> <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/220</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> The <i>Korimako</i> [a-acute], sweetest bird/ Of all that are in forest heard? <i>ibid.</i> Korimako ..... Melliphagidae; Anthornis melanura. <i>ibid.</i> 2/338 ... the Bell-bird or Korimako ..... Anthornis Melanura. This most interesting song-bird seems in danger of extinction, owing, according to Dr. Buller, to the ravages of the Norway rat. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/263</i>)

>  i noticed the following:- Pigeon, tui, korimako, kaka, ruru, piwakawaka, toutouwai, kingfisher, duck and teal in the lakes, and pukeko in the swamp, and various sea birds. <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  12.<i>Anthornis melanura</i>, Sparrm. Bell bird, Korimako, Makomako, which last name settlers often render into mockymock. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,277</i>) [NOTES: 12.<i>Anthornis melanura</i>, Sparrm. Bell bird, Korimako, Makomako, which last name settlers often render into mockymock.]

>  12.<i>Anthornis melanura</i>, Sparrm. Bell bird, Korimako, Makomako, which last name settlers often render into mockymock. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,277</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Anthornis melanura</i>. - Korimako. Both islands, and Auckland islands. Rare in many parts. <i>ibid.</i> 18/113 (Martin) <i>Anthornis melanocephala.</i> - Korimako. Chatham islands. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Korimako, n.</i> Maori name for the <i>Bell-Bird</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,253</i>)

>  A beautiful bush-musician in the South island ... is the bell-bird (<i>korimako</i> or <i>makomako</i>). <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/339</i>)

>  <i>korimako</i>: bell bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,17</i>)

### koro
 
 <i>n.</i> old man, grandfather

>  Dearly loved and missed always Dad and Koro. Love you, Martin whanau. <i>(Jan 27, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,50</i>)

>  in the film, Pai tries to prove her stiff-necked grandfather (koro) and Maori chief she deserves to be his successor, even though she is a girl, unlike her twin brother who died at birth... She said she liked the film, especially the final scene when a revitalised community paddles a waka out to sea with Pai and her koro on board. <i>(Mar 14, 2002,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

### korohea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> TURNAGRA HECTORi. (NORTH iSLAND THRUSH.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Piopio, \{Koropio,\} Korohea, and Tiutiukata. <i>ibid.</i> 136 A specimen [of the North-island Thrush] which i shot in the Kaipara district in the summer of 1852 ... was quite a novelty to the natives in that part of the country; it was recognized, however, by an old Maori who called it a `Korohea', a name quite unknown in the south, and who stated that in former years it was very abundant in all the woods. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,135,{1888 2nd edn 1/26, 1/28} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Korohea (<i>Turnagra Hectori</i>) is the native thrush, and a poor imitation it is of the English throstle. it is scarce. Sometimes its song may be listened to with pleasure. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Korohea (<i>Turnagra Hectori</i>) is the native thrush, and a poor imitation it is of the English throstle. it is scarce. Sometimes its song may be listened to with pleasure. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

### koroi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kor|oi; Fruit so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,168</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>korai, koutu utu, miro</i>, and <i>putuhutu</i>, are wild forest fruits, prized only by the elder natives. The <i>tu pakihi</i> (Coriaria sarmentosa), or elder-berry, is a very pleasing fruit. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/288</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> The kahikatea bears very numerous seeds in bunches: the fruit is double; ... both parts are of the size of pepper-corns. According to the natives, these berries, which they call koroi, ripen only every third year. <i>(1843,TrNZi,1/318</i>)

>  Our people collected great quantities of `Koroi' the berry of the Kahikatea a very paletable [sic] fruit. <i>(May 4, 1843,Journal of Ensign Best,302,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Koroi, <i>Col[enso]</i>. Fruit of <i>Podocarpus dacrydioides. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  ... the Kahikatea ..., - the fruit of which was called Koroi ... and also the fruit (Ureure) and sugary bract-like spadices (Tawhara) of the climbing plant (Kiekie). <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> The fruit is set upon a curious red berry, eaten by the Maoris. This berry is not the actual fruit but is formed from the scales which bear the fruit.] <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,75</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The Rimu and the koroi pine, seventy or eighty feet in height ... <i>(1850,Savage Life,2/19</i>)

>  <b>WHiTE</b> The koroi is a white pine which bears fruits plentifully only once in 7 years. <i>(1874,Te Rou,154</i>)

### korokoro-pounamu
 
 <i>n.</i> bluefish

>  2. <i>Mokohinou islands</i>. - Hapuku (or groper), hiwihiwi, barracouta, maomao, spotty, parrot-fish, leather jacket, korokoro-pounamou. <i>ibid.</i> 24/215 Again, from Mokohinou Mr. Sandager records, in September, 1888, the capture of a fish which he calls `korokoro-pounamou.' The specimen weighed 6lb., and contained Medusae in the stomach. i do not know what fish is referred to. <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  GiRELLA CYANEA Macleay. <i>Blue-fish; Korokoropounamu.</i> A rare and highly esteemed fish, secured up to 6lb. in deep water off Cape Brett and headlands round Hauraki Gulf. ... Distribution: Unknown south of Hauraki Gulf. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/116</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Girella cyanea </i>Macleay. BLUEFiSH; KOROKOROPOUNAMU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,36,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE BLUEFiSH <i>iredalella cyanea (Macleay) </i>... Maori name KOROKOROPOUNAMU ... This beautiful fish has been recorded from the Hauraki Gulf, but is seldom seen south of Whangarei. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,106</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLUEFiSH ... <i>Other names: Girella cyanea</i>; korokoropounamu (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,243</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BLUEFiSH [<i>Girella cyanea</i>] ... The Maori name korokoropounamu is still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,34</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> BLUEFiSH <i>Girella cyanea</i>: A pretty, violet-blue fish with gold flecks the Bluefish inhabits our more northern waters usually around off-shore islands and rocky headlands. it is a close relative of the Australian drummer and luderick. The Maori name of "korokoropounamu' is still heard in some remote areas. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,34</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>BLUEFiSH </i>(Blue drummer, korokoropounamu)<i> Girella cyanea</i> ... As the name suggests their body colour is a uniform bright blue and all the fins are a similar colour. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,229,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] bluefish .. [scientific name] <i>Girella cyanea</i> .. [other name(s)] koro koro pounamu <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### koromiko
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Hebe</i> spp, esp. <i>H salicifolia </i>(formerly <i>Veronica</i>), a large group of shrubs and small trees, found throughout NZ [ORIGIN: Maori, various early spellings]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Koromiko were growing with oleanders in tubs, the blue and purple flowers startling among the pink and white sub-tropical blossoms. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,98</i>)

>  [Advt] Heilbron's German Worm Cakes and Fitzgerald's Koromiko Extract are Patented. <i>(Feb 28, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Koromiko, n</i>. a white flowering arborescent Veronica of New Zealand, <i>Veronica salicifolia</i>, Forst., <i>N.O. Scrophularineae. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,253</i>)

>  <b>NEiL</b> KORAMiKA (NATiVE VERONiCA). ... it is found growing in almost every native bush. <i>(1898,Family Herb Doctor,28</i>)

>  ...we see but few species of the characteristic New Zealand daisy-trees [fn Species of <i>Olearia</i>.] or of the bushy Veronicas (koromiko), which are so conspicuous in other parts of these islands. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Sometimes invalids were taken to a stream by the priest who sprinkled the sufferer ... with a sprig of veronica (<i>koromiko</i>). <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,21</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Veronica salicifolia, Forst.] Koromiko.</i> ... The most widely distributed of the New Zealand species, and one of the most variable. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,504</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... the koromiko [on the Chatham islands] is not <i>Veronica salicifolia</i> - it is <i>V. gigantea; ... </i> <i>(1910,NZ Plants,120</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The flowering <i>koromiko</i> (veronica) and the turpentine plant - the latter invaluable to the camper-out because it burns well even when green - are two of the most plentiful shrubs. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,NZ (Vol ii South island) 66</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> The limestone range east of the lake [Tutira] at one time grew ... dense fern, high tutu, koromiko, and a considerable area of "whitey-wood' bush, kowhai, fuchsia, rama rama, ngaio, kaiwhiria, etc., etc., ... . <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,2</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> in this bush were ... Ohau, (panax or five-finger), ini-ini (whitey wood); konini, white and red matipo; koromiko and other shrubs. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,246,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> (<i>Hebe</i> from the Greek, the cup-bearer of the gods). Maori name, <i>Koromiko.</i> The evergreen, shrubby habit, in addition to other botanical characters, as well as the distribution, separate the genus <i>Hebe</i> from <i>Veronica. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 403 <i>Hebe salicifolia (The Willow-leaved Hebe).</i> A large shrub;... Maori names, [so also 1907 edition] <i>Kokoromiko, Koromiko. (Veronica salicifolia). </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,393</i>)

>  <i>koromiko</i>, a small tree (Veronica sp.), many species of variable round-bushed plant or small tree. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Hebe ... Dieffenbachii </i>... KOROMiKO (curly fingers). <i>(1952,Chatham islands,58</i>)

>  The Maori name of a New Zealand shrub, Veronica, used by the Maoris as medicine. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  The Maori name of a New Zealand shrub, Veronica, used by the Maoris as medicine. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  <b>ALLAN</b> The name <i>koromiko (korokio </i>in the Far North) is commonly used for the larger willow - lvd [Hebe] spp. in the high country the name `boxwood' us. refers to some small-lvd <i> Hebe. </i> <i>(1961,Flora NZ,1/887,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <i>koromiko</i> also <i>koromika n</i> -s [Maori] 1 : any of several shrubs of the genus <i>Veronica</i> (esp. <i>V. salicifolia</i> and </i>V. parviflora</i>) 2 : a drug obtained from a koromiko plant <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1255</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The whole [Waikaka] valley and the ... ridges were covered [in 1876] with luxurious white tussocks (Poa caespitosa) diversified with a few snow grass tussocks (Danthonia raoulii), trees and shrubs comprising Maukoko, or native broom (Carmichaelia australia[?lis]), Koromiko (Veronica salicifo), daisy tree (Aleria rirgata [?virgata]), cottonwood (Cassinia fulvida), ... native thorns or matagouri and mingimingi (Coprosma propinqua). [check] <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,239</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> The koromiko has a medicinal quality ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,24</i>)

>  <b>TUWARE</b> (`That Morning Early') bearing leaves of koromiko/ and black cauldron aswirl/ with the mangled roots of flax or lawyer vine/ <i>(1964,No Ordinary Sun,10,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <i>koromiko n</i>. white-flowering New Zealand shrub; variety of veronica [Maori] <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The vocabulary of New Zealand English has been influenced by ... the adoption of Maori words and phrases. ... Examples of the native words now in general use are the names of birds, fish, plants, and trees: <i>kea, tui, hapuku, tarakihi, koromiko, raupo, and kauri. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 3/705-12; Koromiko .. .. koromiko .. .. <i>Hebe salicifolia </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <i>koromiko ... n.</i> white-flowering N.Z. shrub; variety of veronica [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <i>koromiko [s] Hebe salicifolia</i> (Forst.f.) Penn. <i>Hebe stricta</i> (Benth.) L.B. Moore <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Hebe salicifolia</i> KOROMiKO <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,68</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 167 Koromiko = useful tree if you've got a crook stomach or diarrhea [sic] <i>(1983,Bone People, The,466</i>)

>  <b>HOLMES</b> <i>Hebe Barkeri</i> (Koromiko) - a stout branching shrub with pale leaves and small lilac flowers found mainly in forest areas on Chatham. <i>(1984,Chatham islands,94</i>)

>  <i>koromiko n.</i> = HEBE. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And.) <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: a number of Veronica spp. listed]

>  (And) <i>(1879,NZ Country Journal</i>)

>  Koromiko or hebe has long been valued for beneficial effects on diarrhoea and dysentery and was widely used by Maori and Pakeha. New Zealand troops in North Africa during World War ii found it effective for dysentery. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  The planting programme has wound down now that the pioneer plants such as ngaio, taupata and korimiko are established. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 1</i>)

>  He turned to the Maoris for some of their medical lore and remedies, Koromiko for internal troubles. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> Although koromiko produces very little wood, it was noted by early colonists for its toughness and elasticity and for the fact that it gives off a lot of heat when burned. <i>(2009,Which Native Forest Plant?,51</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> ... alluvial soil, clothed with koromiko and tutu trees, fresh with vivid green of their spring shoots. <i>ibid.</i> 81 As we descended, the bed of the river began to expand into wider spaces ... clothed with Koromiko and Tutu trees ... <i>(Dec 10, 1842,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Dec 10 Selwyn Jrnl in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 78]

>  [Cby settler] Planting some koromiko behind my house <i>(1851,Australian & NZ Gazette,499,1852 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,454</i>)

>  <b>BRACKEN</b> <i>(1884,Lays of the Maori</i>)

>  <b>W.H.S. ROBERTS</b> Writing of the Waimea Plain in 1856: - `The ground was well grassed, but the speargrass was too plentiful. The pasturage was very good. There was a good deal of scrub in the bends of the Mataura known as `wire plant' (Olearia virgata). There were also manuka and koromiko. Maori-heads, a kind of grass tree about three feet high, abounded in the swamps, and in some we saw toitoi grass and raupo. We did not pass any cabbage trees to-day ... we proceeded slowly through tomatakuru scrub, speargrass, Spaniard, snowgrass, and other native grasses ... we camped in a bend with good feed at the Elbow and a swamp on one side. Maori cabbage abounded ...' <i>(1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 W.H.S. ROBERTS Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 18]

>  <b>SMiTH</b> ... one we saw was a little purple bulb ..., and a great variety of koromikos. <i>(Dec 12, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Feb 12 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 383]

>  <b>BEETHAM</b> ... the third pig was a good sow which we steamed with green koromiko in first rate style ... <i>(Oct 15, 1858,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Oct 15 William Beetham Diary in 1967 Rickard Strangers in the Wilderness 99]

>  <b>MOSER</b> [T]he graceful ko-whai (Edwadsia[sic] Microphylla) droops over the banks of a small creek ..., intermingled with the Koro-miko (Veronica) in full flower, and the fresh leaved Mahoe (Melicytus ramiflorus). <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,5</i>)

>  [i] got some Coramicos a nice little shrub. There are innumerable varieties. <i>(Aug 1, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,97,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> i can give you no idea of the variety among the shrubs: the koromika, like an Alpine rose, a compact ball of foliage; the lancewood, a tall, slender stem, ... with a few long leaves at the top, turned downwards like the barb of a spear, and looking exactly like a lance stuck into the ground; the varieties of matapo ... each leaf a study, with its beautiful tracery of black veins on a yellow-green ground; the mappo, the gohi, and many others ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,94,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Koro-miko, Bidwill. Veronica parviflora... Koromiko, <i>Geolog. Surv. Veronica elliptica. </i>... Korumeek<i> Hector. Veronica salicifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> <i>V[eronica] Salicifolia</i>, Forst. ... in allusion to its frequent habit and habitat - on the banks of streams - it is locally known as the New Zealand `Willow', (Buchanan.) The Otago settlers also call it `Korimuka', (Martin,) - `Korumeek,' (Hector,) - properly spelled `Koromiko' - a term also applied to <i>V. parviflora</i>, Vahl. ... and perhaps in general, to all <i>shrubby</i>species of the genus. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,68</i>)

>  VERONiCA FAMiLY. Koromiko. One of the largest in Otago, and forming a prominent feature in the New Zealand Flora. <i>ibid.</i> 47 Koromiko. Veronica salicifolia, Hook. f... Koromiko. Veronica elliptica, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/35</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>WAiTE</b> A shrub called `coarimika' [is a cure for diarrhoea.] <i>(1869,Discovery West Coast Goldfields,25</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> We had found the whole of the ranges above almost bare of bush, patches of the manuka and coromica alone affording shelter and firewood for a camp, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,77</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> NOTE. - As i have no desire to mystify readers unacquainted with New Zealand flora, i append a brief description of the shrubs mentioned:.. <i>Koromiko </i>- A white flowering <i>Veronica...</i> <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iii/16-17,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOGG</b> The meek manuka and the korimiko,/ Adorn the rugged banks of Motupiko. <i>(1875,Lays and Rhymes,43</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> The narrowed gully - just a ditch/ With flowering <i>koromiko</i> rich -/ [fn] Veronica. <i>ibid.</i> 1/301 Koromiko .. .. O. Scrophularinae; G. Veronica. Dr. Hooker describes forty species ... <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/122</i>)

>  <b>BODELL</b> ... i had got some of the Native recipes ... and by taking a concoction of Koromiko this shrub grows plentifully ... and is a sure cure for dysentery. <i>(1890,Soldier's View of Empire, A,153,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CARRiCK</b> Then, there is a species of the koromiko (<i>Veronica elliptica</i>) interspersed in considerable quantities throughout the Campbell as well as the Auckland [island] bushes. <i>(1892,New Zealand's Lone Lands,23</i>)

>  <i>Ko&#769;.ro-mi&#769;.ko</i> (k&omacr.&#769;.ro.-m&emacr.&ko.; colloq. k&obreve.r&#769;.|o-m&emacr.&#769;.k|o), n. [Maori.] [Written also <i>koromika.] (Bot.)</i> A New Zealand scrophulariaceous tree <i>(Veronica salicifolia). [Local, New Zealand] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2025</i>)

### Korotangi
 
 <i>n.</i> a carved stone bird figure revered in Maoridom [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  A romantic mystery envelops that priceless link with the origin of the ancient Maori - Te Korotangi, the Polynesian interpretation of which means "The Crying Dove". <i>(Nov 14, 1931,Aussie (NZ Section),66</i>)

>  There are passages that will offend indigenous sensibilities. One is the suggestion that Tainui Maori's most valued taonga, the korotangi bird carved in serpentine, may be no more than "as banal an object as an italian garden ornament brought to New Zealand in 19th-century colonial baggage." <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  The origins of the Korotangi, a stone bird carved of dark green serpentine, are shrouded by controversy and ignorance. Some descendants of Tainui claim that the Korotangi came from Hawaiki on their canoe. <i>(Dec 1961,Te Ao Hou,37: 22</i>)

>  Korotangi is made of a kind of dark green serpentine... it somewhat resembles a prion; but the nostrils are those of a duck. <i>(May 20, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

>  The Korotangi is the name given to a stone bird said by the Maoris to have been brought from Hawaiki by them in their canoe Tainui. <i>(1890,TrNZi,22/499</i>)

>  <i>The mystery of the `Korotangi'.</i> [title] Korotangi is the name given by the Maoris to a curiously-carved stone bird ... <i>(Sep 1904,NZ illustrated Magazine,442</i>)

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> Traditions lead only these few centuries back, but the holy bird, Korotangi, about which learned argument was raging when i left New Zealand, may perhaps prove a link with a still more distant past. The Korotangi is the figure of a small sea-gull carved out of dark green serpentine. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,198,1933 ed.</i>)

>  Recognised as one of the most famous of the Maori historical relics found in New Zealand, because of the tradition woven around it, the korotangi, a carved stone bird highly valued by the Maoris, has passed into the keeping of the Dominion Museum. <i>(Oct 13, 1941,NZEF Times,i/15 : 11</i>)

### korou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> A long lane was formed on an extensive level plain, fifty feet in width, by two high walls, formed by perhaps upwards of ten thousand baskets of potatoes, kumeras, indian corn (<i>Holcus indicus</i>), tarro (<i>arum</i>), water melons, turnips preserved, <i>kou</i>) steamed kernels of the native fruit, (<i>karaka</i>[as-acute]) baked roots of the<i> ti</i> palm tree, (<i>korou</i>) a quantity of dried cod, eels and other fish, also of shell-fish, bundles of fern-root, onions, peaches, grapes, &c. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/91</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [korau](../K#korau))

### koroua
 
 <i>n.</i> an elderly Maori man; a grandfather [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Uncle's coming home from overeas. ... Mum rang our kuia Wai and our koroua Kupu. They will sort out the whaikorero and waiata. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,249</i>)

>  Having lost the battle, Whatarangi was musing on the koroua he'd got to know so well, and had so much regard for. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 11</i>)

>  TOKO-JONES Stuart Temai 1990-2006 ... A great big thank you to out kai karanga, our koroua on the Pae Pae, na manu Tioriori, and our kaimahi who worked tirelessly at the back. <i>(Jun 16, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,22</i>)

>  We would like to express our deepest appreciation to the relatives, friends who supported us in the loss of our father, koroua, husband, father-in-law, brother, cousin on November 7, 2005. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,46</i>)

### korowai
 
 <i>n.</i> cloak ornamented with black twisted thrums

>  So, too, the korowai - " a fine cloak with black cord tags" as Prendergast and other writers describe it. May i put in a plea for this description: a fine cloak with fringe adornments. <i>(Oct 24, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Blankets worn by Maori were resilient and strong, whereas korowai were worn for special occasions, she says. "For me, blankets were the poor man's korowai." <i>(Jul 5, 2001,City Voice,10</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> When the Maoris wore blankets, or <i>korowais</i>, they could be as amphibious as they liked without harm. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,323</i>)

>  From korowai through to wearable art, the designers will link the past with the future. <i>(Jun 2006,Tumai,76/27</i>)

>  <b>WADMAN (ed).</b> in a portrait of Te Papatahi [by Goldie] shown at the Royal Academy an admirer describes every thrum and weave of the korowai as being delineated with loving care. <i>(1947,Yearbook of Arts in NZ,3/12</i>)

>  Taonga Tete Kura, an exhibition of third year art students' raranga flax-weaving, used traditional techniques and materials fused with modern materials and creativity. The result was a display of everything from wearable art to traditional kete, tuku-tuku and korowai. <i>(Nov 22, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|Oro ai; A certain garment. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,168</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> The <i>korowai</i> and <i>tatata</i> are two garments nearly alike in texture; they both have a number of loose strings hanging outside, which gives them a neat and comfortable appearance. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),158</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... their dresses consisted of the kaitaka and karowai, made of silky flax, and covered with dog-skin mats, or <i>neris</i> [sic: e-acute] made of rushes, as protection against rain. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/158</i>)

>  <b>E.J. WAKEFiELD</b> ... a black string mat or korowai <i>(Mar 27, 1840,Diary</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The smallest garment woven is called the <i>patai</i>[a1-acute], principally worn by females for an inner garment; the <i>korowai</i> differs but little from the former, except in having a deep fringe of knotted flax strings. The <i>tatata</i> [as-acute] is similar in size to the former, having pending from every part, an infinity of small cords six inches long, of no use or ornament; its origin must be ascribed to the arbitrary taste of fashion, <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/177</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The mat most generally worn is the black-string mat, called <i>e koroai</i>, a flax dress thickly ornamented with black strings, ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/324</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The mat most generally worn is the black-string mat called `e koroai,' a flax dress thickly ornamented with black strings or filaments, about a foot long, which have a very graceful appearance as they hang over the folds of the drapery. <i>(1866,Polynesia,156</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> The principal sorts of cloak or mantle at present made by the Maoris from their native flax are - 1. The `Korowai,' or shaggy mantle. 2. The `Kaitaka,' or fine mantle; and 3. The `Pokeha,' or rain cloak. Of these, the variety of flax plant called the `Koura' yields the best fibre for the `Korowai'. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,78</i>)

>  <i>(1901,NZ Short Stories,10</i>) [NOTES: 1901 Grace Tales in NZ Short Stories 10]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The <i>korowai</i> was of fine flax beaten out with a club to make it soft. it was generally worn by women and girls. The white ground was thickly covered with black thrums or hanging tags (<i>tahuka</i>) of twisted flax. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,234</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,90</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> She was dressed only in a beautiful <i>korowai</i> , a white cloak of fine dressed flax. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/13,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> <i>(1926,Tutira,92</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,89</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,10</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Apart from greenstone heirlooms, etc., the most valuable gifts that can be bestowed include the dogskin cloak, the <i>korowai</i> cloak with black edges, and the kiwi feather cloak. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,15</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> korowai: a light traditional cloak worn by high-ranking men on ceremonial occasions. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)
