### koru
 
 <i>n.</i> a (carved) spiral reflecting an unwinding fern frond [(OED2) A common motif in Maori carving and tattooing, consisting of a spiral pattern terminating in a bulb. 1938 W. J. PHILLIPPS in Art in N.Z. X. 205 The koru, or pikopiko, which today rarely appears in carving, but is used in the construction of composite patterns for rafters. 1946 in Dominion Mus. (N.Z.) Rec. Ethnol. I. 16 In tattoo it was also customary to incise a koru type of design on the centre of the forehead. Ibid. 21 Two pairs of koru, large and small. 1964 T. BARROW Decorative Arts N.Z. Maori iv. 64 Painted patterns appear on the underside of rafters in ceremonial meeting-houses and superior dwellings. Their elaborate curvilinear designs are usually based on a small bulb-like motif (koru) shaped like the looped top of an uncurling fern frond. 1970 Dominion Mus. (N.Z.) Rec. Ethnol. II. 31 An example of a carved koru design in its simplest form. Ibid. 34 The koru motifs (the curved stalk-like forms that terminate in a bulb).]

>  There's no stage as such either - the stage is the big koru, the life spiral painted on the floor. <i>(Nov 22, 1998,Sunday Star Times,F8</i>)

>  Moss Asher-Patterson sets Koru, a flowing dance inspired by kowhaiwhai patterns, to the surge of Telemann's viola concerto. <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  He.. proposes a completely new design of three vertical bands of blue, white, blue, with the blue Air New Zealand koru on the white. <i>(Mar 24, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  "i think the Air New Zealand koru, for instance, is a very successful piece of design," says Mousdale. <i>(Dec 3, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Company member Moana Nepia made his "Koru" in 1984 as one of the works in the New Moves season, and has re-worked it here. inspired by the repeated spiral motifs of Maori art this dance makes use of spiralling movement, patterns and paths to sculpt the space. <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  it is an unnerving thing to witness - you wonder what you'd do if this 1.9m bald guy in a koru shirt was to spit out a mouthful of broken teeth... <i>(Mar 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  Architects have designed a church based on the spiral and the spirit of the koru. <i>(Oct 19, 2003,NZ Catholic,2</i>)

>  "The koru is a stylised adaptation of a fern frond, symbolising new growth and rebirth."... The koru is the image of the "springing fern" and is seen in the art of Maori and Polynesians and, according to <i>New Zealand Traditions & Folklore</i> by Gordon Ell, has become a traditional symbol of New Zealandness in recent years, a lead that probably came from Air New Zealand. <i>(May 5, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  The description.. fails to mention.. the striking koru pattern she [sc. Jennings] used to decorate the waistline. <i>(Nov 1988,Pacific Way,47</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLPPS</b> [? OEDS] [?=KORURU] <i>(1955,Maori Carving,12</i>)

>  McLEAVEY said `Gordon Walters invented the koru'. He knows and i know that the koru has its origin in ancient Maori folk art; Walters only discovered it in the 1940s. if popular usage annexes new words to a language, we will allow Walters the distinction of being the inventor of the koru in the language of contemporary New Zealand art. <i>(Nov 26, 1981,Dominion,17/1</i>)

>  <i>koru n. NZ</i> stylized fern-leaf motif used in Maori carving and tattooing. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 274]]

>  An unfurling fern frond, the koru, is artist Friedensreich Hundertwasser's chosen symbol for a New Zealand flag... Yes, his green koru flag.. does indeed start at the black flagstaff head and then decreases sharply to where the flag is divided into two diagonal halves before curling up into a spiral at the other end. <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Colensoa physaloides</i>, a plant.

>  Maori know this shrub variably as koru, roru or oru and some people call it the New Zealand hydranger. <i>(Dec 11, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kod|u, s. A shrub so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,165</i>)

>  <b>TUAPEKA</b> Here [at Taeri] was the tall and graceful black pine; the manukau, a shrub like the [Victorian] tea-tree scrub, only more greenly leaved; a few broad leaved grass-trees; the mappo, the kolu, the red pine, and others too numerous for me to remember; but their leaves were ... delightful to one accustomed to the dingy foliage of Victorian forests. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Colensoa physaloides (The Physalis-like Colensoa)... (Physalis</i> is the Cape Gooseberry). One of our few distinctly blue flowers, Maori name <i>Koru. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,434</i>)

>  <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,290/1</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a symbol for Air New Zealand

>  "The other rib.. described a clear arc from earth to air, tipped by an unfolding koru, breaking through the remains of a.. circle reminiscent of a goal. The logo does more than use part of the plant to represent sport. in a sport-loving country it celebrates energy." Phew, and we thought it was just a shark eating a koru by moonlight! <i>(Apr 22, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  When Tasman Empire Airways Limited (TEAL) became Air New Zealand in 1965, Roundhill came up with the koru, which was reduced to a stylised form by his studio team. <i>(Apr 16, 2004,Dominion Post,B12</i>)

>  One of the most famous and lasting of [Bernard Roundill's] images is one of the most simple: the Air New Zealand koru. <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  The koru is one of the few things outside sport that excites patriotism. <i>(Dec 7, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  "Amidst the welter of figures and finger pointing," declared reporter this side of the ditch, Mark Crysell, "one clear and awful truth; the koru, a once proud national symbol, has gone into virtual meltdown!" <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

>  But the alternative - a ruinous hiatus of no planes flying in this country while some overseas business baron deigns to scavenge the Koru carcass - is surely unthinkable. <i>(Sep 29, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  The control carrier, aka the Cabinet, has a few words with the national koru-carrier, most of them unprintable. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  This won't look good to staff facing redundancy but, heck, who does it hurt if a spare seat and a little chardonnay keep on board someone whose skills might prevent another koru-tastrophe? <i>(Oct 27, 2001,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  There's a good argument that her suggestion that small shareholders hang onto their sagging koru stock has cost the taxpayer a packet. <i>(Oct 13, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### Koru Club
 
 <i>n.</i> an Air New Zealand club with pre-flight lounges for business persons and others paying an annual subscription

>  Many of these people have little real connection with the real world because they live in five-star hotels and they swing around from Koru to the Golden Wing. <i>(Dec 1996,North & South,98</i>)

>  "You compare notes with Mike Moore in the Koru Club and he speaks for 20 minutes without using a single verb." <i>(Jan 18, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  [Hdg] Staff lose Koru Club travel perks. <i>(Mar 7, 2001,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The Koru Club and Qantas lounges: airport members' lounges have revolutionised modern politics more, perhaps, than MMP. <i>(Apr 14, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  Men are still predominant in the New Zealand professions and in business. if you doubt it, check out the gender balance in the Koru Club. <i>(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  This new furniture is in all the ViP rooms in the Air New Zealand's Pacific network, as well as in the new Koru Club rooms in Wellington, Auckland and Christchurch... Air New Zealand Koru Club, their domestic business class - annual subscription $140. <i>(Jul 1987,Metro,132</i>)

>  Facilities include a self-service bar and a coffee and snack table offering light refreshments similar to the airline's domestic Koru Clubs. <i>(Mar 1991,Pacific Way,76</i>)

>  Waiting around for my connecting flight to good old Hawke's Bay in the Koru Club it was good to catch up with home. <i>(Aug 11, 1992,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Waiting in the Koru Club for my flight home to Auckland, i pick up a copy of the <i>Evening Post</i>. There, on the front page, is a huge, three column, full-colour photograph of Rankin arriving at court... And then! i look up from my newspaper and there she is in the flesh, striding through the Koru Club. <i>(Jun 16, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  if your lifestyle includes everything from the Koru Club to Viagra, you are definitely going to need more [of a pension]. <i>(Apr 26, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### Koru Lounge
 
 <i>n.</i> an Air NZ airport lounge for members of the Koru Club

>  Phillida Bunkle says "i live in the Koru Lounge, actually"... To quote Air New Zealand material regarding the Koru Lounge, this area is designed for "the frequent traveller to enjoy a world of special privileges." <i>(Dec 31, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  Senior MPs in other parties have made an unofficial pact to audit Hide's life and times, down to the last free club sandwich at the Koru Lounge. <i>(Apr 5, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  if you are a diesel mechanic in Gore, it is of little comfort to know that you can now buy an excellent latte in Taihape, that the Koru Lounge and Golden Wing Club are the finest of their kind in the world. <i>(Jun 1, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  A funny thing happened to me last night in the Koru Lounge at the airport, says Anderton. <i>(Nov 27, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  <b>36</b> But what Cull woulkd really like to see is "when you're in the Koru lounge and some prat comes out of the toilets without washing his hands and heads straight for the food and fingers it, that a big flashing light shines over the door of the washroom, just like in the Far Side cartoon: 'Didn't wash his hands!'" <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  A succession of party presidents turned out to be inept embarrassments starting with Sue Wood in the mid-1980s.. and including the postings of John Collinge to London, Geoff Thompson to Coventry and John Slater to the wilds of the Koru lounge. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  ...there i was after Corngate, in the Koru Lounge, and he came up to me and shook my hand and said, 'Bloody good work! Keep it up!'" <i>(Feb 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  Wellington's Koru lounge is soon to be completely refurbished to feature a 90's-style decor in Air New Zealand National's corporate colours of blue and teal. <i>(Dec 1991,Pacific Way,87</i>)

>  Ugly scenes ruptured breakfast in the Koru Lounge at Wellington Airport on Wednesday. <i>(Aug 7, 1992,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Bleary eyed at 6.30am in the Koru Lounge i was appalled to hear speakers pouring forth carols in vile arrangements so loudly that it was impossible to taste the fruit juice or read the newspaper... i have.. taken the scissors to my Koru Club membership card. <i>(Feb 1992,Metro,131</i>)

>  "Funny, i was in an airport, some Koru lounge somewhere, and i was doing some writing. This bloke blundered straight into my face and said, "Gidday Sam, how are you?" <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Dominion Post,F3</i>)

### koruru
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A carved face (<i>koruru</i>) was placed at the junction of the barge-boards, and above this was an image (<i>tekoteko</i>) which, especially on state occasions, was decked with feathers and ornaments. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,273</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> HE KORURU [title] ... According to Williams, <i>koruru</i> is a figure placed on the gable of a house. Elsdon Best preferrred to use the term to apply to a single carved head only. <i>(1941,Maori Carving,394</i>)

>  <i>koruru</i>, carved head covering gable-join of house; <i>see</i> tekoteko. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: ANDERSEN]

>  <b>BUCK</b> A carved head(<i>koruru</i>) was placed over the vertical join of the two barge-boards forming th gable apex. ... Sometimes a complete human figure (<i>tekoteko</i>) was used instead of a <i>koruru</i> type ... <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,129</i>)

>  ... the Maori Meeting House that stood under the silent pines in the Recreation Grounds. ... the face of the Koruru figure on the ridge-pole of the Meeting House, staring blindly at its past. <i>(Nov 1979,islands,7/5/503</i>) [NOTES: Yvonne Du Fresne A Little Talk about our (Winter) District]

### Kotahitanga
 
 <i>n.</i> unity [ORIGIN: Maori]

[Glossary, 119 kotare kingfisher <i>=(<i>Halcyon sanctus</i>); Lesson made the first collection in the Bay of islandsin 1824.] <i>(1844,NZ Journal,115,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ARDEA SYRMATOPHORA \{EGRETTA\}. (WHiTE HERON.) ...<i>Native name,</i> - Kotuku; `White Crane' of the Colonists. [227] The White Heron occurs so sparingly in most parts of New Zealand, that `rare as the Kotuku' has passed into a proverb among the Maoris; <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,226,{1888 2nd edn 2/124} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ARDEA SYRMATOPHORA \{EGRETTA\}. (WHiTE HERON.) ...<i>Native name,</i> - Kotuku; `White Crane' of the Colonists. ### kotata
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPHENOEACUS PUNCTATUS. (\{COMMON UTiCK \{FERN-BiRD\}.) ... <i>Maori names</i>. Mata, Matata, Kotata, Nako, and Koroatito. ... it frequents the dense fern (<i>Pteris aquilina</i>) of the open country, ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,128,{1888 2nd edn 1/59} ed.</i>)

>  SPHENAECUS. - Swamp-bird, two species (<i>Kotata</i>). <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/102</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

### kotero
 
 <i>n.</i> preserved potato; small potatoes

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> each [Maori] having a kit of potatoes and taro, a large quantity of the kotiro, or preserved potato, ... <i>(1847,Great Journey,54,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> Small potatoes, termed `kotero', and the berries of the karaka tree, (<i>corynocarpus laevigata</i>), are both ... considered great delicacies. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Title] Kumara kotero (cured kumara) ... kotero may be fried or, as in the traditional recipe, cooked in hot embers. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,4</i>)

### kotihe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kot|ie; Name of a certain bird. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Kotihe</i> - This bird is about the size of the gold-finch; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),63</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> <i>7. Ptilotis cincta. ... </i>Kotihe of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. ihi of the natives of Taranaki. - <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/187</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/187]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The Kotihe is a beautiful honey-bird about the size of a bullfinch. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/24</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> POGNORNiS CiNCTA (STiTCH-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. ihi [omitted 1888], Hihi, Tihe, Kotihe, Kotihewera, \{Tiora, and Tiheora\}; male and female are someties distinguished as Hihi-paka and Hihi-matakiore or Tihe-kiore. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,{1888 2nd edn 1/101}</i>)

### kotiro
 
 <i>n.</i> a girl [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Thanks to .. Jason, Mother's Whangai Kotiro, those who sent flowers ... <i>(May 12, 2006,NZ Herald,C9</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> A girl Ko-teedo <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/333</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kot|iro, s. A young girl. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Pomarre[acute] .. Rungatyeeda no te Kaaingha. E Wharre[acute] .. Whaheene[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. Poaka .. Tamyte[acute] no Pomarre[acute] .. E Po .. Koeteedo no Pomarre Whaheene no Tenana. <i>(1820,Journal,27,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> My friend Harry .. had fraternized with a bevy of Kotiros (or young girls) with whom he was flirting prodigiously ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,34</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> .. the family having been established in the days when the white man came alone into the native settlement and picked his wife from the bright-eyed kotiros of the hapu. [fn. Maidens of the tribe.] The founder of the family slept in the graveyard, beneath the manoa trees on the summit of the hill, and his grave vibrated .. to the tread of the ocean rollers on the bar. [ed. fn. Correctly, manoao, a podocarp tree also known as Barrier pine.] <i>ibid.</i> 71 The Mallons .. belonged to the earlier pioneers, the family having been established in the days when the white man came alone into the native settlement and picked his wife from the bright-eyed kotiros of the hapu. <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,41,1985 ed.</i>)

>  Caught in the Act. Maori Tangata and Pakeka Kotero At Tauranga Police court .. a Maori and a white girl were charged ... <i>(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65-6-3</i>)

>  When you look at all that golf champion Larissa Eruera has achieved, it's hard to believe that she is a kotiro of only 14 years of age .. And this talented kotiro has, without a doubt, got the drive and passion to fulfil her aspirations and turn her dreams into reality. <i>(Jun 2006,Te Mana  Taiohi,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### kotuku
 
 <i>n.</i> the white heron, white crane

>  A lake so calm it appeared as a solid mirror to the mountains and the graceful, full-winged flight of a kotuku. <i>(Oct 1999,North & South,30</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> The other kotuku are arriving back, elegantly aloof in nuptial plumes. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,108</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> The white heron, kotuku, is not a rare bird on a world-wide scale. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,17</i>)

>  There is also a very fine specimen of the white crane, or kotuku, a bird that is now so seldom seen that "rare as a kotuku" has passed into a proverb with the Maoris in the North. <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  New Zealanders, therefore, faced the prospect of having a cheeky carnivorous parrot sporting a kotuku badge and urging its compatriots to 'give 'em a taste of Kiwi!' <i>(1991,Kiwi,73</i>)

>  Okarito's other famous residents are the majestic kotuku, or white herons. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  The Maoris had a profound respect for the kotuku, or white heron, and placed a very high value on its plumes. <i>(May 11, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,39</i>)

>  Hulme embroiders this Maori-inflected story of the lurking presence of death by casually remarking that, in Maori terms, the kotuku are aitua, and one of their functions is to announce death... <i>(Jun 1, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

### kotuku-ngutupapa
 
 <i>n.</i> royal spoonbill

>  <i>101. Platalea leucorodia regia</i> Gould, 1838 <i>Royal Spoonbill (Kotuku-ngutupapa) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,36,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Royal Spoonbill Platalea regia</i> GOULD, 1838 ... OTHER NAME: <i>Kotuku-ngutupapa</i>. ... Known to the Maori as kotuku-ngutupapa, the royal spoonbill must have reached New Zealand from Australia many times before European settlement. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,137</i>)

### kotukutuk
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kotukutuku](../K#kotukutuku))

### kotukutuku
 
 <i>n.</i> tree fuchsia (See [kohutuhutu](../K#kohutuhutu))

>  Kotukutuku produces a sweet juicy fruit in the summer, but don't eat too much because it can give you a violent case of the runs. <i>(Jul 24, 2002,Capital Times,25</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> The fruit of the kotukutuku, called konini, is a pleasant tasted berry, and is ripe about this month. <i>(Feb 17, 1847,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Feb 17 Brunner Exped. Middle isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 267]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kotukutuku, <i>Col[enso]. Fuchsia excorticata.</i>.. Otago kotohituku nf Will.] Kotohituk, <i>Hector. Fuchsia excorticata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  The berry of the Kohutuhutu, or Kotukutuku (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>); the Kohoho, or Poroporo, (<i>Solanum aviculare</i>), which ... was sometimes planted ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Fuchsia, or Kotukutuku (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>). <i>ibid.</i> 41 Kotukutuku Fuchsia procumbens, R. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/37</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kotukutuku (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>) is akin to the fuchsia seen in the gardens at home. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/194</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>, Linn. kotukutuku. The fruit is called Konini.-- A small and ornamental tree, 10-30 feet high; trunk sometimes 3 feet in diameter. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,127</i>)

>  <i>Kotukutuku, n</i>. Maori name for the New Zealand tree, <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>, Linn., N.O. <i>Onagrarieae</i>; written also <i>Kohutuhutu</i>. This name is not much used, but is corrupted into <i>Tookytook</i> (q.v.). See <i>Konini</i> and <i>Fuchsia. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,254</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>F[uchsia] excorticata, Linn. ... Native fuchsia; Kotukutuku</i>; the fruit <i>Konini. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,186</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Fuchsia excorticata (The Tree Fuchsia)</i>... Maori name <i>Kotukutuku</i> or <i>Kohutuhutu</i>, and of the fruit, <i>Konini. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,294,1940, p. 311 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Here and there a little watercourse ... arboured over by a matted roofing of <i>kotukutuku,</i> ... and <i>mahoe</i> or whitewood and glossy-green <i>karaka. </i> <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol ii South island) 32</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> i never tasted a drink made of kotukutuk (fuchsia berries), but i once helped to eat a pie made of them and it was very sweet. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]tukutuku, fuchsia</i>, see <i>ko[o]hutuhutu. </i> <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> During the warm months there was a long season for koninis from the kotukutuku trees (native Fuchsia) then ... the ramarama berries (Myrtaceae) were ripe. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,16</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Other berries popular as delicacies were the fleshy, white, pineapple-shaped bracts and berries of the kiekie (<i>Freycinetia banksii</i>), the ripe, black berries of the makomako or wineberry (<i>Aristotelia serrata</i>) and the small, sweet berries (known as <i>konini</i>) of the kotukutuku or tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excorticata). Lyall. </i> <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Fuchsia excorticata </i>TREE FUCHSiA ... Kootukutuku <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,70</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> He said, `Well, there's a swarm on a kotukutuku by the track - how about taking them?' <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,67</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]



 
 <i>n.</i> traditional reed wall panels

>  The traditional reed wall panels, known as kotukutuku, can be seen between the wall posts. <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/140</i>)

### kou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> A long lane was formed on an extensive level plain, fifty feet in width, by two high walls, formed by perhaps upwards of ten thousand baskets of potatoes, kumeras, indian corn (<i>Holcus indicus</i>), tarro (<i>arum</i>), water melons, turnips preserved, (<i>kou</i>) steamed kernels of the native fruit, (<i>karaka</i>[as acute]) baked roots of the <i>ti </i>palm tree, (<i>korou</i>) a quantity of dried cod, eels and other fish, also of shell-fish, bundles of fern-root, onions, peaches, grapes, &c. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/91</i>)

### kouka
 
 <i>n.</i> cabbage tree (See [ti-kouka](../T#ti-kouka))

>  To force young kouka to sprout, he ties the top leaves together in a ponytail. <i>(May 2000,NZ Gardener,81</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kouka, <i>Lindsay. Cordyline australis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> The bushy crowns (kouka) of <i>Cordyline australis</i>, the Maori ti tree, were relished ... also by early European settlers, who found the kouka tasted like cabbage and named the tree accordingly. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,34</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The sweetish, sago-like fecula (kouka) [of the cabbage tree] was eaten and the fibres spat out <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

### koukou
 
 <i>n.</i> owl, morepork

>  i know, too, the great trees where the owls (koukou) sit that cry in the night-time. <i>(Dec 16, 1897,Otago Witness,26</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Koukou</i> - The bird so called is a small owl, a native of New Zealand; and partakes of all the [54] character of the common British owl. ... its name has been given to it as an imitation of its cry. misspg <i>roukou </i> <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),53</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Roukou [Ruru] is the screech owl, abounding in New Zealand fern forests, and its cries are considered ominous at times by the natives or aborigines. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,115,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The <i>Owl</i> family has only one representative, and the cry of the bird, ... resembles the words, `More pork;' from which circumstance, this bird is familiarly known among the settlers by the name of `More pork ... [Maoris] call the owl kou-kou or Ru-ru. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/23</i>)

>  Athene Novae Zelandiae. More-Pork. Heruru. Kou-kou. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPiLOGLAUX NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (NEW-ZEALAND OWL, OR MOREPORK.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Ruru, Koukou, and Peho; `Morepork' of the colonists. <i>ibid.</i> 20 This note resembles the syllables <i>kou-kou,</i> uttered from the chest; and among the northern tribes the bird is usually called by a name resembling that cry. it is, however, more generally known as the `Ruru', and in some districts as the `Peho'. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,17,{1888 2nd edn 1/192, 196} ed.</i>)

>  <i>A few stray Notes on the New Zealand Owl, </i>Athene novae-zealandiae,<i> Gml. - Ruru and Koukou of the Maoris, and Morepork of the Settlers. </i> <i>(1889,TrNZi,21/200</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso)]

>  <i>ruru, koukou,</i> an owl (Ninox novaeseelandiae), morepork: the name, which is echoic, should really be kroukrou; but as the Maori disliked the sound of paired consonants, the two forms resulted according to the sound more prominent to the ear of the name-giver - koukou, or ruru: ruru much the more common. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>KiNG</b> We called that bird a koukou, that was the Hokianga name for it. Other places call it ruru. <i>(1983,Whina,134</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... we made shift to lighten this inevitable infliction of the deep bush with yarns ... that go best to the accompaniment of ... the `kou-kou' of the wondering morepork ... <i>ibid.</i> 15 The mournful ruru calls his `kou-kou', `kia toa' from a branch ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,12</i>)

>  ... lighten this inevitable infliction of the Maori bush with the yarns that go best to the accompaniment of a crackling log, the `Koukou' of the Morepork ... <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

### koukoupara
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish (See [kokopara](../K#kokopara),[also kokopu](../A#also-kokopu))

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Koukoupara - is a fish like the kokopu, but differently marked, and following the example of one of his informants, the translator [sc Beattie] has translated this as `mountain trout'. Koukoupara is the spelling adopted in the lists, but the [Maori] compilers have varied this with kopara, koupara, and kokaupara. Never once do they use the southern form of kokopara, which the southerners pronounced kokobala, which the early settlers soon turned into `cockabully'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 63]

### koura
 
 <i>n.</i> a flax plant

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> The principal sorts of cloak or mantle at present made by the Maoris from their native flax are - 1. The `Korowai,' or shaggy mantle. 2. The `Kaitaka,' or fine mantle; and 3. The `Pokeha,' or rain cloak. Of these, the variety of flax plant called the `Koura' yields the best fibre for the `Korowai'. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> crayfish [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [crawler](../C#crawler))

>  A favourite on the menu is 'koura', freshwater crayfish. <i>(Aug 20, 2002,independent Herald (Wellington),6</i>)

>  We'd spend a few lazy hours catching koura with a piece of meat tied on a 20ft length of weighted twine. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,130</i>)

>  Francis Driver knew it wasn't going to be easy, but she was determined to establish New Zealand's first freshwater crayfish farm... To set up Sweet Koura Enterprises, she had to fight government regulations, the weather and unpredictable - if not overly large - livestock. <i>(Sep 2002,Cuisine,No. 94 : 166</i>)

>  in macro, the koura, or freshwater crayfish, resembled an alien dinosaur; it was extraordinary to find them at a depth of 130 m. <i>(Jan 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> 'i'll take koura over melting moments any day,' he laughed. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,170</i>)

>  The standard market price for legally farmed koura is about $80 to $90 a kilogram. <i>(Jul 20, 2005,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Small freshwater lobsters, known as koras, shared a like view. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,135</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition. Check spelling "kora"]

>  When KAL drains the first pond, along with "many thousands" of the somewhat rare native freshwater fish "Cran's Bully", a number of good-sized native koura will also be destroyed. <i>(May 1992,North & South,71</i>)

>  They were down at the creek after school hunting for koura and she was sick of turning over stones and nothing there. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  <i>koura n. NZ</i>small freshwater crayfish. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> <i>gorau</i>, a species of cray-fish, or rather fresh water lobster for they more resemble the latter. <i>(1847,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from JrNl in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 152]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Amongst the Crustacea is the <i>koura</i>, which is the general name for both the sea cray fish and the fresh water; the former is nearly two feet long, and abundant on all the rocky shores; the latter is from four to eight inches long. The Rotorua lakes abound with the largest, but near Paparoa, on the Wanganui, i met with one nearly a foot long. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,414</i>)

>  <b>St JOHN</b> Rotorua has its <i>kora</i>, a large prawn, or diminutive fresh water lobster, whichever you prefer. <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 554]

>  <b>TiNNE</b> i had heard ... of the fresh-water cray-fish (goura) that are found in these lakes ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,17</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The other principal species formerly taken were the inanga and kokopu (<i>Galaxias</i> and <i>Retropinna</i>), upokororo (<i>Prototractes</i>), and panoko (<i>Cheimarrichthys</i>); while the lamprey (piharau and korokoro) and fresh water crayfish (koura) were taken in considerable numbers in some districts. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,71</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The other principal species formerly taken were the inanga and kokopu (<i>Galaxias</i> and <i>Retropinna</i>), upokororo (<i>Prototractes</i>), and panoko (<i>Cheimarrichthys</i>); while the lamprey (piharau and korokoro) and fresh water crayfish (koura) were taken in considerable numbers in some districts. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,71</i>)

>  <b>FiRTH</b> <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,51</i>) [NOTES: inland, freshwater fish and <i>koura</i> (crayfish) were taken ...]

>  <i>koura</i> , crayfish (Palinurus edwardsii), place-name Kai-kooura, the place where a noted Tama enjoyed a meal of koura. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  So they [Maori children] all went catching koura up the creek because it was the thing they liked doing best. <i>(1978,Listener Short Stories,2/18</i>) [NOTES: 1946 Morice in 1978 Listener Short Stories Vol 2 18]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The newcomer's attention was never caught by the kakahi shellfish which is quite common in the Waikaka, but they noticed an occasional koura or freshwater crayfish. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,6,1956 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 6]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>And in the creek they saw the crayfish and Mr Jones said he called them crawlers, but the Maoris called them koura</i>. [ital sic] <i>ibid.</i> 102 And there are little crayfish in the creek. Koura? Crawlers? - you wouldn't get very fat on them, Dave said. <i>ibid.</i> 273 (gloss.) koura: used for both freshwater lobster and saltwater crayfish. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,82,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> ... to wade up the clear gully stream in pursuit of the small koura, or crayfish, and any native trout or kokopu we could spear, or guddle. <i>(1952,Faring South,79</i>)

>  <b>HiNTZ</b> The koura has also been granted a reprieve after what seemed to be a death sentence from the early trout. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,67</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Koura - the <i>ou</i> sound is near English <i>oh</i> - is the Maori word for both marine crayfish and freshwater<i> Paranephrops ... </i>There are three species of the freshwater koura: <i>P. zealandicus </i>(Stewart island to South Canterbury), <i>P. setosus </i>(North Canterbury), and <i>P. planifrons</i> (northern and western parts of the South island and throughout the North island), found in lakes, ponds and creeks. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,109</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> She would go to the creek ... to look for koura under the stones or fish for karawakas with a worm tied to a stalk of paspalum; or to the bush to gather karaka-berries ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,177</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> CRAYFiSH (freshwater) ... A successful method of catching freshwater crayfish or `koura' as he is known to the Maoris, is to use their method. <i>ibid.</i> 140 KOURA Maori name for the crayfish and more often the Freshwater Crayfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,42</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> Sometimes on Sunday afternoons, before milking began around half past three, we'd go down to the creek at the back of the farm to catch koura, those small freshwater crayfish we called crawlies. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,5</i>)

>  Farming koura, or fresh-water crayfish, could provide a supplementary income for farmers according to Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries scientist Brian Jones. <i>(Dec 13, 1985,Dominion,17</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> They taught me how to catch eels, kupai, and koura (crawlies) out of nearby streams ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,22</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kowhai, n.</i> Maori name given to (i) Locust-tree, <i>Yellow Kowhai (Sophora tetraptera</i>, Aiton, N.O. <i>Leguminosae</i>). (2) Parrot-bill, <i>Scarlet Kowhai ( Clianthus puniceus, N.O. Leguminosae</i>, or <i>Kaka-bill</i>. (q.v.). Variously spelt <i>kowai</i> and <i>kohai</i>, and corrupted into Goai(q.v.). by the settlers. <i>(1898,Austral English,254</i>)

>  Mr Tuapawa, whose work includes retrieving golf balls from the lake for a hole-in-one company, knows only too well the koura's larcenous ways. "You get koura that steal the balls and run off. i've picked up rocks to look for the golf balls and seen crayfish that stand their ground and i just say 'i'll be back for you later, mate." <i>(Jan 23, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Marine) crayfish [[Ma. <b>koura</b> crayfish (<i>jasus spp</i>), gold prawn (<i>penaeus setiferus</i>)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 115.]

>  Some genius would think it funny to chase the kids around with a live koura... <i>(Jan 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D9</i>)

>  English New Zeland South-sea islands ... ... ... Come here Haromai Harromai ... ... ... A Lobster Kooura Tooura ... ... ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/287,1955 ed.</i>)

>  Northern Southern Otahite -->a Woman Wahine Wahine Wahine Come here Horomai Horomai Harromai Fish Heica Heica Eyca a lobster Kooura Kooura Tooura Sweet potatoes Cumala Cumala Cumala <i>(Mar 1770,Banks Journal,2/35,19i62 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Cray-fish Kahouda <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/334</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|oura, s. The crayfish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> [The cavern] is mostly in request by parties fishing for the kohuda, (craw fish), and other fish, ... <i>ibid.</i> 2/27 ... one of them [sc. nets], in the shape of a bag fixed to a pole, is made use of in fishing for the <i>kohuda</i> [a-acute] or crayfish, among the rocks, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/136</i>)

>  <b>WALTON</b> Of the crustaceous genus there is the kohuda, or crayfish, about the size of our lobster, and resembling it in flavour. <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... this net is found to be extremely serviceable in fishing for the <i>kohuda</i> or [198] cray-fish, that congregate among the rocks in certain places very numerously; ... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/197</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 11. <i>Palinurus</i> ?sp. `Lobster, or Sea Cray-fish.' <i>Cook</i> ... `Kohuda or cray-fish', Kohura. <i>Dieffenbach. Polack</i>, i. p.326. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/266</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Anulose Animals) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/266]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species i will name a part, viz. ... Gora [Koura], crawfish, similar to lobster; ... [<i>Glossary </i>119 koura freshwater crayfish] <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> There was `mango' or shark, `koura' or cray fish, `huahua' or potted birds, besides preserved eels, and a variety of other dainties. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,70</i>)

>  At the beach i got a few potatoes and some koura crayfish and then went on ... to the pa at Ohinemutu. <i>(Sep 15, 1875,Chudleigh Diary,241,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> <i>Kaura</i> (crayfish) are very plentiful among the rocks of a picturesque little island in the Bay. <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,113</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 113]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Koura - this is the crayfish, the same name applying both to the big sea ones, and the small river ones found far inland. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  The fishing off the island is very good, there being abundance of hapuku, kokiri, kohikohi, maumau [sic], schnapper, kahawai, tarakihi, in addition to plenty of shell-fish such as koura or crayfish, crabs, paua, etc. The mako shark, so well known for its beautiful teeth, which are highly prized by the Maoris as ornaments, is found off this island and nowhere else in the world i believe, <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  <i>ko[o]ura, crayfish (Palinurus edwardsii), place-name Kai-ko[o]ura, the place where a noted Tama enjoyed a meal of ko[o]ura. </i> <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  koura The Maori name of the crayfish. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  [species name] koura .. [scientific name] <i>Paranephrops planifrons .</i>. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> COMMON CRAYFiSH ... <i>Other names: Jasus lalandei</i>; spiny crayfish; koura, koura-papatea (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,298</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The red crayfish, or <i>koura papatea</i> of the Maori, is a type of lobster found only in the New Zealand region, although it is related to Australian and South African species and is a member of a worldwide family, the Palinurids. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,298</i>)

### kouraraki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. whalefeed](../W#whalefeed),[spawn](../S#spawn))

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The tide was ebbing, and had left the beach red with `kouraraki,' a small crustaceous animal not unlike the shrimp, but more diminutive. They were still alive, and so numerous that they formed a stratum three or four inches thick, to walk over which was as if one trod on new-fallen snow. The `kouraraki' are fed on by the whale; and their appearance early, and in abundance, was hailed as the prognostic of a good season. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,185</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 4 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 185]

### kourepoua
 
 <i>n.</i> spotted stargazer

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SPOTTED STARGAZER ... <i>Other names: Geniagnus monopterygius;</i> catfish, monk, dogfish; kourepoua (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,263</i>)

### koutereke
 
 <i>n.</i> quail

>  K|outa r|eke; Bird so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,LEE & KENDALL,169 ed.</i>)

### koutou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>koe, korua, koutou</i>, second pers. pro., sing., dual, and plural. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>)

### koutu utu
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kohutuhutu](../K#kohutuhutu))

### kow-rowdy-dowdy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRiGHT</b> [The Maoris] bruise the corn [maize] and boil it in water, calling it kow-rowdy-dowdy - i suspect a name given by some jocular pale-face. [? <i>kuurarirari </i>wet, sloppy (The road is quite full of slush and mud)] <i>(1841,Handbook for Emigrants,44</i>)

### kowai
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of kowhai q.v. [also various esp. early spellings with initial g- in cits below]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Trees of various kinds spreading their branches over the river and occasionally the <i>Kowai</i> was very conspicuous whose tender boughs hung elegantly over the stream ... <i>(Nov 13, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,346,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>Kowai, (Edwardsis mycrophylla,) </i>is met with [397] principally on the banks of rivers; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/396</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> ... the Rowai (or Rowly) [Kowhai] which is found by the seaside or by the swamps, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,106,1956 ed.</i>)

>  The timber in [the groves] is chiefly Kaikatea, (white pine), Mahi, (black pine), Remu, (red pine), Pokaka, Kowai, and Totara. ... That in the other forest [ie. not Harewood or Alford] is principally tawai, (black birch), the best timber for boat and ship building, but sometimes unsound: ... <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  ... between the two is a pretty grove of yellow kowai .. <i>(Jan 14, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 14 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 361]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Along the banks of rivers, the `kowai' or `native laburnum,' displays its pro-[52]fusion of golden blossom. <i>(1866,Polynesia,51</i>)

>  [KOWAi] ... it feeds on the leaves of the Kowai (<i>Edwardsia microphylla</i>) or on wild cabbage. <i>(1869,TrNZi,TrNZi 1/iii/14</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  Sophora tetraptera, var. Kowai <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>T.C.KiERNAN</b> Kite and C.H.S. sledged posts from Tylee's spur and cut some Kohi posts for the horse paddock. <i>(Jan 4, 1878,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1878 Jan 4 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1926 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 122]

>  <b>LADY MARTiN</b> it was spring-time, and the great white clematis and the yellow kowai blossoms hung over the brim of the river in masses of gold and silver, which were reflected in the clear blue water. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,94</i>)

>  [KOWAi] b. Mainly Si goai forms. Spg <i>goai</i> etc. A chiefly Si var. spelt variously <i>gohi, goi, gowai, gowhai, ghoa(i), ghoi;</i> a hearing of Maori /k/ as a realised [g]; pronunciations [gouai] and [goi]. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2</i>)

>  <b>TANCRED</b> Another valuable tree is the goi or yellow chanthus [S. microphylla] <i>(1856,Natural History of Canterbury,20</i>)

>  Mr M.Tom & J.F. pulling down Goi on Roughridge. <i>(Jul 13, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Ghoi Bush. <i>(Sep 4, 1860,St Leonard's Journal</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> Bullocks dragged home Goi, cut up part of it into stobs, mutton block, etc. <i>(Aug 27, 1860,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Aug 27 Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 97]

>  <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> The different varieties of indigenous timber known to exist in the province are the black birch or pine, the goa, the totara, and manuka. Most of these ... serve to supply the limited demand created by what is called `bush architecture'. <i>(1862,Otago As it is,45</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> i remember nothing but a rather curiously shaped gowai-tree. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,104, also 95</i>)

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> `Gohi' [and later, 176] `Kowhai Bush' ... i can give you no idea of the variety among the shrubs: the koromika, like an Alpine rose, a compact ball of foliage; the lancewood, a tall, slender stem, ... with a few long leaves at the top, turned downwards like the barb of a spear, and looking exactly like a lance stuck into the ground; the varieties of matapo ... each leaf a study, with its beautiful tracery of black veins on a yellow-green ground; the mappo, the gohi, and many others ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,94,1874 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> (And) [GHOAi,GOA, GOAi] <i>Sophora tetraptera </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany</i>)

>  i refer to the Kowhai, or as it is called in the south the Gowhai <i>(Edwardsia). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/15</i>) [NOTES: -->1869 TRNZi (Monro)]

>  (And)[GOAi] <i>(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> ... goi, rata, bokako, birch, manuka, maple ... <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,107</i>)

>  <b>MciNDOE</b> (And) [GOi] <i>Sophora tetraptera </i>... goai <i>(1878,Sketch of Otago</i>)

>  <b>PAULiN</b> (And) [GOWAi] <i>(1889,Wild West Coast</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Goai blossom. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas</i>) [NOTES: c1895 (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas]

>  <b>COURAGE</b> Yellow Goi, as the men called it, or `Kowhai', as the Maories called it. <i>(1897,Light and Shade,237</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Goai ... </i>common name in southern island of New Zealand for<i> kowhai</i> (q.v.), of which it is a corruption. it is especially used of the timber of this tree, which is valuable for fencing. The change from K to G also took place in the <i>Otago</i>, formerly spelt <i>Otakou. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,162</i>)

>  <b>BLAiR</b> `The land of the <i>goai</i> tree, mapu, and pine, / The stately <i>totara</i>, and blooming wild vine.' <i>(1860,New Zealand For Me</i>) [NOTES: cited in Morris 163 s.v. goai]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> `i remember nothing but a rather curiously shaped gowai-tree.' SALE BY AUCTiON Bricks, Goi Coggs, Manuka and Goi House Piles. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,104</i>)

>  `Kowhai' went through many stages - `goa', `goai' etc before settling to the two forms, `kowhai' in the North and `gowhai' in the South. <i>(1926,TrNZi,670</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Pop. Names]

>  <i>Gowai </i>.A common spelling and pronunciation of <i>kowhai</i> (Sophora). it was from this tree that many bullock-yokes were made. (Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement],p.95) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> i have seen Maoris making fire with two sticks. A piece of dry hinahina would be laid flat on the ground and a piece of hard goai rubbed up and down in a groove in it. ... The Maori soon produces fire ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,15</i>)

>  Bushmen usually called a puketea, buckyteer; a kowhai, goey; and a kaponga, bunger. <i>(Aug 12, 1957,Dominion,[corresp. col.]</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Many native plants have Maori names more or less mutilated such as <i>matagowri, goai</i> (kowhai), and <i>biddy bid</i> (piri piri). <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> So between Mr. M---'s and the next halting-place (save two sheep-stations) i remember nothing but a rather curiously shaped gowai-tree ... that can form milestones ... to mark progress. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,v ii/104</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year v ii/104]

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> The chief use to which `Goai' timber was put in Otago ... was ... posts for fencing ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,76</i>)

### kowaowao
 
 <i>n.</i> a fern, <i>Phymatosorus diversifolius</i> HOUND'S TONGUE FERN (See [hound's tongue fern](../H#hound's-tongue-fern))

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Phymatosorus diversifolius</i> HOUND'S TONGUE FERN ... Koowaowao ... Fronds glossy green, strap-shaped to variously lobed ... <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,465</i>)

### kowarawara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Of Mamaku of Otaki] On its being remarked that he was going gray he said, yes, you don't see the kowarawara (an epihpyte) growing on the ngaio or mahoe, a tree which is of no value and never attains any size, but on the large and valuable timber trees, only such a the totara and rimu. i am a totara and pointing to a young man, he is a ngaio. <i>(Jun 16, 1849,Journal,6/113</i>)

### koware
 
 <i>n.</i> ediBle raupo root (See [koareare](../K#koareare))

### kowhai
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Sophora tetraptera, S. microphylla</i>, trees which grow mainly on river banks and the edges of forests and in gardens with a mass of bright yellow flowers in spring; aka yellow kowhai, New Zealand laburnum [ORIGIN: Maori, [(1844) 1898 Hocken Contributions (A).]] (See [also kowhai-ngutukaka](../A#also-kowhai-ngutukaka),[red kowhai](../R#red-kowhai))

>  <b>Crowe</b> ...in 1783 kowhai became one of the first New Zealand plants to appear in London plant catalogues. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,36</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... the yellow <i>kowhai</i> tree, at this season of spring, covered with a profusion of golden blossoms, ornamented the banks of streams ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/10</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Edwardsia <i>grandiflora</i>, Salisb. ... Nat. name, `Kowhai', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 52</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> [T]he graceful ko-whai (Edwardsia[sic] Microphylla) droops over the banks of a small creek ..., intermingled with the Koro-miko (Veronica) in full flower, and the fresh leaved Mahoe (Melicytus ramiflorus). <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,5</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kowhai, <i>Col[enso]. Sophora tetraptera</i>, var. <i>grandiflora. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> <i>S. tetraptera</i>, Aiton, var. <i>microphylla</i>, Jacq. ... The `Kowhai' of the North island Maori; the `Kowai' of settlers, - or as the word is variously corrupted and spelt by them, - `Goai' or `Ghoai,' `Goa' or `Ghoa.' According to Hector, the type is known to Otago Maoris as `Houma;' while the term `Kowhai' is applied to var. <i>grandiflora</i> in common with its other varieties. Var. <i>microphylla</i> is also sometimes designated by the colonists the Native `Laburnum' or `Mimosa,' from the similarity of its foliage, in the arboreous condition, to that of these familiar ... European trees. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,73</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea </i>..; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety, <i>Sophora tetraptera</i> var.: <i>grandiflora</i>; .. and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum </i>... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Kohwai [sic] (<i>Sophora tetraptera</i>, var. <i>grandiflora</i>). A splendid tree, with laburnum-like flowers, the trunk often attains a diameter over two feet. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/34</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> NOTE. - As i have no desire to mystify readers unacquainted with New Zealand flora, i append a brief description of the shrubs mentioned:- Kowhai - A papilonaceous flowering tree, of the acacia tribe. [17] .... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iii/16</i>) [NOTES: (1889,1974)]

>  <b>HAY</b> There is the cabbage-tree palm, with bare shank and top-knot; the nikau palm, with weird and wondrous frondage; the lancewood, ...; the fern-tree, .... There is the twisting, squirming rata; the gaunt ... kahikatea; the golden kowhai; the dark velvet-covered rimu; the feathery red tawai; the perfumy mangiao; ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/187</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Kowhai</i> - a small or middling-sized tree ... Wood red, valuable for fencing, being highly durable. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,131</i>)

>  [Native name] Kowhai .. [Settlers' name] Kowhai .. [Family] Leguminosae <i>(1886,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> Kowhai [Maori name] .. Yellow Kowhai [Settlers' name] .. Sophora Tetraptera .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  Kowhai .. Sophora tetraptera .. Wood of a pale brown colour, heavy, tough, elastic, and very durable; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11</i>)

>  The pea-flowered kowhai [fn <i>Sophora tetraptera</i>.] attracts the melodious tui to its honey-laden blossoms, ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]whai, </i>a tree (Sophora tetraptera), the yellow-flowering tree, first to blossom in spring, and adopted as the national flower of Newzealand. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  The Maori name of a small tree which bears beautiful yellow flowers. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> [Maori glossary] <i>kowhai </i>Tree (<i>Edwardsia microphylla</i>) <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,302</i>)

>  <i>kowhai n </i>-s [Maori] : a shrub or small tree (<i>Sophora tetraptera</i>) of Australasia and Chile that yields a hard strong wood <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1255</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Kowhai .. .. kowhai .. .. <i>Sophora </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>kowhai n</i>. Maori name for two trees (1) the locust tree (2) the parrot-bill [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),619</i>)

>  <i>kowhai ... n</i>. N.Z. tree with bright gold flowers in spring; <i>red kowhai</i>, see <i>kaka bill. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

>  <i>kowhai [s] Sophora microphylla </i>Ait. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> The kowhai is a tall thin tree, with greybrown bark. ... anyway, co-eye English pronunciation, kor-fie Maori pronunciation, alla same tree, get it? <i>(1983,Bone People, The,133</i>)

>  <i>kowhai n.</i> N.Z. a small leguminous tree of New Zealand and Chile with clusters of yellow flowers. [C19: < Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,624</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 416; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 274]]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The kohai too ... covered with bright yellow blossoms. <i>ibid.</i> 144 Everywhere are to be seen ... the beautiful mimosa, called Kohai, (<i>Edwardsia microphylla</i>,) covered with clusters of yellow flowers. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/58</i>)

>  30 Kowhai slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

### kowhaiwhai
 
 <i>n.</i> traditional Maori rafter decoration

>  <i>Pitau</i>. a work by Patterson that derives its movement fropm the designs of kowhaiwhai panels and the curved shape of the koru, is part of Footnote Dance Company's national tour <i>Best Foot Forward</i>. <i>(Jul 23, 2003,Capital Times,13</i>)

>  My favourite moments of the walk are the beautiful kowhaiwhai patterns in the ceiling of the ASB Bank.. and the quite breathtaking, rose pink Daily Telegrapoh building. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,8</i>)

>  Kowhaiwhai patterns have appeared on stamps, currency, and airline insignia but the breadth and complexity of this Maori art form has never been fully recognised... Traditionally, the art of kowhaiwhai painting was associated with the heke (rafters) of whare whakairo (carved ancestral meeting houses) and was the most highly developed form of Maori painting. <i>(Oct 25, 2002,Dominion Post,A16</i>)

>  <i>Te Paki</i> by Patterson is a joyful celebration.. of the kowhaiwhai patterns painted on to the rafters of whare nui throughout the country. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  Te Pouhono Courses in Art: Kowhaiwhai, Whakairo, Raranga and more. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera</b> She lets herself be absorbed into the meeting house...with its figurative paintings and beautiful kowhaiwhai rafter patterns. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,250</i>)

>  This chunky AdamsGibb jersey, with a panel of kowhaiwhai patterning, is handknitted in Hawke's Bay... <i>(Jul 24, 2003,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  The souvenir store where you could get kowhaiwhai tea towels is long gone. <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  the peaks are an important spiritual landmark for Ngati kahungungu people..a dn appear in a in the kowhaiwhai in the meeting house at Papawai marae. <i>(Dec 27, 2005,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  <b>ZiMMERMAN</b> Overhead, the beams were painted with the lovely intricate <i>kowhaiwhai</i> pattern in black, white, and brick red - a graceful, involved scroll work pattern, based on the beautiful, natural curve of the unfolding fern frond, ... <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,122</i>)

>  The tuku tuku or kowhaiwhai (coloured scroll work) and the tukutuku (decorative reed panels) are admirable examples of Maori art ... <i>(Aug 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,187</i>)

>  <b>iHiMAERA</b> The gravity and reverence for [191] the past were evident on all the rafters, painted in the typical designs of the kowhaiwhai, bold red, white and black curvilinear designs in the shapes of the unfolding fern, ... the flower of the kaka beak, and the red lips of the ngutukura. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,190</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> They've got their fancy cardigans on, natural wool with kowhaiwhai patterns across the chest. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,93</i>)

>  He personally supervised the weaving of tukutuku (panels) for the interior walls and the painting of kowhaiwhai patterns on the rafters. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/24</i>)

>  Talks about the tattoo on his arm, the family kowhaiwhai pattern he had done last year and which replaces the more amateurish one he had done years ago as a teenager in Levin. <i>(Jun 14, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kuaka (<i>Limosa Baueri</i> the bird spoken of as `curlew' and `grey snipe' by the colonists. Large flocks are to be seen on our rivers, feeding on the mud-banks. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/222</i>)

>  [Animals Protection Act] There is a distinction in the case of the godwit (Maori kuaka, or hakakao), the season for which comprises the months of February, March, and April. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,139</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> These thoughts flew through my brain like a covey of kuaka (snipe). <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,250</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> The young of many sea birds ... were used as food [on the Chathams], such as <i>Kuaka</i> (plover), young gulls (<i>Ngoiro</i>), shags (<i>Kuau</i>) ... <i>Ho[macron]po</i> (the albatross), <i>Hakoakoa</i> (mutton bird), <i>Taiko</i>[macron] (a smaller-sized mutton bird of slatey blue colour), <i>Titi[i'</i>s macron] (a still smaller size) ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,6</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... Mr. Pitcairn ... was killed on Uretara island, where he was camped shootin kuaka (godwit). <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/314,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... the most important [Chatham island] edible sea-birds: AS WRiTTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLiSH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358]

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> New to me was a blunt-winged Petrel ... [97] rather larger than the Kuaka <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist</i>)

>  <i>kuaka</i>, a migratory bird (Limosa novae zealandiae), godwit; nests in Siberia. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>POWELL</b> GODWiT (<i>Limosa lapponica</i>), or Kuaka of the Maoris is our best known migrant. (<i>Pelecanoides urinatrix</i>) of Southern waters. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,81</i>)

>  <i>kuaka</i>: petrel godwit. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the diving petrel, <i>Pelecanoides urinatrix</i>

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> We also got four kinds of Petrel in their burrows,- the Mutton Bird, Parara, Titi Wainui, and the Kuaka. <i>ibid.</i> 201 <i>Pelecanoides urinatrix</i> - Diving Petrel - Kuaka. Kuaka is also in the North island a name given to the Southern Godwit - Limosa Novae Zealandiae. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,16</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> These are the names used by the southern Maori; in the North id. Kuaka, Parara and Titi are applied to other birds. Kuaka: <i>Pelicanoides Urinatrix</i>, or Diving Petrel. Black Bill. Wing 4.7 inches. Titi Wainui:<i> Prion Turtur</i> or Dove Petrel. Parara: <i>Prion vittatus</i>, or Broad-billed Petrel. Bill very wide. Wing, 9.5 inches. Titi: <i>Puffinus Griseus</i> or Mutton-Bird. Sooty brown. Bill horn colour. Wing, 12 inches. <i>(1940,Rakiura,205</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Pelicanoides ... urinatrix .</i>.. var. <i>chathamica </i>... Diving Petrel. KUAKA. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,87</i>)

### kuku
 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine) a mussel [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kuku</b> mussel <i>(perna</i> and <i>mytikus spp)</i>] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 117.]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Then follows a series of shell beds, consisting of the remains of the following species, now still inhabiting the estuary;- <i>Chione stutchburyi</i> (Cockle); <i>Huai</i> or Pipi, <i>Mesodesma chemnitzii,</i> Pipi; <i>Amphibola avellana</i> (Periwinkle) Hetikutiku, and <i>Mytilus smaragdinus</i> (Mussel) Kuku ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,416</i>)

>  For general purposes the shells of both the <i>pipi</i> and the <i>kuku</i>, or mussel, were constantly in request. <i>(1894,TrNZi,26/433</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> White seashore sandhills, representing the accumulated deaths of ages of pipi, pupu, kuku, and other molluscs, mingled with the disintegrated mountain and volcanic quartz ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,2</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,106</i>)

>  in two feet of water, fixed to the rocks, were shellfish - paua, kuku, kinas. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,153</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> `Where's those kukus?' `in the washhouse.' <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,55</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (wood) pigeon, kereru, kukupa [[Ma. <b>kuku</b> pigeon] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 117.] (See [kereru](../K#kereru))

### kukupa
 
 <i>n.</i> = wood pigeon, kuku [aka kereru]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The pigeon, or kukupa [a-acute], is caught by the fowler placing a leaf, similar to the spear-grass, between his lips, and whistling, imitates the peculiar note of the bird; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/303</i>)

A new kind of rail was formerly very common; ... <i>(1840,Journal of Royal Geog. Society,11/195-215</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Dieffenbach An Account of the Chatham islands Journal of the Royal Geographical Society 11 pp195-215 in 1984 Holmes Chatham islands 95]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 42. <i>Carpophaga Novae Seelandiae</i>. - ... Hagareroo [e-grave] of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst</i>. Koukoupa of the natives. Kukupa of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. Kuku and Kukupa of the natives. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/194</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/194]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> in the lower regions the forest was much enlivened by birds. i shot some parrots [fn Nestor meridionalis. Kaka of the natives.], pigeons [fn Columba spadicea, Lath. Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. Among the branches of the trees hopped the neat fantail flycatcher [fn Muscicapa flabellifera, Gml. Piwakawaka.], and a social bird of a yellow colour, which has been depicted by Forster [fn Orthornyx heteroclytus, Lef. Popokatea.], and which had much the habits of the finch. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Searles amused himself with shooting a Kukupa one of the most beautiful birds the country posesses [sic]. <i>(Dec 16, 1851,Auckland Journals,14,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Many of the native names of the birds are derived from their cries: thus the pigeon is Kukupa, the brown parrot Kaka, and the owl Ru-ru, or Kou-Kou. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/23</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> To his inquiry the reply of the natives was that the cry of the <i>kukupa</i> had been heard. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/7/115</i>)

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] Once ...[sic] they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork. ... They were stewing the two birds when a kuku-pa (bush pigeon) flew up and perched. Bob shot it ... This is not exactly what the Britishers call `sport' - but there is more of nature about it than 30 gents in pink and tops ... waiting for a wretched little fox who, when he is killed, is not equal for culinary purposes to a more-pork. Once coming up the Waikato in 1851 your Uncle James got a pot-shot at a lot of parera (grey duck) ... <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

 <i>n.</i> (by metonymy) a Maori

### kumara bird
 
 <i>n.</i> long-tailed cuckoo

>  in many parts of Otago and Canterbury [long-tailed cuckoos] were called potato birds by the early settlers as they invariably came on the scene as the potato-planting season was going on; similarly, in the North island they got the name of `Kumara-birds'. ... Like the Cuckoos in other parts of the world, they appear before rainy weather or coincident with it, and have thus come to be known in many widely different localities as `Rain-birds' and `Storm-birds'. in many parts of Otago and Canterbury they were called by the early settlers `Potato-birds', as they invariably came on the scene as the potato-planting was going on; similarly, in the North island they got the name of `Kumara-birds'. <i>(1904,TrNZi,36/134</i>) [NOTES: (Fulton)]

### kumara house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> Kumera-houses, expressly built for the storing of that favourite vegetable, are put together with much care and neatness, of similar materials and workmanship to the superior houses <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/211</i>)

### kumara moth
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native moth, <i>Agrius convolvuli</i>, also known as convolvulus hawk moth and sphinx moth, that feeds on convolvulus and kumara plant crops.

>  <b>JOHNSTONE-SMiTH</b> Another popular pastime was collecting the four inch long, grotesquely beautiful kumara moth caterpillar, which abounded in the convolvulus which grew on the river bank. <i>(1975,Battles, Buggies and Babies,8</i>)

### kumara pit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  An old friend.. took me to see some shallow holes on the side of a ridge, which he spoke of as being old Maori kumera pits. <i>(Sep 18, 1912,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  Maori would also smoke the insides of their kumara pits before laying down the excess crop for winter. <i>(May 24, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  Along the way, they pass through a covenanted area of rare coastal forest with more than 200 species of native plant, including kauri, as well as historic Maori fortifications and kumara pits. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/88</i>)

>  <b>MANiNG</b> These <i>kumera</i> pits, being dug generally in the stiff clay of the hill tops, have ... retained their shape perfectly ... [199] They are oblong in shape, with the sides regularly sloped. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(xiii) 198</i>)

>  Maori dogs and <i>kumara</i> pits caused sad havoc, however, with this small flock of well-bred sheep. <i>(1940,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement,193 fn</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> `This is really an old Maori fort ... You can see the earthworks and the kumera pits, and the fighting pits.' <i>(1976,Pacific Star,166</i>)

>  <b>MciNTOSH386</b> (W.J. Elvy `Maori History') Much has been writted concerning the so-called pit [387] dwellers, who formerly inhabited the district. When Europeans commenced to settle in the Sounds the bush had practically established itself to the water's edge. The few vestiges of aboriginal cultivation were rapidly becoming clothed with a dense second growth and, except for a few localities occupied by the remnants of native tribes, had been practically obliterated. As the colonists commenced to clear the bush, despite the fact that on their land were large forest trees that had apparently taken centuries to grow, vestiges of an ancient occupation were brought to light in the shapes square or oblong pits. These pits were of varying shapes and sizes, many bunched into steep spurs and forming a series that reached from the shores to the tops of the low hills, and all placed in secure positions on land not liable to slip. These numerous pits were widely scattered throughout the Sounds and, some being similar to those in use in the North as store pits, they were called by the settlers' `Kumera pits' under the impression that they had been used to store those tubers for food supply. investigation proved this idea to be erroneous; the pits were found in positions unsuitable for such storage and the presence of shell heaps amongst them proved that they had formed part of the dwellings of an ancient people. <i>(1940,Marlborough</i>)

### kumara/kumera
 
 <i>n.</i> a Polynesian sweet potato, ipomoea batatas (grown commercially by Maori farmers since the 1860s). [ORIGIN: Maori, many variant spellings in early use]

>  <b>MCLAUCHLAN</b> The original Maoris, more than a thousand years ago, brought with them the kumara, the taro, gourd, yam ... [coomra] <i>(1981,Farming of NZ,52</i>)

>  Our people demand that our leaders have the humility of a kumara. <i>(Dec 15, 2003,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Near sunset we arrived at a settlement of one of Rewa's friends where he overtook us here six large baskets of <i>kumera</i> were immediately turned out for our part. <i>ibid.</i> 132 Before we left, Rewa brought out some baskets of<i> kumara </i>for our boys to carry home. <i>(Mar 20, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,112,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CARL.ETON</b> `Kumara had been planted over the whole plain.'<i>ibid.</i> 1830 p. 79:`We passed over the hill, and found the assailants feasting on the kumara, or sweet potato, which they just pulled up from the garden at which they had landed.' <i>(1874,Life of Henry Williams,69</i>) [NOTES: >1828. `Henry William Diarys' [sic] (in Life by Carleton), p. 69:]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> We had to go through Cultivation mostly all the way. indian Corn, potatoes and Cumeras [kumara] or Sweet potatoes, Tarraw [taro] a kind of Yam. The Tarras comes i believe from the South sea islands. <i>ibid.</i> 56 ... after the Cumeras are gathered. ... Moyterra has 4000 Kits of Cumera besides the Common seed store. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> There are also two crops of kumeroe (red and white); it is a species of sweet potato, smaller, though far superior in every way; ... <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,15,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> At the time of planting the <i>kumera (convolvulus batatas)</i>, or sweet potato, all who are engaged in the work ... are under the same restrictions. <i>ibid.</i>-154 Their kumera-stores are far better built than their most superb houses; ... spg <i>kumera</i> <i>ibid.</i> 268 i am thinking that i may be likened to stagnant water ... that does not run down in brooks, upon the banks of which kumera and trees grow. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),85</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> ... fish, potatoes,<i> kumera</i> and melons. <i>(Feb 11, 1835,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,410,1961 ed.</i>)

>  (Sydney Herald) ... tutu, kumera, tea-tree. <i>(1837,British Colonization of NZ,324</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i> ... kumara,</i> a sweet potato, and sometimes so named. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ</i>)

>  <i>ku[u]mara</i> is the spelling of the name of this tuber, and this has been the spelling in the <i>Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>, for instance, from its first volume in 1892 to the present day. it has more than one been suggested that the tuber first came from Peru, and this is fully discussed in <i>Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>, vol. 54 (1945), 175, where the point is made that the northern Quichua-speaking people of Peru and Ecuador have the sweet potato and know it under the names kumar, komal, and kumal, which names have as counteraprts in Polynesia the terms kumara in Newzealand (Maori), Rarotonga, the Tuamotus, Mangareva, and Easter island; umara and umaa in Tahiti; kumala in Tonga, 'umala in Samoa; uala and uwala in Hawaii; with kumaa in the Marqueses; and ku'a'ra in Mangaia. Remembering the "l" and :r: interchange in some of the dialects, and remembering the dropping of letters as indicated by hamzah, it seems evident that the form kumara represents all these different forms: seeing, too, that in none of them is the median "a" replaced by "e," there seems to be no doubt that the correct spelling is kumara and not kumera. in Williams' <i>Dictionary</i>, ku[u]mara is the main entry: there is an entry kumera, but is no more than a reference; ku[u]mera = kumara. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/148</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>kumara</i>: sweet potato <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <i>KUMARA, GLAZED .</i>.. Wash and pare ... kumaras. Cook ... put in a buttered pan ... boiling 1/2 cup sugar ... water ... butter. Brush kumaras with syrup and bake. <i>(1932,St Andrew's Cookery Book,48</i>)

### kumarahou
 
 <i>n.</i> (aka gumdiggers' soap, poverty plant) Pomaderris elliptica, golden tainui, a common shrub of northern NZ whose crushed leaves can form a lather. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Golden Tainui or Kumarahou (Pomaderris kumeraho) - Another fast growing 2-3m high shrub, northern in origin, but certainly at home in Wellington's clay soils... Gum Diggers Soap is another name for this species. When water is added to leaves and rubbed in the hand, a lather forms. Seemingly utilised by the gum diggers of old to clean their hands. <i>(Nov 11, 1993,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  "This tree from Northland grows where the more well-known Pomaderis kumeraho does, and for many years people thought it was just a bigger form of the kumeraho. But it is its own species," Alastair says. <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> Kumarahou was, and still is, widely used in Northland for relief of all chest complaints and as a 'blood purifier'... <i>(2009,Which Native Forest Plant?,59</i>)

>  The kumarahou which i get for my own use grows miles and miles away in rough gum-lands. <i>(Oct 26, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,24</i>)

>  <b>SHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[omaderris] elliptica, Lab</i>. ... North Cape to Tauranga Harbour, on open clay hills. <i>Kumarahou. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,99</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pomaderris elliptica (The Elliptical-leaved Pomaderris)..</i>. Native name, <i>Kumarahou</i>, from <i>Kumara, a tuber-like root, and hou, growing deep or strongly.</i> [Now much cultivated in New Zealand gardens.] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,236,1940 p. 249 ed.</i>)

>  i got a better medecine. Maori medecine. i got it from a bush in the north. <i>Kumarahou. </i> <i>(Aug 1953,Here & Now,20</i>)

>  ~ The Maori name of a yellow flowered plant of northern New Zealand recommended as a cure for asthma. (Pronounce <i>koo|-ma-ra-ho|</i>). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),232</i>)

>  <i>kumarahou [s] Pomaderris kumeraho</i> A. Cunn. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,43</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>POMADERRiS KUMERAHO</i>. Kumarahou is at home in the gumlands of Auckland, those broad tracts of hungry country where great kauri forests once grew, leaving deposits of resin - ... Flower heads rubbed between wet hands make a lather, hence the name `gumdiggers' soap'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,88</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Pomaderris kumeraho</i> A. Cunn. Common names: <i>Poverty weed, gumdigger's soap </i>Maori names: <i>Kumarahou, papapa </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,84</i>)

>  <b>LOCKE</b> Botanical discoveries were spice to the dish. i shan't forgot the first time i saw [near Auckland] ... masses of kumarahou adorning a barren outcrop of white clay. (A cultivated variety is now sold as golden tainui.) <i>(1981,Student At The Gates,73</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> [On gumland] other characteristic shrubs are <i>Dracophyllum lessonianum</i> and the strikingly yellow-flowered kumarahou or `Gumdigger's Soap' (<i>Pomaderris kumeraho</i>). Among herbs, sedges and the umbrella fern (<i>Gleichenia circinata</i>) are prominent. [253: fn 80] Kumarahou flowers rubbed together with water form a sort of lather. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,114</i>)

>  in the 1930s, kumarahou was heavily promoted by radio personality Aunt Daisy. "Old people and young ones with rheumatism or wheezing with asthma would come to the studio and the office girls would hand out portions of kumarahou," said Aunt Daisy, recalling her days on 1ZB. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### kumi
 
 <i>n.</i> a tall tale
