 <i>n.</i> a large lizard

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> On the right below the high top called Pou-nui-a-Hine is another small cave beneath a limestone projection, in ancient times the home of a <i>kumi. </i> <i>(1921,Tutira,65</i>)

>  The Kumi, seen for the first time in three generations, used to live in trees and among boulders, grew to 12 ft. long, had huge jaws with curved teeth (like the one seen) and used to lie in wait for its prey. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(15) : 13</i>)

>  Most people in Gisborne are inclined to doubt the whole story, and think the native might have seen anything but a real kumi. So now, when anyone tells a story of any kind, the truth of which seems open to doubt, people say, "Ah, that's a kumi." <i>(Oct 28, 1899,NZ Times,3</i>)

>  This [indigenous arboreal] animal was supposed to be known to the Maori, who called it "Kumi." <i>(Aug 28, 1926,Press, The,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> variant of kamokamo (marrow) q.v. [also as cumicumi, kummy kum]

>  The kumikumi, or Maori vegetable marrow crops, have been exceptionally heavy in the Tauranga district this season. <i>(Jul 28, 1916,Dannevirke Evening News,4</i>)

>  That trifle.. published in the Observer.. caused ructions.. in the land of cumicumi, and milk and pumpkin squash. <i>(Nov 1, 1890,NZ Observer,18</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> They grew corn and kumaras, ... and the wonderful iron-barked ridged marrows that they called [c 1890s, Northland] `kumikumi'. (i have never ascertained whether it is really a Maori name or not.) <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,151</i>)

>  The crop subsidy scheme.. provides for a subsidy to be paid on..kumikumi. <i>(Jun 30, 1944,Westport News,5</i>)

### kumukumu
 
 <i>n.</i> gurnard

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|umu k|umu; Name of a certain fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,171</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> A fish was often caught which is nearly allied, even if it be not the same, to one described by Cuvier under the name of Trigla papilionacea: the natives called it kumu kumu. it is of bright orange-colour; ... taken out of its element, and in expiring lose its vivid colours. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/64</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species i will name a part, viz. ... Cuma [Kumakuma], a red fish similar in form to a catfish in the UStates; ... <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956 ed.</i>)

>  English Name Native Name ... ... Gurnet kumukumu New Zeland Salmon kahawai <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. <i>Cottoideoe, </i>there are six enumerated, of these the <i>Gurnard (kumu-kumu)</i> is most common, and the <i>Trigla Volitans (korama) </i>the most beautiful. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> GURNARD. ... The Red Gurnard or Kumukumu (<i>Trigla kumu</i>) is very abundant during the summer months in the harbours in the north, ... <i>(1972,Edible Fishes,113</i>)

>  Kumukumu, Gurnard .. [Scientific Name] <i>Trigla kumu</i> <i>(1878,TrNZi,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago) [Maori or Settlers' Name]]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> GURNARD (<i>Trigla kumu</i>). ... [37] The red gurnard, or kumukumu, is very abundant during the summer months in the harbours of the North, ... <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,36</i>)

>  <b>POATA</b> TE KUMUKUMU (or Gurnett). <i>(1919,Maori as a Fisherman, The,14</i>)

>  CHELiDONiCHTHYS KUMU (Lesson and Garnot). <i> Gurnard; Kumukumu.</i> Commonly secured by trawlers up to 40 fathoms. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (rarer in the south). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/122</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Chelidonichthys kumu</i> (Lesson and Garnot). RED GURNARD; KUMUKUMU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,54,1971 ed.</i>)

>  48. <i>Chelidonichthys kumu</i> Lesson and Garnot. Red Gurnard. Maori: Kumukumu <i>(1929,TrNZi,61/249</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>POWELL</b> RED GURNARD (<i>Chelidonichthys kumu</i> ), Kumukumu of the Maoris, is easily recognised by its parchment-like side fins which resemble wings, and curious finger-like processes associated with these fins. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Kumukumu or Red Gurnard <i>Currupiscis kumu</i> (Lesson) (Syn. <i>Trigla papillonacea</i> Cuv. and Val., and <i>T. kumoides </i>Steindachner) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,112</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 112]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> RED GURNARD (KUMUKUMU) <i>Currupiscis kumu</i> (Lesson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,359</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Maori name [for Red Gurnard, <i>Currupiscis kumu</i> ] kumukumu is often shortened by Europeans to `Kumu'. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,168</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> RED GURNARD ... <i>Other names: Chelidonichthys kumu</i> ; gurnard, common gurnard; kumukumu (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,291</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> RED GURNARD [<i>Trigla kumu</i>] ... Other names are gurnard, common gurnard, and the Maori kumu-kumu. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  any of a group of ..... fins;<i> kumukumu. </i> <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,483</i>)

>  <i>kumukumu n. Maori</i> gurnard <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,274</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]



 
 <i>n.</i> variant of kamokamo q.v.

### kunekune
 
 <i>n.</i> a Polynesian pig; a kind of wild pig [Jim Cameron writes: The entries in DNZE and NZPOD need modification and updating. Doubtless originally feral, the kunekune is a registered breed of domestic pig, much favoured by lifestylers. See article Pigs Might Fly, <i>Growing Today</i>, Feb. 1995, 49 and ad in same magazine May 1995 - "Kunekune boar registered pedigree."]

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The kune-kune pig, with its short distinctive beads (or dewlaps).. possessed a remarkable ability to fatten quickly. <i>(1987,Guide to Hunting in NZ,184</i>)

>  Kay is on the lookour for some old Kune-kune pigs; they have no snouts so they cannot damage trees and are marvellous for cleaning up old fruit. <i>(Jun 1990,NZ Gardener,35</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The hogs [of the Marquesas, Friendly isles and New-Hebrides] are of that breed which we call the Chinese, having a short body, short legs, belly hanging down almost to the ground, ears erect, and very few thin hairs on the body; ... <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,188</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The wild hog, which is caught with dogs, and afterwards domesticated and home-fed, partakes a good deal of the Chinese breed about the head, and frequently grows to a great size.] <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,167</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The kune-kune pig [a smaller type], with its short characteristic beads (or dewlaps) hanging from his lower jaw, possessed a remarkable ability to fatten quickly ... <i>(1982,Wild Pig,24</i>)

>  Also feral pig (Captain Cooker) and kuni kuni (Maori pig). <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,45</i>)

>  if animals had their pop. charts, New Zealand's kune kune pig would have to find a high place among the list of unusual animals. Even those most closely attached to the kune kune shake their heads over them. it's surprising, therefore, that such a rare character should be so little known and so little appreciated - and that so few are aware of how close kune kune pigs are to becoming extinct. TESSA WARD continues her series of articles. <i>(May 21, 1986,Press, The,20</i>)

>  New Zealand has never had a native pig. The <i>Dominion</i> newspaper, National Radio's <i>Rural Report</i>, and the 12.30 National News are all in the process of perpetrating a myth. Each of their news items told of a `rare native pig - the kunekune'. Dr Clive Dalton, of Ruakura Animal Research Station, said that only about 80 of these small round pigs remain; ... No trace of pig of any species has been excavated from archaic sites in this country.... The kunekune pigs are likely to have been brought to New Zealand by the Chinese coming to the goldmines of central Otago and for that reason as a special breed are well worthy of preservation. [signed] Mary Jeal/ New Zealand Archaeological Assn <i>(Apr 25, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  The Kunekune is a small and highly distinctive breed of pig, characterised physically by its dumpy legs, pot tummy, short upturned nose and its generally fat, roundabout appearance. <i>(Feb 2001,Growing Today,42</i>)

>  New owners should be aware that a Kunekune boar is still a sizeable male animal which, like all pigs, will have a healthy appetite for a bit of meat as well as standard vegetables. Young lambs and young children should be watched around all pigs. <i>(Feb 2001,Growing Today,43</i>)

>  Presently in residence are five cats, two miniature dogs, a kune kune pig, two white rabbits, a Canada goose... and two German owls. <i>(Oct 14, 2000,Dominion,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a short, round breed of pig. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Here at Haha i saw my first Kunikuni pig. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North island,226</i>)

>  Kunekune - Originally feral, but a registered breed of domestic pig. <i>(Feb 1995,Growing Today,49</i>)

>  .. Kuni-kuni sows sold for $45-48. <i>(Jul 6, 1995,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

>  Kunekune weaner bores[sic] white. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,42</i>)

>  A breed such as the Kune could exist, even thrive, in a community .. Kunes do less damage to the ground than their larger cousins. <i>(Feb 2001,Growing Today,43</i>)

>  Kunekune pigs have become synonymous with lifestyle farmers. The name kunekune means 'fat and round' in Maori but the breed's small size and placid nature mean it is well suited to small blocks. [kunikuni; kuni-kuni; kune-kune; kune] <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Feb/March 7</i>)

### kupapa
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand passionflower

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Passiflora <i>tetrandra</i>, Banks et Sol. ... Nat. name, `Ku-papa', <i>R. Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 73</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Ku-papa, <i>R.Cunn. Passiflora tetrandra. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori collaborating with Pakeha [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  A class of people was created - we [Maori activists] call them kupapa - from the first time the Pakeha arrived. These are collaborators or `crouchers' who serve white needs and white interests and prop up the colonial system that we have ... <i>(May 1, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a Maori serving in the Crown's forces in the New Zealand Wars [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [cf. friendly](../F#friendly))

>  May kupapa be fairly compared with a handful of deserters like Kimble Bent? <i>(Sep 3, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),109</i>)

>  in a curious historic reversal, they have become Pakeha kupapa: skilled in the ways and means of their culture, they constitute a powerful fifth column in the Maori cause. <i>(Dec 12, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <b>S. PERCY SMiTH</b> Ten <i>kupapas</i>, or allies, arrived in camp today from Wanganui. <i>(Oct 21, 1866,Taranaki Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Oct 21 S. Percy Smith Taranaki Journal in 1922 Cowan NZ Wars 2/532]

>  <b>COWAN</b> it was in fact only the help of these loyalist or Kupapa tribes ... that turned the scale and brought lasting peace to the old frontier. <i>ibid.</i> 2/206 The Armed Constabulary, the Wellington rifles ... made up the first and second detachments; the third was composed of the Kupapa, or Maori Force. ... [207] McDonnell advanced and his leading Maoris surprised a sentry's camp on the track. Here the Kupapas killed a man and two little children. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/1</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> it was in fact only the help of these loyalist or Kupapa tribes ... that turned the scale and brought lasting peace to the old frontier. <i>ibid.</i> 2/206 The Armed Constabulary, the Wellington rifles ... made up the first and second detachments; the third was composed of the Kupapa, or Maori Force. ... [207] McDonnell advanced and his leading Maoris surprised a sentry's camp on the track. Here the Kupapas killed a man and two little children. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/1,1983 ed.</i>)

>  The Kingite part of the Maniapoto are all right and so are those we may call the `Kupapa'. [ed fn] Those who supported the Queen during the New Zealand wars of the 1860s. <i>(Feb 9, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/70</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  He was on the point of leaving the Colony when Colonial and kupapa [fn. Kupapas, friendlies] troops ... carried an outwork at Weraroa ... without loss. <i>(1960,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/138</i>) [NOTES: 1960 Scholefield in Richmond-Atkinson Papers (1960) 2/138]

>  <b>KiNG</b> ... his mother was a niece of the kupapa Ngapuhi chief Tamati Waka Nene, from whom he took his second name. <i>(1983,Whina,27</i>)

>  <b>KiNG</b> ... his mother was a niece of the kupapa Ngapuhi chief Tamati Waka Nene, from whom he took his second name. <i>(1983,Whina,27</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> Some months earlier, at Ormond's request, he had led an expedition of kupapa (allies) against Te Kooti and the Hauhau ... <i>(1984,City of the Plains,11</i>)

>  <b>BELiCH</b> The <i>kupapa</i>, Maoris fighting on the British side, were variously known as `loyalists', `Queenites', and `the friendly natives'. ... [212] The motives of <i>kupapa</i> groups varied enormously ... <i>ibid.</i> 214 But throughout the period colonial units did ... have the support of `native auxilliaries' - the <i>kupapa</i>. <i>ibid.</i> 337 <i>kupapa</i> pro-government Maori (originally passive or neutral) <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,211</i>)

>  Certainly, the issue is complicated by the fact that the Moutoa battle was, to a large extent, a family fight, the victors of which provided kupapa ("friendlies") for many of the subsequent wars. <i>(Dec 3, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> [Te Kooti] was chased by kupapa and colonial troops through the East Coast, Urewera and central North island for four years. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,197-8</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> How did they regard the Queen's Maori soldiers? They had a name for them - <i>kupapa</i>, a derisive term used by Kingite and Hau Hau to identify those they believe "to be crawling on their bellies to the pakeha. They saw the `friendlies' as collaborators. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,44</i>)

>  For years, kupapa and their partners / scoured the quiet bush / for Tito and his tribe. <i>(Feb 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR & HARREX</b> Some of the main tribes fought alongside the settlers during the Anglo-Maori wars, and became known as the <i>kupapa<i\> or 'friendlies'. <i>(1978,Looking Back,35</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> friendly

>  The wily wit and intelligence of the Maori warrior was pitted against superbly trained British forces, and friendly kupapa allies, with their technological superiority. <i>(Apr 16, 2006,NZ Herald,A14</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> The Field Force divisions, augmented by kupapa units, were a select force kept at full strength until the mid-1870s. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,48</i>)

>  The reviewer is tempted to extract and highlight the stories of individuals:.. the kupapa warrior Te Keepa (Major Kemp), who asserted his independence of Pakeha governance just when the European colonists believed they had secured his loyalty... <i>(Oct 10, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

 <i>n.</i> John Dory

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kup|adu, s. Name of a certain fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,171</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 25. <i>Pagrus latus</i>. - ... Taken in the sea ... in Queen Charlotte's Sound. in the latter locality its native name is "Ghooparee.' <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/209</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/209]

>  ZEUS FABER LiNNAEUS. <i>John-dory; Kuparu</i>. Distribution: Odd examples have been secured as far south as D'Urville island and French Pass. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Zeus faber</i> Linnaeus. JOHN-DORY; KUPARU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,25,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> JOHN DORY (KUPARU) <i>Zeus australis</i> Richardson [<i>ibid.</i> 180] For some obscure reason the local abundant Seaperch, or Jock Stuart, has been termed the John Dory by Otago commercial and sport fishermen and others. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,178</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE JOHN DORY <i>Zeus australis (Richardson)</i> ... Maori name KUPARU. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kuparu</i>: species of <i>hapuka. </i> <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> JOHN DORY ... <i>Other names: Zeus japonicus</i>; St. Peter's fish; kuparu (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,214</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> JOHN DORY [<i>Zeus japonicus</i>] ... Kuparu is a Maori name still in use <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> JOHN DORY<i> Zeus japonious</i>: A member of the family Zeidae, of which there are at least 6 species in New Zealand waters. Also known as St Peter's fish of kuparu (Maori). <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,129</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> JOHN DORY (Kuparu) <i>Zeus faber (Zeus japonicus) </i>... very similar species occur in all other temperate seas. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,180,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTRTON</b> Closely related to the European species ... , John Dory, or <i>kuparu</i> in Maori, is widely distributed around New Zealand, especially in northern waters. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,75</i>)

### Kupe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ~ A famous Maori navigator said to have discovered New Zealand about 925 A.D. (Pronounce <i>koo-pe</i>...). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),233</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> Kupe, who is said to have been the first great sea voyager, came upon these islands over 1000 years ago. <i>(2000,Maori Heroes,15</i>)

>  Why did you not place Kupe in your list of explorers (Millenium Top 10, <i>Upfront</i>, June 12)? Did you not know of the Polynesian explorer who traversed the largest ocean on earth, Te Moananui a Kiwa (the Pacific Ocean), and discovered Aotearoa (circa 1300s)? <i>(Jul 3, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> We don't know the names of those who first left evidence of their lives at Titahi Bay, but it is said that the great Polynesian explorer Kupe was the first to tread its sands, coming ashore at Titahi Bay, Onehunga Bay and Whitireia. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,8</i>)

### kupenga
 
 <i>n.</i> a fishing net

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> A fishing-net Koope[acute]ngha <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/334</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kup|enga, s. A seine, or large fishing-net. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,171</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Our direct and nearest road would have been to pass a beach that lay before us, but that was strictly tapued [a-acute]; a party being engaged making a kupenga [e-acute], or seine; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/189</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> in size the seines (kupenga) used by the natives rival our largest, and are made of unprepared flax ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> At one bend of the stream, where it breaks into foam over masses of rock, we observed an eel-pah, whither the natives occasionally resort for the purpose of taking these fish in their <i>kupengas</i> or nets. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/97</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Finished making a kupanga, or net, which is about fifty feet by four. took a draught of about fifty good-sized fish with it, called the upokororo, or fresh-water herring. <i>(1847,Great Journey,25,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The fishing-net (<i>kupenga</i>) was, when of large size, a most valuable possession of the Maori. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,187</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> in consequence of using these nets (<i>Kupenga</i>), the old bone fish-hook fell into disuse at a remote period of their history; the <i>Kupenga</i> proved to be much more efficacious. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,5</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Hand-nets were also used - the kupenga by women, who did most of the fishing for kokopu; and the kape by men. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,13</i>)

### kupu
 
 <i>n.</i> ? error for <i>puku<i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>The Belly</i> ... [New-Zeeland] K-opoo [o1-grave] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

### kura
 
 <i>n.</i> school

>  Kura were first legislated for in 1989, by which time there were seven modelled after Hoani Waititi. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,70</i>)

>  She also goes along to Kura Reo Maori immersion classes around the motu. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,issue 18 summer 2005/2006, p5</i>)

>  A small urban kura, newly built in 1999 seeks a motivated and inspiring principal to lead the kura into the future. <i>(Nov 16, 2005,Dominion Post,E14</i>)

>  The kura has a roll of 65 students from year 1 to year 9, and projections could see that number rise to about 100 students in a few years. <i>(Jul 9, 2002,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### kura kaupapa (Maori)
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori-immersion primary school

>  The money would be used for.. specialist support for kura kaupapa Maori. <i>(Nov 25, 1997,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Her greatest regret is being pre-kura kaupapa and not having learnt to speak Maori. <i>(Apr 12, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  i believe some of the students who go through kohanga reo and kura kaupapa develop a kind of Maori and English bilingualism that enable them to move socially through both languages while having access to continue to improve their Maori. Kura kaupapa is especially exciting for the reason that it deals with quite difficult pragmatic problems in running small individual schools while facing major philosophical problems such as what is the goal of schooling and what are we doing about it. <i>(Jul 14, 2000,NZ Education Review,12</i>)

>  About 45 children from the marae's kura kaupapa tease each other as they gather under the veranda before going off to school. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### kura Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori school [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  it has taken 13 years for Porirua to get a Kura Maori (Maori School), but according to the board of trustees chairperson Tamati Olsen, the sacrifices and hard work have been worthwhile. <i>(Jun 14, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

### kurahina
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> KURAHiNA (<i>Eleotirs radiata</i>). <i>ibid.</i> 302 <i>Eleotris radiata</i> Quoy Kurahina <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,139</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Eleotris radiata</i> Quoy and Gaimard. KURAHiNA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,49,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Eleotris radiata</i> Quoy and gaimard. <i>Kurahina. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/415</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GRAHAM'S GUDGEON (KURAHiNA) <i>Grahamichthys radiatus </i>(Cuv. and Val.) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,320</i>)

### kuraihoro
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We landed in a pretty little [Taupo] bay ... from the cracks in the sides spring many beautiful shrubs especially one called e kuraihoro an auralia. <i>(Apr 26, 1847,Journal,V/47</i>)

### kurekure
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of earthworm

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Earthworms] were apparently steamed in bowls of hot water for several hours ... Two varieties, known to the Maori as <i>kurekure </i>and <i>whiti</i>, were reseved for the chiefs or as food for the dying. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,10</i>)

### kuri
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a mongrel, a ratbag.

>  <b>CLEARY</b> [A Maori speaks] `You kuris better tell your mates that any more / rowdiness ... and we bottle you all up with no escape.' <i>(1975,Pocketful of Years,23</i>)

>  <i>kuri n. NZ</i> a dog; <i>sl.</i> an unpleasant person. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,417</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 274]]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> `Where is that Jimmy? Dog of a child. Kuri. Feed me, woman, i'm done for.' <i>(1965,Outcasts,37</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the dog introduced by the pre-European Polynesian settlers [ORIGIN: Maori, variant spellings including goorie/goory q.v.] (See [Maori dog](../M#maori-dog))

>  The "trial" was a crew prank in Queen Charlotte Sound, when crewmen "tried", executed and then served up a kuri to another crewman who had intended taking it back to England as a gift to his patron. <i>(Jun 28, 2003,Dominion Post,F13</i>)

>  The Waikare-moana people.. decided that the man-eating "kuri" must die. <i>(Dec 4, 1912,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  Each of the featured items, from Fredd Dagg's gumboots to a stuffed kuri (Maori dog), is presented in glossy colour with its personal history and a description of its general importance to local culture. <i>(May 19, 2004,Capital Times,9</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> As long as he remained in the room, no one would think of asking who owned the kuri. <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,180</i>)

>  "i break my kuri in," said the Maori. <i>(Jun 2, 1926,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>PARKiNSON</b> A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Egooree, <i>A dog.</i> Teyka,<i> Fish. </i>Hewhai[a-grave], <i>A skate</i>. Eraperape, <i>A fish called Chimaera. </i> <i>(1770,Journal,126</i>)

>  [?June 2] ... this liberty of the [Maori] Boy offended old Will the Ram Goat who up with his head and knock'd the boy backwards on the Deck, ... when he told a very lamentable story against Goure the great Dog, for so they call all the quadrepeds we have aboard, nor could he be pacified till his shirt was wash'd and dry'd. <i>(Jun 3, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/170,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>A Dog</i> ... [New-Zeeland] gh-oorea [o1-grave] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> [see quotation at Maori dog] <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,10</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|udi, s. A young dog, pig, &c.; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,170</i>)

>  dog - goo'ree [kuri] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111</i>) [NOTES: >c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 111; ALSO 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 284 (|= macron precedes) dog, go|o|ree [kuri]]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>kararahe</i> [a2,a3,e-acute], or dog (Canis Australis), which, when young, is known as <i>kuri</i>, has been an inhabitant some two or three centuries. <i>ibid.</i> 2/46 The native name is kuri, the general name for the dog amongst the Polynesian race; but it is very curious that the Spanish word `pero' is also known to them. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/308</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The old native dog (kuri) seems to be extinct. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,135</i>)

>  Those kuris ... have not vanished yet. They were the ancestors of the wild dogs that used to be plentiful in all this Taupo country. <i>(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,15</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> He left the door open, the firelight near her, a crouching golden dog; not rough-coated dirty white, like poor old <i>kuri</i>, the Maori dog, who has died out. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,136</i>)

>  <i>kuri</i>, Maori dog, brought with him from the Pacific; now applied to any mongrel: long extinct, and only surviving in the strips of skin with hair attached used as ornaments on the Maori valued cloaks (kaku-waero). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> These wild dogs were the Maori `kuri' and were about the size of a collie, were usually white or tan in colour, with prick ears and sharp snouts and they howled instead of barked. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,49</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 49]

>  <i>kuri</i>: The Maori word for the dog introduced into New Zealand by the first settlers, and used for food; it is long since extinct.(Pronounce<i> koo-re</i>...). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),233</i>)

>  Along with the kuri, or Maori dog... the kiore was part of the first wave of introductions to this country. <i>(Mar 28, 1992,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Never forget.. <i>the kuri's</i> ecstasy and exit when detecting a stinting burned opossum barbecued <i>cordon bleu</i>. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,122</i>)

>  A kuri (dog) had been brought which was very good to eat, which fed on fern-root, but which could ko (dig) its own fern-root. <i>(Jul 16, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,27</i>)

>  The Kuri or Maori dog was very much like our own Pomeranian... it was also known to the Maori as peropero. <i>(Apr 29, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

>  The <i>kuri</i> is extinct. it is thought that the moa hunters used these dogs in small packs to hunt moa and flightless birds. <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,47/12</i>)

>  Along with the kuri, or Maori dog... the kiore was part of the first wave of introductions to this country. <i>(Mar 28, 1992,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  Even the spotted Gouri is definitely out. <i>(May 4, 1942,NZEF Times,i/45 : 6</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] 1. orig. dog brought by the Maori. 2. an early whaling ship-dog that bred onshore in the wild. 3. dog [often as goory.]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> kuri: a dog. The word comes direct from Maori. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,48</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> The kuri, as this type of dog was known, was quite a pet with the native women ... 2. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,x</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> The whaling stations that became established round the coast also had their dog population and, as the years passed, interbreeding with the earler canine residents resulted in a different type of kuri inhabiting the pas. 3. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,x</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> "By cripes, Bert, keep your blasted kuris tied up, will you," he bellowed. "You better wait and see me have my run. it'll give you some idea of how to handle a heading-kuri in the proper way." "There's a real brainy kuri for you." <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,13</i>)

>  <b>MCGiLL</b> goory: Pakeha corruption of 'kuri', Maori for dog. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> in early, Maori contextual use, occas. applied to the ?young of any (?smallish) animal; a young pig.

>  <b>POLACK</b> in the fullness of his joy, he ... promised me a small pig, or <i>kuri</i>, raising his extended hand the height it should be from the floor, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/240</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a dog owned, often communally, by Maori. <i>Canis familiaris</i>, the dog introduced by Europeans, and early associated with Maori ownership, and ill thrift, esp. such dogs running wild in packs. (in the mid to late 19th c. such wild dogs were often thought erron. to be descendants of the Polynesian dog, prob. largely because of the often derog. epithet Maori being attached to the wild dog.) (See [Maori dog](../M#maori-dog))

>  <b>COWAN</b> We had a couple of pig dogs with us, Maori kuris, which Hursthouse had borrowed from an old wahine at Te Kuiti ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,19</i>)

>  `Alexa' contributes a note on the modern Maori Kuri. ... the Maori tykes that roam the wilds of Ohinemutu ... For six months, while `batching' near the pah i made a study of these ... animals. <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,27</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> At this time - June, 1841 - the colonists ... were constantly overlooking annoyances ...: such as ... the perpetual worries caused by the half-starved and abominable <i>kuri</i> or dog. <i>ibid.</i> 113 [They] found it difficult to be as amiable with the native <i>kuris</i> or dogs. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,77</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> They had been in dread lest some of the village dogs should scent them out ... But no <i>kuri</i> came prowling in their direction. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,184</i>)

>  As children we looked forward from one shearing's end to the beginning of the next, when the gang of twenty or so Maori men, women, and children, with the inevitable kuri ... would come stringing into the station. [fn kuri] Dog. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,51,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> They [sc. early runholders] had good sport together [108] among the native game and the wild cattle and the hunting of the kuri. <i>(1945,Historical Southland,107</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The dogs mentioned [in Puketoi Station Diary, 1860] as worrying the sheep were wild dogs, the real `kuri', descendants of Maori dogs run wild before the white people came. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,107</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> Around her, younger [Maori] women ..., together with youngsters of all ages, and several dogs - real lean goories - and piles of bedding and boxes ... <i>(1949,Brown Man's Burden,82,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Like the Pakeha']



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> any mongrel or unruly dog [ORIGIN: Maori, >ca1890-1900 p.c. Prof. P.S.Ardern. goory used in Auckland for a mongrel dog, or often humorously.]

>  <b>DAViS</b> That's Campbell's kuri you're giving the kai to. <i>(1964,Watersiders,58</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> it was a sort of poodle .... No one would think of asking who owned the kuri. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,180</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>goori</i>, a dog, [286] from Maori <i>kuri</i>; <i>(1935,Slang Today,285</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 285-286]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Goori</i>. A dog: New Zealanders': late C. 19-20. A corruption of Maori <i>kuri</i>. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,343</i>)

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [and include] ... "go-ashore,' a cauldron (kohua), `taihoa,' and `goory,' a dog (kuri). <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>goory</i>, a mongrel dog, from Maori <i>kuri</i> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

>  <i>kuri</i>: dog <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> [Maori glossary] <i>kuri</i> Dog <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,302</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kuri</i>: dog. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <i>goory n.</i> dog [Maori <i>kuri</i>]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> kuri <i>dog </i> <i>(1982,Collected Poems,188</i>)

>  (Meikle) My brothers' slang and `funny' sayings delighted me. ... i remember [126] such words as `batty', `hoot' and `spondulicks' and many that ... were corruptions of Maori words. i spell them here as they sounded to me then: <i>ehoa, harka, kupai, kure, munga munga typo, markuta, pooku, tangy. </i> <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,125</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>kuri</i>: dog <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>kuri</i>: a dog: New Zealanders: late 19-20c <i>(1961,Dictionary of Slang</i>)

>  <b>MACKAY</b> The Maori people who passed by could understand the defunct kuri right enough but were unable to grasp the significance of the inscription. <i>(1961,Puborama,63</i>)

>  [interview: B.Crump] <i>Crump</i>: ... it's been good of you to tell these ignorant characters a thing or two about the old kuri [pig dog] <i>(Apr 19, 1962,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  (Cape `Down the Hall on Saturday Night') As soon as i've tied up the guri,/ As soon as i've broomed out the yard,/ As soon as i've hosed down my gumboots,/ i'll be living it high and living it hard. <i>(1964,Book of New Zealand, A (J.C. Reid),258</i>)

>  Mr Kirk said that Mr Hanan, while in opposition, had inflamed the passions of the people against apartheid, but we now saying [sic] those people protesting against it were `kooters on a kuri' - fleas on a dog. <i>(Jul 2, 1965,Dominion,7/1</i>)

>  <b>BAiLEY & ROTH</b> Soon as i tied up the goory,/ Soon as i swept out the yard,/ <i>(1967,Shanties,148</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> goories: mongrel dogs <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>)

>  <i>goorie</i> or <i>goory n. N.Z. inf.</i> a mongrel dog. [Maori <i>kuri</i>] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,480</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 213]]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Never forget the toitoi plumes of the dogs' tails leading on, the <i>kuri's</i> ecstasy and exit when detecting a stinking burned possum barbecued <i>cordon bleu. </i> <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,122</i>)

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAiPiRO - - An acursed drink ... TAiHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

### kuri dog
 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori dog

>  <b>POLACK</b> Koruhaua [a's-acute], her brother, and the kuri dogs, who appeared to thrive well on the expedition, occupied places in my canoe. These faithful animals had never been so well fed before; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/150</i>)

>  Everywhere you looked there were squalling kiddies and goory dogs and grey-headed old men with carved pipes. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,17</i>) [NOTES: >19??Finlayson in 1977 N.Z. Listener Short Stories 17]

### kurikuri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Aciphylla <i>squarrosa</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Kuri Kuri', Middle island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 87</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kuri-kuri, Middle island, <i>Lyall. Aciphylla squarrosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> <i>A. squarrosa</i>, Forst. - ... The `Papaii' of the North island Maoris, (Colenso); the `Kuri-kuri' of those of the South island, (Lyall). <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,50</i>)

>  Kurikuri, or Spear grass. Aciphylla squarrosa, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/42</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>A[ciphylla] squarrosa, Forst. ... Taramea; Kurikuri; Spear-grass. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,209</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Aciphylla squarrosa (The Spaniard)..</i>. Maori name [also 1907] <i>Kuri-Kuri</i>; local name, <i>Spaniard </i>or<i> Wild Spaniard. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,339</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Dicksonia fibrosa</i>, a tree-fern.

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Dicksonia fibrosa, Col.] Weki-ponga; Kuripaka. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,953</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [kuruwhengi](../K#kuruwhengi))

### kuriwhengu
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kuruwhengi](../K#kuruwhengi))

### kuruwhengi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPATULA \{RHYNCAPSiS\} VARiEGATA. (NEW-ZEALAND SHOVELLER.) ... <i>Native name.</i> Tete, \{Pateke,\} Putaitai, Kuriwhengi, \{Kuruwhengu, Papaungungu,\} Kahoho, and Wetawetangu; `Spoon-bill Duck' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,252,{1888 2nd edn 2/269} ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND SHOVELER <i>Anas rhynchotis</i> [?line missing] <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,92</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand Shoveler Anas rhynchotis variegata</i> (GOULD, 1856) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Kuru whengi, spoonbill, spoonie. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,148</i>)

### kuta
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kutu](../K#kutu))

### kutai
 
 <i>n.</i> mussel

>  <b>Harris</b> But i am too eagerly waylaid by kutai off the rocks ... <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,62</i>)

>  send back his bursting tinana fat with kutai, / kina, fish-heads, salt and words. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>) [NOTES: Glenn Colqhoun]

>  it wasn't always like this, though - why, when i think back to the times we rode many miles over the hills to gather kai moana to supplement our everyday diet. There were paua, kutai, and kaura, big and red, when they were in season. This entailed a whole day's journey, and more often than not we stayed half the night also, netting for fish at the narrows of the bay. We would return home with our pihau bags laden heavy with shellfish and fish. The fish, mostly mullet, were sorted, cleaned, gutted and then smoked. Fat plumpy kutai were shelled and bottled. <i>(Mar 1964,Te Ao Hou,46: 5</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> The mussels, those stubby fat blueblack southern kutae, have eroded away entirely over the years. <i>(1989,Hold This Land,64</i>)

>  There were several kinds of shellfish, including kutai, kokote, and huangi; of fish, tamure (snapper), takeke (guardfish), arara (travalli). <i>(Jun 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  The mussel, Myrtilus [sic] latus, and the Maori kutai, makes excellent eating and is much sought after by the natives. <i>(May 25, 1910,Otago Witness,89</i>)

### kutieish
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MACRAE</b> The Whistler kid would scratch his head then rub kutieish hands all over Caleb. <i>(1986,New Outlook,21/49</i>)

### kutu
 
 <i>n.</i> head louse

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG</b> So Hemi would give up trying and play along - even improving his image by being the brightest at school, the cleanest, no kutus in his hair, always a clean hankie, and clean nails. <i>(1982,into the World of Light,217</i>)

>  Specimens of Language South Sea Malay Java Princes island Outou Coutou a louse <i>(Jan 1771,Banks Journal,240,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> ... that species of loathsome vermin to which uncleanly Europeans have long been familiarized, were crawling over them [sc. New Zealanders] in myriads. These vermin, which are too well known among us to require their name being repeated, are in their language called cootoos, and the inhabitants in general are equally full of them, ... <i>ibid.</i> 2/329 A worm Cootoo-cootoo <i>ibid.</i> 2/331 A louse Kootoo <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/87</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|uta, s. A louse. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,171</i>)

>  louse - koo'ta [kutu] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,114</i>) [NOTES: 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 286 (|= macron precedes) louse, ko|o|ta [kutu] c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 114]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Every prospect of being over run with fleas and <i>kutus. </i> <i>(Apr 20, 1831,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,176,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... it prevented me from seeing the kutus' [sic] that filled the [line missing] <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/186</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Kuta - a louse Kutu - <i>id. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/370</i>)

>  <b>GRiEVE</b> in the earlier days when Mr. Bird inspected the Native Schools himself, word of his coming was the signal for ... a feverishly waged war upon the wily<i> kutu.</i> [fn] <i>Kutu</i> - a louse. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,45,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kutu n.</i> louse (also <i>cootie</i>) <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,274</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  `You had the Whistlers here,' i said. `Yes', said the Moslem lady [dairy-keeper]. ... `Do you worry about catching their kutus?' i asked interested. 21/49 All of them got kutus. ... Even if a Whistler manages to get his head clean he soon catches them again . <i>(1986,New Outlook,21/49</i>) [NOTES: Macrae (The Kingsland Kid)]

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Cleaning / <i>kutu</i> heads, waiting / for washing to dry. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,100</i>) [NOTES: From Evelyn Patuawa-Nathan (1979) The Kaihu]



 
 <i>n.</i> a louse, esp. a head louse [ORIGIN: Maori, also cootie] (See [also cootie](../A#also-cootie))

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The Maori wife of Steve Smith, a whaler, was named Kuti. The word `tuiau' means a flea in Maori and according to us youngsters [c1840s] `kuti' meant lousy. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

### kuwaka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [godwit](../G#godwit))

>  The special bird of those shores is a large sea snipe, kuwaka (limosa Baueri), a godwit. <i>(Nov 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### kuwharu
 
 <i>n.</i> a shell-fish, a grub

>  Kuw|adu, s. Name of a certain shell-fish; also a certain insect. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,171</i>)

>  Turned out by waeroas about 4 a.m. ... Eat kumara, ti & kuharu. [fn.] The root of the ti - rather like licorice root. <i>(Dec 20, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/670,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

### kweis kateer
 
 <i>adj.</i> [various spellings]

>  She sure baked a kwais-kiteer scone... Yeah, and she reckoned she'd put on a fresh batch for them when they got back... There they sat.. drinking hot tea and eating a fresh batch of old lady Murphy's scones. <i>(Feb 22, 1943,NZEF Times,ii/87 : 5</i>)

>  Quice. Often coupled with the word 'bint', e.g. 'quice bint.' All prospective customers to a native shop are 'quice catear.' if they leave without purchasing they are 'musquice'. <i>(Jan 24, 1944,NZEF Times,iii/135 : 11</i>)

>  Sa-eeda Ahmed, Akers, arrak,/ Bints, Bomonti. backsheech, barrak;/ Centipedes and Cleopatra,/ Dust and desert, djinns and Fatima;/ `Eggs-cook, ana miskeen,'/ Fever, flies, and felaheen;/ Gehenam, Galabeyah, gods,/ Harems, hooshters, heat and hods;/ `igri wallah, imshi Y'allah.'/ Jackos, jackals, `gala gala';/ Khamseen, kan kan, tala hena,/ Lice and locusts, love and Lena;/ Mirage, Mahomet, mosquistoe,/ Niggers, T.A.B. and C2;/ Olives oranges, askarie./ Peanuts, pyramids, and gharry;/ `Queens' and `Quacks' and `k'weis kateer,'/ Ruins, rum, and Gyppo beer;/ Scorpion, sand, sun, sin, and Scarab,/ Tibbin, taube, and tricky Arab;/ Whizz-bangs, wadis, `yin-al-deenak,'/ Yids, and yesterday, yeh meenak;/ Kestrels, kingfishers, and kites,/ Torrid days and sleepless nights;/ Mosques and minarets--maleesh;/ `O'ariglak,' `feluce--mafeesh'--/ Bookra, badein, ack-ack-emma,/ The soldier's farewell--ma-es salemma; <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> He knew that..<i>kweis ketir</i> indicated that something or other was pretty good. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,32</i>)

>  Nile quis kitia; Cairo no good. <i>(Oct 16, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,i/4 : 19</i>)

>  in its colloquial sense.. the word "bumper" means big and kwaiyis kattir. <i>(Oct 19, 1942,NZEF Times,ii/69 : 6</i>)

### kwila
 
 <i>n.</i> a New Guinea timber, much used for building and decks.

>  Both rooms have kwila flooring... <i>(Jul 21, 2004,TV1,C15</i>)

### kwise
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  And so to breakfast.. Some very 'kwise' tea, a little sand and sausage with good old army biscuits. <i>(Sep 7, 1942,NZEF Times,ii/63 : 5</i>)
